🟢 Part 1: Foundation Chapters 1-4

Chapter 1: What is Cloud Native? (The Big Picture)

Before diving into containers and Kubernetes, let's understand why Cloud Native exists, what problems it solves, and how Platform Engineering fits into the bigger picture. We'll use our anihpj/jobpost Django app to make every concept concrete.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 📖 Conceptual 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The evolution from Waterfall → Agile → DevOps → Cloud Native → Platform Engineering
  • ✅ The CNCF definition of Cloud Native and its 5 pillars
  • ✅ What Platform Engineering is and why it matters for modern organizations
  • ✅ The "Golden Path" concept and Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs)
  • ✅ How Platform Engineering differs from DevOps and SRE
  • ✅ Real-world Platform Engineering at scale (Spotify, Netflix, Airbnb, Shopify)
🎓 CNPA Relevance
Domain 1: PE Core Fundamentals 36% Domain 5: IDPs & DevEx 8%
Understanding "Why" Matters on the CNPA

The CNPA exam tests your understanding of Platform Engineering principles, not just tool commands. Key concepts to internalize from this chapter:

  • Platform as a Product — The CNPA emphasizes treating your IDP like a product with real customers (developers).
  • Golden Paths — Understanding the difference between paved roads and free paths is critical for the exam.
  • Cloud Native Pillars — Containers, Service Mesh, Microservices, Immutable Infrastructure, and Declarative APIs form the foundation.

1.1 The Evolution of Software Delivery

Software delivery has evolved through five major eras. Each solved problems of the previous one — but introduced new challenges. Understanding this journey is essential for every Platform Engineer.

📖 Key Concept: Platform Engineering

Platform Engineering is the discipline of designing and building toolchains and workflows that enable self-service capabilities for engineering teams in cloud-native environments. Think of it as "building the paved road" so developers don't have to navigate the muddy path of infrastructure themselves.

📋
2000s
Waterfall + Manual IT

One app per physical server. Manual provisioning. Ticket-driven ops. anihpj would need its own dedicated box.

❌ Months to deploy
🔄
2010s
Agile + DevOps

Multiple VMs per server. "You build it, you run it." CI/CD emerges. Still heavy overhead per VM.

⚠️ Weeks to deploy
☸️
2015
Cloud Native

Containers, microservices, declarative APIs, immutable infrastructure. K8s becomes the standard.

✅ Days to deploy
🏗️
2020
Platform Engineering

Internal Developer Platforms. Golden paths. Self-service APIs. Reducing cognitive load for devs.

✅ Hours to deploy
🚀
2025
AI-Enabled Platforms

AI copilots for platform ops. Predictive scaling. Automated remediation. Natural language → infra.

✅ Minutes to deploy

What This Means for anihpj

When we first built anihpj, deploying the jobpost app meant: SSH into a server, install Python, set up a virtual environment, configure nginx, and hope nothing broke. With Cloud Native and Platform Engineering, the entire process becomes automated, repeatable, and self-service.

1.2 Cloud Native Defined — The CNCF Definition

The Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) defines cloud native technologies as those that "empower organizations to build and run scalable applications in modern, dynamic environments such as public, private, and hybrid clouds."

📖 The CNCF (Cloud Native Computing Foundation)

The CNCF is a Linux Foundation project founded in 2015 that hosts critical cloud native components like Kubernetes, Prometheus, Envoy, and 200+ other projects. The CNPA certification is also administered by the Linux Foundation.

The 5 Pillars of Cloud Native
                    ┌──────────────────────────┐
                    │    ☸️  Containers         │
                    │  Docker, containerd,      │
                    │       CRI-O               │
                    └──────────┬───────────────┘
                               │
          ┌────────────────────┼────────────────────┐
          │                    │                    │
┌─────────┴─────────┐ ┌───────┴───────┐ ┌─────────┴─────────┐
│ 🕸️ Service Mesh   │ │ 📦 Microservices│ │ 🏗️ Immutable Infra│
│ Istio, Linkerd,   │ │ API-driven,    │ │ Terraform,        │
│ Cilium, Consul    │ │ loosely coupled│ │ Containers as     │
│                    │ │                │ │ immutable units   │
└────────────────────┘ └────────────────┘ └───────────────────┘
                               │
                    ┌──────────┴───────────┐
                    │ 📜 Declarative APIs  │
                    │ Kubernetes YAML,     │
                    │ Terraform HCL,       │
                    │ GitOps Manifests     │
                    └──────────────────────┘
📦
Containers

Standardized packaging of applications with all dependencies. Docker, containerd, and CRI-O are the most common runtimes. Containers are the "building blocks" of cloud native.

🕸️
Service Mesh

Infrastructure layer for service-to-service communication. Handles mTLS, traffic splitting, retries, and observability. Istio, Linkerd, and Cilium are popular choices.

📦
Microservices

Architectural pattern where applications are built as small, independent, loosely-coupled services. Each service owns its data and can be deployed independently.

🗿
Immutable Infrastructure

Servers/containers are never modified after deployment — they are replaced. "Cattle, not pets." If an anihpj container has an issue, we replace it, not fix it in place.

📜
Declarative APIs

You declare WHAT you want, the system figures out HOW to achieve it. Kubernetes YAML says "I want 3 replicas" — K8s makes it happen without you specifying steps.

1.3 What is Platform Engineering?

Platform Engineering is the practice of building Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs) that provide self-service capabilities to development teams. It's the natural evolution of DevOps at scale.

Before vs After Platform Engineering
  ❌ BEFORE PLATFORM ENGINEERING
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer → Opens Jira Ticket → Ops provisions     │
  │  "I need a DB"   "Wait 2 weeks"   Manual everything │
  │                                                      │
  │  😰 Results:                                         │
  │  • 2-week wait for a database                        │
  │  • Inconsistent environments                         │
  │  • "Snowflake" servers (each unique)                 │
  │  • Developers frustrated with ops                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ AFTER PLATFORM ENGINEERING
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Developer → Self-Service Portal → Automated         │
  │  "I need a DB"   "Click — done!"    Terraform + K8s  │
  │                                                      │
  │  🎉 Results:                                         │
  │  • Database provisioned in < 5 minutes               │
  │  • Consistent, reproducible environments             │
  │  • Everything versioned in Git                       │
  │  • Developers love the platform!                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🏭
Platform as a Product

Your IDP is a PRODUCT, not a project. It has customers (developers), a roadmap, feedback loops, and success metrics like Developer NPS and Time-to-First-Deploy.

🧠
Reducing Cognitive Load

Developers shouldn't need to be K8s experts to deploy an app. The platform abstracts complexity behind simple interfaces (CLI, portal, API).

1.4 The "Golden Path" Concept

A Golden Path is a pre-configured, supported, and recommended workflow for developers. Think of it as a paved road — it gets you where you need to go quickly and safely. Developers can go "off-road" if needed, but the paved path handles the 80% use case.

Golden Path for anihpj — Code to Production
  👨‍💻 Write Code → 📝 Git Push → 🏗️ CI Build → 🧪 Tests → 🐳 Docker Build
                          │              │              │
                          ▼              ▼              ▼
                     ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐
                     │ Linting  │ │ Unit     │  │Container │
                     │ & SAST   │ │ Tests    │  │ Registry  │
                     └──────────┘ └──────────┘  └────┬─────┘
                          │              │            │
                          └──────┬───────┘            ▼
                                 ▼               ┌──────────┐
                          ┌──────────┐           │ Deploy   │
                          │ Security │──────────▶│ to Staging│
                          │ Scan     │           └────┬─────┘
                          └──────────┘                │
                                                     ▼
                          ┌──────────┐           ┌──────────┐
                          │ Approval │◀──────────│ Smoke    │
                          │ Gate     │           │ Tests    │
                          └────┬─────┘           └──────────┘
                               ▼
                          ┌──────────┐
                          │ 🚀 Deploy│
                          │ to Prod  │
                          └──────────┘
💡 Platform Engineering Tip

The Golden Path should handle 80% of use cases with zero friction. For the remaining 20%, provide escape hatches — developers should be able to customize when needed, but the default path should always be the easiest option.

1.5 Platform Engineering vs DevOps vs SRE

These three disciplines are often confused. Here's how they differ — and how they complement each other in a modern organization.

AspectDevOpsSREPlatform Engineering
FocusCulture & CollaborationReliability & AutomationSelf-service Platform
Key Concept"You build it, you run it"SLOs, Error BudgetsIDP, Golden Paths
Primary UsersDev + Ops teamsSite Reliability EngineersALL developers
MeasuresDORA Metrics (DF, LT, MTTR)SLO attainment, MTTR, toil %Developer NPS, Time-to-Deploy
Key ToolsJenkins, GitHub ActionsPrometheus, PagerDuty, GrafanaBackstage, Terraform, Crossplane
anihpj ExampleDevs write CI/CD pipelineSRE defines 99.9% uptime SLOPE builds self-service deploy button
🤝
They Work TOGETHER

DevOps provides the culture, SRE ensures reliability, and Platform Engineering builds the tools that make both possible at scale. A mature organization has all three.

📐
The Platform Engineering Sweet Spot

Platform Engineering is most needed when: teams > 20 developers, services > 10, or infrastructure complexity is high enough that developers spend > 30% of time on non-coding tasks.

1.6 Why CNPA? — The Value of Platform Engineering Certification

The Certified Cloud Native Platform Engineering Associate (CNPA) certification validates your knowledge of platform engineering principles, tools, and practices. Here's why it matters:

📈
Industry Demand

Platform Engineering roles have grown 300% from 2020-2025, making it one of the fastest-growing tech disciplines.

💰
Salary Range

US Platform Engineers earn $130K-$220K (2025 data). Senior and Staff roles command premium compensation.

🎯
What CNPA Validates

IDP design, IaC, GitOps, CI/CD, Observability, Security, Service Mesh, FinOps — all tested on the exam.

🪜
Career Path

Platform Engineer → Senior → Staff → Principal → Platform Architect. CNPA is your first step on this ladder.

📖 CNPA vs Other Certifications

CKA certifies you to administer Kubernetes clusters. CKAD certifies you to deploy applications on K8s. CNPA certifies you to build the platform that makes both of those activities self-service and scalable across an entire organization.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — PE vs DevOps vs SRE

This is one of the most tested concepts on the CNPA exam (Domain 1, 36%). You WILL see questions comparing these three. Memorize: DevOps = culture (you build it, you run it), SRE = reliability engineering (SLOs, error budgets, toil reduction), Platform Engineering = self-service tooling (IDPs, Golden Paths, reducing cognitive load). They are complementary, not competing. A question may ask: "Which discipline focuses on building Internal Developer Platforms?" Answer: Platform Engineering. "Which defines SLOs and Error Budgets?" Answer: SRE.

📝 Domain 1 Quick Check — Can You Answer These?

These are the exact types of questions you'll see on exam day:

Q1: A startup has 15 developers managing their own K8s clusters. Developers spend 40% of time on infrastructure. Which discipline solves this?
Answer: Platform Engineering — it builds self-service IDPs that reduce cognitive load. DevOps is the culture, but PE provides the tools.

Q2: What is the primary purpose of an Internal Developer Platform (IDP)?
Answer: To provide self-service capabilities that abstract infrastructure complexity, enabling developers to deploy software without deep platform knowledge.

Q3: Which of the following is NOT one of the 5 pillars of Cloud Native? a) Containers b) Service Mesh c) Virtual Machines d) Declarative APIs
Answer: c) Virtual Machines. The 5 pillars are: Containers, Service Mesh, Microservices, Immutable Infrastructure, Declarative APIs.

1.7 Real-World Platform Engineering at Scale

🎵
Spotify
1,600+ developers

Created Backstage (open-sourced to CNCF) — a unified developer portal with software catalog, templates, and documentation. Result: 90% faster onboarding for new developers.

🎬
Netflix
1,000+ microservices

Built paved road with Spinnaker (CD), Titus (containers), and Atlas (telemetry). Developers deploy 4,000+ times per day without needing cloud expertise.

🏠
Airbnb
500+ services

Standardized service templates, CI/CD pipelines, and infrastructure abstraction. Result: 80% reduction in deploy time across all teams.

🛒
Shopify
2,000+ developers

Built internal PaaS on Kubernetes. Developers define their app, the platform handles everything else. Result: 5 minutes from commit to production.

💡 Lesson for anihpj

Start small — your first IDP doesn't need to be Backstage. A simple Git repo template with Dockerfile + K8s manifests + CI config will already reduce your team's deploy time by 80%. Evolve from there.

✅ Chapter 1 Summary — Cloud Native & Platform Engineering Foundations

You've journeyed through five eras of software delivery: Waterfall → Agile → DevOps → Cloud Native → Platform Engineering. You now understand the 5 pillars of Cloud Native (Containers, Service Mesh, Microservices, Immutable Infrastructure, Declarative APIs), the CNCF's role in hosting critical open-source projects, and how Platform Engineering emerged from DevOps to solve the "cognitive load" problem at scale. You learned the distinction between PE, DevOps, and SRE — three overlapping but distinct disciplines. Most importantly, you've seen how real companies (Spotify's Backstage, Netflix's paved road) implement platform engineering, and how the anihpj/jobpost Django app serves as your learning vehicle throughout this guide.

🔑 Key Takeaway 1

Cloud Native = containers + microservices + declarative APIs + immutable infra + service mesh, all orchestrated by K8s.

🔑 Key Takeaway 2

Platform Engineering = building self-service IDPs so developers can deploy in hours, not weeks — "Platform as a Product."

🔑 Key Takeaway 3

Golden Paths = paved roads that make the right way the easy way. Developers stay productive without knowing every infra detail.

📝 Chapter 1 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
A startup's developers spend 40% of their time on infrastructure tasks (provisioning databases, configuring CI/CD, managing K8s manifests). The CTO wants to reduce this to less than 10%. Which discipline should they invest in, and what specific approach would solve this?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Platform Engineering — specifically building an Internal Developer Platform (IDP) with Golden Paths. An IDP provides self-service capabilities that abstract infrastructure complexity, allowing developers to deploy software without deep platform knowledge. The platform team builds the paved road once, and all developers use it.

Explanation

This is the #1 reason organizations adopt Platform Engineering. DevOps says "you build it, you run it" — but at scale, this means every developer must be a K8s expert, a Terraform expert, and a CI/CD expert. Platform Engineering solves this by building self-service tools that let developers focus on code while the platform handles infrastructure. The CNPA exam tests this distinction repeatedly: PE ≠ DevOps, PE = building tools that make DevOps scalable.

Q2
Which of the following is NOT one of the five pillars of Cloud Native?
a) Containers   b) Service Mesh   c) Virtual Machines   d) Microservices   e) Immutable Infrastructure   f) Declarative APIs
`n Show Answer
Answer

c) Virtual Machines — VMs are the pre-cloud-native approach. The five pillars are: Containers (standardized packaging), Service Mesh (service-to-service communication), Microservices (loosely-coupled architecture), Immutable Infrastructure (never modify, always replace), and Declarative APIs (declare desired state, system reconciles).

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests your understanding of what Cloud Native IS and ISN'T. VMs represent the old way — mutable, heavy, manually managed. Cloud Native moves to containers (lightweight, immutable) orchestrated by Kubernetes. Knowing the 5 pillars cold is essential for Domain 1 questions that ask "Which of these is a Cloud Native principle?"

Q3
Differentiate between DevOps, SRE, and Platform Engineering. Which discipline would define an SLO of 99.9% uptime for the anihpj jobpost API?
`n Show Answer
Answer

SRE would define the SLO. Here's the breakdown:

DevOps = Culture + practices. "You build it, you run it." Focus on collaboration between dev and ops. Measures: DORA metrics.

SRE = Reliability engineering. Defines SLOs, Error Budgets, automates toil. Focus on system reliability. Measures: SLO attainment, MTTR, toil percentage.

Platform Engineering = Building IDPs. Creates self-service tools and Golden Paths. Focus on developer productivity. Measures: Time-to-deploy, Developer NPS.

Explanation

This is a high-yield exam topic. The CNPA will ask you to match disciplines to their activities. Think: DevOps = culture, SRE = reliability (SLOs, error budgets), PE = self-service (IDPs, Golden Paths). They are complementary: DevOps provides the philosophy, SRE ensures reliability, PE builds the tools that scale both. A mature org has all three.

Q4
What is a Golden Path? Give an example using the anihpj/jobpost Django application.
`n Show Answer
Answer

A Golden Path is an opinionated, supported, and well-documented workflow that gets developers from code to production quickly. It's the "paved road" that makes the right way the easy way.

anihpj Example: A Golden Path template on GitHub that includes: Dockerfile (multi-stage Python), K8s manifests (Deployment + Service + Ingress), GitHub Actions CI pipeline, and a Makefile. A developer creates a new service by clicking "Use this template" → code → push → deployed. Time: 10 minutes instead of 2 weeks.

Explanation

Golden Paths are NOT mandatory — they're "paved roads" with escape hatches. Teams CAN deviate if needed (e.g., special security requirements), but they accept the cost of maintaining their own path. The key insight: make the supported path so good that deviation is rarely needed. The CNPA exam asks: "What is the purpose of a Golden Path?" Answer: reduce cognitive load and accelerate delivery while maintaining governance.

Q5
What is the CNCF and what role does it play in the cloud native ecosystem? Name three graduated CNCF projects relevant to platform engineering.
`n Show Answer
Answer

The Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) is a Linux Foundation project founded in 2015 that hosts and governs critical open-source cloud native technologies. It provides a vendor-neutral home for projects, sets maturity levels (Sandbox → Incubating → Graduated), and defines cloud native standards.

Three graduated projects for PE: ① Kubernetes (orchestration), ② Prometheus (monitoring), ③ Helm (package management). Others: ArgoCD, Linkerd, Cilium, containerd, etcd, Jaeger, Envoy.

Explanation

The CNPA exam may ask about CNCF project maturity levels. Graduated = production-ready, widely adopted, governance mature. Incubating = growing adoption, working toward graduation (e.g., Backstage, Crossplane, OpenTelemetry). Sandbox = experimental, early stage. The CNPA itself is a Linux Foundation certification — knowing the CNCF's role shows domain awareness.

Q6
Spotify created Backstage and open-sourced it to the CNCF. What problem did it solve, and what are its three core components?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Problem: 1,600+ developers across 150+ teams had complete tool sprawl — no single place to find services, documentation, or create new services. New developers took months to become productive.

Three core components:
① Software Catalog — centralized registry of all services, APIs, websites, and libraries (what exists, who owns it)
② Software Templates — self-service scaffolding for creating new services following Golden Paths
③ TechDocs — documentation-as-code, auto-generated from Markdown in each service's repo

Result: 90% faster developer onboarding.

Explanation

Backstage is the reference implementation of an IDP portal. The CNPA exam tests your knowledge of IDP components: Catalog (discoverability), Templates (self-service scaffolding), TechDocs (documentation). Backstage is CNCF Incubating. Remember: Backstage is NOT the only IDP — Port, Humanitec, Qovery, and Mia-Platform are alternatives that may appear as distractor options.

Q7
Explain the concept of "Platform as a Product." What does it mean to treat your IDP like a product, and what practices does this imply?
`n Show Answer
Answer

"Platform as a Product" means treating your Internal Developer Platform with the same discipline as a commercial product: you have real customers (developers), you conduct user research, you build roadmaps, you measure satisfaction (NPS), and you iterate based on feedback.

Practices: ① Product Manager for the platform. ② Regular developer surveys (DevEx metrics). ③ Roadmap prioritized by developer pain points, not infrastructure team preferences. ④ Adoption metrics (# services onboarded, time-to-10th-deploy). ⑤ SLAs for platform services (e.g., "99.9% availability for CI/CD pipeline").

Explanation

This is a core CNPA concept (Domain 5, 8%). The old model: infra team builds what they think is needed, forces developers to use it. The new model: platform team treats developers as customers, builds what developers actually need, measures satisfaction, and iterates. The exam may ask: "Which approach best describes modern Platform Engineering?" Answer: Platform as a Product.

Q8
Netflix's platform engineering approach is often described as a "paved road with escape hatches." What does this mean and why is it important?
`n Show Answer
Answer

The paved road is the supported, well-maintained path — pre-built CI/CD pipelines, standardized Dockerfiles, approved deployment patterns, monitoring dashboards. Most teams use it and get fast, reliable deploys with zero infrastructure knowledge.

The escape hatches allow teams to deviate when they have legitimate needs — specialized hardware (GPU for ML), unique security requirements, or experimental architectures. BUT: teams that deviate accept the cost of maintaining their own path (they lose platform support for those customizations).

Why important: Pure "locked-in" platforms breed resentment and shadow IT. Pure "free-for-all" leads to chaos. Paved road + escape hatches = the right balance.

Explanation

Netflix operates 1,000+ microservices with 4,000+ daily deploys. They can't possibly support every custom configuration — so they make the supported path fast and easy, and let teams choose their own adventure if needed. The CNPA exam values this balanced approach: opinionated defaults with flexibility for edge cases.

Q9
What is cognitive load in the context of platform engineering, and how does an IDP reduce it?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Cognitive load is the mental effort required for a developer to understand and work with the infrastructure needed to deploy and run their application. High cognitive load = developers must understand K8s, Terraform, Helm, CI/CD, networking, security policies, monitoring, and logging — before they can ship a single feature.

An IDP reduces cognitive load by abstracting infrastructure complexity behind self-service interfaces: "I want a database" → platform provisions it. "Deploy to staging" → platform runs CI/CD + K8s deploy. Developers focus on business logic, not infrastructure.

Explanation

Cognitive load is the fundamental problem Platform Engineering solves. Team Topologies (a seminal book on the topic) identifies three types: intrinsic (core domain), extraneous (infrastructure complexity), and germane (learning). Platform Engineering targets extraneous load — the stuff developers shouldn't need to know. The exam may reference this concept.

Q10
A company has 30 developers, 15 microservices, and developers wait an average of 2 weeks for infrastructure requests. The CTO wants "5-minute deploys." What is the minimum viable platform (MVP) they should build first?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Start with the biggest pain point: the 2-week infrastructure wait. The MVP platform should include:

① Golden Path template repo — Cookiecutter/Django scaffold with pre-configured Dockerfile, K8s manifests, and CI pipeline. Developers create a new service by cloning the template.

② GitOps deployment (ArgoCD) — Git push triggers automatic deploy to K8s. No manual kubectl apply needed.

③ Self-service database provisioning — a simple CLI or web form that creates a PostgreSQL instance (backed by Crossplane or a Terraform module).

Do NOT start with: Backstage (too complex for MVP), full service mesh (premature), or custom CLIs (build after templates work).

Explanation

The #1 CNPA exam mistake is thinking you need Backstage or a full IDP on day one. The exam emphasizes incremental platform adoption: start with the biggest pain point (usually slow deploys or manual infra), build Golden Paths, then layer on portal/catalog/advanced features as the platform matures. Maturity levels: L1 (Docs) → L2 (Templates) → L3 (Self-Service) → L4 (Automated) → L5 (AI-Assisted).

Chapter 2: Containers and Docker — The Foundation of Cloud Native

Containers are the packaging format of the cloud native world. Before we deploy anihpj to Kubernetes, we need to understand how to package it as a container image. This chapter covers Docker fundamentals, creating production-ready images, and essential commands every Platform Engineer needs.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 🐳 Hands-On 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Why containers solve the "Works on My Machine" problem
  • ✅ Containers vs Virtual Machines — architecture and performance comparison
  • ✅ Docker fundamentals: images, containers, Dockerfiles, registries
  • ✅ Creating a production-ready multi-stage Dockerfile for anihpj
  • ✅ Container registries: Docker Hub, ECR, GCR, ACR, Harbor, GHCR
  • ✅ Essential Docker commands for Platform Engineers

2.1 Why Containers? The "Works on My Machine" Problem

Every developer has experienced this: anihpj runs perfectly on your laptop (Python 3.11, Django 5.2), but crashes in production (Python 3.8, missing dependencies). Containers solve this by packaging the application with ALL its dependencies into a single, portable unit.

The Shipping Container Analogy
  🚢 THE SHIPPING CONTAINER ANALOGY

  Before Containers:                    After (Containerized):
  ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐             ┌─────────────────────┐
  │🍌Ban│ │📱iPh│ │👟Sne│             │  📦 CONTAINER        │
  │anas│ │ones│ │aker│    Each item   │  ┌───────────────┐   │
  └─────┘ └─────┘ └─────┘   loaded    │  │ App + All Deps │   │  Same box fits:
                              separately│  │ Python 3.11    │   │  🚢 Dev Laptop
                                        │  │ Django 5.2     │   │  🚛 Staging
  Messy, slow, inconsistent!            │  │ gunicorn       │   │  🚂 Production
                                        │  └───────────────┘   │
                                        └─────────────────────┘
                                        Stackable, portable, consistent!

2.2 Containers vs Virtual Machines — Deep Comparison

  ┌─────────────────────────┐    ┌───────────────────────────┐

  │  ┌────────┴──────────┐  │    │  ┌───────────┴───────────┐│

  │  ✅ Lightweight          │    │  ❌ Heavy overhead         │
  │  ✅ Seconds to start     │    │  ❌ Minutes to start       │

Disk per unit~100 MB~10-40 GB

When to Use Which
  CHOOSE CONTAINERS WHEN:              CHOOSE VMs WHEN:
  ✅ Microservices architecture        ✅ Strong isolation needed
  ✅ CI/CD pipelines                   ✅ Running untrusted code
  ✅ Cloud-native apps                 ✅ Different OS kernels needed
  ✅ Dev/Test environments             ✅ Legacy monolith migration

  ✅ Stateless workloads               ✅ GPU/NIC passthrough

  💡 PLATFORM TIP: Modern platforms use BOTH — K8s on VMs!
     Containers for apps, VMs for node isolation and security.

2.3 Docker Fundamentals for Platform Engineers

📝
Dockerfile

Declarative recipe for building container images. Like a requirements.txt on steroids — it specifies the base image, dependencies, and runtime commands.

📦
Image

A blueprint/template built from a Dockerfile. Images are immutable and layered. Like a Python class — defines the structure but isn't running yet.

▶️
Container

A running instance of an image. Like a Python object — instantiated from the class (image). Can be started, stopped, and destroyed.

🗄️
Registry

Storage for container images. Like PyPI for containers. Docker Hub is the most popular; also ECR, GCR, ACR, GHCR, Harbor.

Docker Image Layers — Caching Strategy for anihpj
  Layer 6 ┌────────────────────────┐ ← CMD ["gunicorn"...]
          │ CMD (startup command)  │   Changes? → rebuild this
  Layer 5 ├────────────────────────┤ ← COPY . /app/
          │ Application code       │   Changes? → rebuild this
  Layer 4 ├────────────────────────┤ ← RUN python manage.py collectstatic
          │ Static files           │
  Layer 3 ├────────────────────────┤ ← RUN pip install -r requirements.txt
          │ Django, gunicorn...    │   ✅ CACHED if requirements.txt unchanged!
  Layer 2 ├────────────────────────┤ ← COPY requirements.txt
          │ requirements.txt       │   ✅ CACHED if unchanged!
  Layer 1 ├────────────────────────┤ ← FROM python:3.11-slim
          │ Base Python image      │   ✅ Downloaded once
          └────────────────────────┘

  💡 TIP: Copy requirements.txt BEFORE application code!
    This way, pip install only re-runs when deps change.
🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Docker Fundamentals (Domain 1, 36%)

The CNPA exam tests Docker conceptually, not hands-on. Key facts: (1) CMD vs ENTRYPOINT — CMD provides defaults (overridable), ENTRYPOINT defines the executable (harder to override). (2) Layers are cached in order — put frequently-changing lines LAST in Dockerfile. (3) Multi-stage builds = separate build from runtime → smaller images → faster deploys → better security. (4) Containers share the host kernel — this is why they're lighter than VMs and why you can't run Windows containers on Linux hosts. (5) containerd is the CNCF-graduated container runtime used by K8s (not Docker directly since v1.24).

2.4 Creating a Production Dockerfile for anihpj

Dockerfile 
# ===== STAGE 1: BUILDER =====
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends gcc libpq-dev
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

# ===== STAGE 2: PRODUCTION =====
FROM python:3.11-slim
RUN addgroup --system anihpj && adduser --system --group anihpj
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends libpq5
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
COPY --from=builder /app/static /app/static
COPY --from=builder /app /app
ENV DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE=anihpj.settings PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
USER anihpj
EXPOSE 8000
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4"]
💡 Multi-Stage Build

This Dockerfile uses two stages: Builder (installs deps, collects static) and Runtime (only what's needed to run). Result: 200MB image instead of 800MB — a 75% reduction!

🏷️ Tag Strategy

Use anihpj:v1.2.3-prod-abc1234 (semver + env + git SHA). NEVER use :latest in production! Immutable tags ensure you can always trace what's running.

2.6 Essential Docker Commands for Platform Engineers

Terminal
# 🏗️ IMAGE BUILDING
docker build -t anihpj:v1 .
docker build --no-cache -t anihpj:v1 .

# 🚀 RUNNING CONTAINERS
docker run -d --name app -p 8000:8000 anihpj:v1
docker run -e DB_HOST=postgres -v ./data:/app/data anihpj:v1

# 🔍 INSPECTING
docker ps                  # List running containers
docker logs -f anihpj-app  # Follow live logs
docker exec -it app bash   # Shell into container
docker stats               # Live resource usage

# 🧹 CLEANUP
docker system prune -a     # Remove ALL unused
docker rm anihpj-app       # Remove container

# ☁️ REGISTRY
docker tag anihpj:v1 ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1
docker push ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1

✅ Chapter 2 Summary — Containers & Docker Mastery

You now understand why containers exist — they solve the "works on my machine" problem by packaging code + dependencies + config into immutable, portable units. You learned the critical differences between containers and VMs (shared kernel = 10x density), mastered the Dockerfile anatomy (FROM → WORKDIR → COPY → RUN → EXPOSE → CMD), built a multi-stage production image for anihpj, and know how to push to registries. The process-steps at the end give you a repeatable workflow: Dockerfile → .dockerignore → build → run → verify.

🐳 Key Takeaway 1

Containers = isolated processes sharing the host kernel. VMs = entire OS per instance. Containers are ~10x denser on the same hardware.

🐳 Key Takeaway 2

Multi-stage builds: builder stage compiles deps, production stage copies only what's needed. Image size: ~900MB → ~150MB (83% reduction).

🐳 Key Takeaway 3

docker build → docker run → docker push. Registry = container image repo (ghcr.io, Docker Hub). Images are layered, immutable, and versioned.

📝 Chapter 2 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the fundamental difference between a container and a virtual machine? Why can you run 10x more containers than VMs on the same hardware?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Containers share the host operating system kernel. Each container is an isolated process with its own filesystem, network, and process namespace — but all use the same kernel. A container only needs to package the application + its dependencies (libraries, binaries).

Virtual Machines each run a complete guest OS with its own kernel. Every VM needs memory for the OS itself (~500MB-1GB just for the OS), plus CPU overhead for kernel operations.

10x density: No guest OS overhead = much less RAM per instance (container: ~50MB baseline vs VM: ~500MB baseline). Faster startup (container: ~1s vs VM: ~30-60s).

Explanation

This is the foundational container concept tested on the CNPA. The shared kernel is both the advantage (density, speed) and the trade-off (less isolation than VMs, can't run Windows containers on Linux hosts). The exam may ask: "Why are containers more efficient than VMs?" — answer: shared host kernel, no guest OS overhead.

Q2
You have this Dockerfile for anihpj. What's the purpose of COPY requirements.txt . on line 3 BEFORE COPY . . on line 5?
`n Show Answer
Answer

This is a Docker layer caching optimization. Docker caches each layer. If requirements.txt hasn't changed, Docker uses the cached layer from line 3-4 (COPY requirements + pip install). If you copied ALL files first, any code change would invalidate the cache and force a full pip install re-run — wasting 2-5 minutes per build.

FROM python:3.11-slim          # Layer 1: Base image (cached)
WORKDIR /app                    # Layer 2: Working dir
COPY requirements.txt .         # Layer 3: Deps file (cached if unchanged)
RUN pip install -r requirements.txt  # Layer 4: Install deps (cached!)
COPY . .                        # Layer 5: App code (changes every build)
CMD ["gunicorn", "..."]        # Layer 6: Startup command
Explanation

This is the #1 Docker optimization technique. The rule: put things that change LEAST often at the TOP of the Dockerfile. Dependencies change rarely → cache them. Application code changes every commit → put it last. The CNPA exam may ask about Dockerfile optimization — the answer always involves layer caching.

Q3
What is a multi-stage build and why would you use one for the anihpj Django application?
`n Show Answer
Answer

A multi-stage build uses multiple FROM statements in one Dockerfile. The first stage ("builder") compiles dependencies with build tools (gcc, headers). The second stage ("production") copies only the compiled artifacts — NOT the build tools.

anihpj benefit: Builder stage: installs gcc, libpq-dev, compiles psycopg2 and other Python C extensions. Production stage: copies only the compiled wheels + app code. Result: ~150MB final image vs ~900MB single-stage — 83% smaller, faster deploys, smaller attack surface.

Explanation

Multi-stage builds are a production best practice. The CNPA exam tests: (1) Why multi-stage? Smaller images, better security. (2) What stays in the builder stage? Build tools (gcc, compilers). (3) What goes to production? Only runtime deps + compiled artifacts. (4) COPY --from=builder is the key instruction.

Q4
What is the difference between CMD and ENTRYPOINT in a Dockerfile? Which is easier to override at runtime?
`n Show Answer
Answer

CMD: Provides default arguments to the entrypoint (or a default command if no entrypoint). Easily overridable: docker run myimage /bin/bash replaces CMD entirely.

ENTRYPOINT: Defines the main executable. Harder to override (needs --entrypoint flag). CMD becomes the default arguments to ENTRYPOINT.

ENTRYPOINT ["gunicorn"]       # Always run gunicorn
CMD ["--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]  # Default args
# docker run myimage → gunicorn --bind 0.0.0.0:8000 --workers 3
# docker run myimage --workers 5 → gunicorn --workers 5 (CMD overridden)
Explanation

The CNPA exam tests Dockerfile instruction knowledge: CMD = defaults (overridable by appending to docker run), ENTRYPOINT = the binary itself (hard to override). Combined: ENTRYPOINT is the executable, CMD provides default flags. This distinction matters for platform engineering — you want CMD for flexibility, ENTRYPOINT when the container MUST run a specific binary.

Q5
What is containerd and why did Kubernetes deprecate direct Docker support in v1.24?
`n Show Answer
Answer

containerd is a CNCF-graduated container runtime that manages the complete container lifecycle. It was originally developed by Docker but is now a standalone project. Kubernetes used to talk to Docker via a shim (dockershim), then containerd — an unnecessary intermediate layer.

Why deprecated: Docker includes many features K8s doesn't need (Docker CLI, Docker Compose, Docker Swarm). K8s only needs the container runtime part — containerd provides exactly that, is lighter, and is CNCF-standardized. Removing dockershim simplified the K8s codebase and reduced attack surface.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests this distinction: Docker != containerd. Docker is a developer tool (CLI, Compose, build). containerd is the runtime K8s uses. CRI-O is an alternative runtime. The exam may ask: "Which container runtime does Kubernetes use?" Answer: containerd or CRI-O (via CRI), not Docker directly.

Q6
You run docker build -t anihpj:v1 . and it takes 3 minutes. You change one line of Python code and rebuild. Which layers are rebuilt and which are cached?
`n Show Answer
Answer

Only layers AFTER the changed layer are rebuilt. If the Python code is the last COPY instruction, only that layer and subsequent layers rebuild. All previous layers (base image, apt-get install, pip install) use cache.

Rebuild time: ~5 seconds (only the COPY . . and any subsequent RUN layers) instead of 3 minutes.

Explanation

Docker layer caching is deterministic: it walks the Dockerfile from top to bottom. When it finds a changed instruction, it rebuilds that layer and ALL subsequent layers (because they might depend on the changed layer). This is why you put frequently-changing instructions (COPY application code) at the BOTTOM — to maximize cache hits for expensive operations (apt-get, pip install) at the TOP.

Q7
What is a container registry? Name three registries and explain the difference between public and private registries.
`n Show Answer
Answer

A container registry is a storage and distribution system for container images. Like PyPI for Python packages or npm for JavaScript — but for container images.

Public registries: Docker Hub (default), GitHub Container Registry (ghcr.io), Quay.io. Anyone can pull public images.

Private registries: Amazon ECR, Google Artifact Registry, Azure ACR, Harbor (self-hosted). Require authentication to pull.

Push/pull: docker tag anihpj:v1 ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1 → docker push ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1

Explanation

The registry is how your CI pipeline (build) connects to your K8s cluster (deploy). CI builds the image → pushes to registry → K8s pulls from registry. The CNPA exam tests this flow: Build → Push → Pull → Deploy. Know that registries store images by registry/namespace/repo:tag format.

Q8
What is a .dockerignore file and what should it exclude for the anihpj Django project?
`n Show Answer
Answer

.dockerignore excludes files from the Docker build context, making builds faster and images smaller. It works like .gitignore.

# anihpj .dockerignore
__pycache__/
*.pyc
*.pyo
.pytest_cache/
.git/
venv/
env/
*.sqlite3
node_modules/
.DS_Store
*.md  # except README.md

Without .dockerignore: Docker sends the ENTIRE directory (including venv/, __pycache__/, .git/) as build context — wasting time and potentially including secrets.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests Docker best practices. .dockerignore is a security and performance best practice: (1) Prevents secrets from leaking into images (never COPY .git/), (2) Reduces build context size (no venv/ or node_modules/), (3) Makes builds faster. Always have a .dockerignore in every project.

Q9
A developer runs docker run anihpj:v1 and the container exits immediately. What are three possible reasons and how do you debug each?
`n Show Answer
Answer

① No foreground process: The CMD/ENTRYPOINT runs, completes, and exits. Debug: docker logs <container> — see what the process output was. Fix: ensure CMD runs a long-lived process (gunicorn, not a script that exits).

② Application crash: Python/Django has an import error, missing dependency, or syntax error. Debug: docker logs <container> — look for traceback. Fix: fix the code, rebuild.

③ Wrong CMD: CMD is a shell command that finishes (e.g., CMD ["echo", "hello"]). Debug: docker inspect <image> | grep CMD — see what command runs. Fix: use a server command.

Explanation

Containers stay alive as long as their PID 1 (main process) is running. When PID 1 exits, the container stops. For web apps, PID 1 must be a server process (gunicorn, nginx) that runs indefinitely. The debugging pattern: docker logs first → see output → trace the issue. This is a practical skill for platform engineers troubleshooting deployments.

Q10
Explain the entire container lifecycle for deploying anihpj: from docker build to the container serving HTTP traffic on port 8000.
`n Show Answer
Answer

① Build: docker build -t ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 . — Docker reads the Dockerfile, executes each instruction as a layer, creates the image.

② Tag: Image is tagged with registry/repo:version for push.

③ Push: docker push ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 — uploads layers to registry. Only changed layers are pushed.

④ Pull: On target machine/K8s: docker pull ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 (or K8s pulls automatically).

⑤ Run: docker run -d -p 8000:8000 --name anihpj-app ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 — creates a container from the image, maps port 8000, runs in background.

⑥ Serve: Gunicorn starts, binds to 0.0.0.0:8000, Django loads, listens for HTTP requests.

Explanation

This is the complete container lifecycle every Platform Engineer must understand. On the CNPA exam, you may be asked about the flow: Build → Tag → Push → Pull → Run. Know each step's purpose and command. The K8s equivalent: kubectl apply triggers the kubelet to pull the image and start the container — same lifecycle, orchestrated automatically.

Chapter 3: Kubernetes Architecture — Deep Dive for Platform Engineers

Kubernetes is the engine room of every modern platform. Understanding its architecture — the Control Plane, Worker Nodes, and how they communicate — is essential for every Platform Engineer. This chapter takes you from "What is a cluster?" to "How does a pod get created?" using anihpj as our example throughout.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours ☸️ Architecture 🎯 Domain 1 & 4

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The Kubernetes cluster architecture — Control Plane + Worker Nodes
  • ✅ Deep dive into every Control Plane component (API Server, etcd, Scheduler, Controller Manager)
  • ✅ Worker Node components (kubelet, kube-proxy, Container Runtime)
  • ✅ The complete Pod creation journey — from kubectl to running container (8 steps)
  • ✅ The Kubernetes API — API groups, versions, and the reconciliation loop
  • ✅ Essential K8s objects every Platform Engineer must master

3.1 The Kubernetes Cluster — Your Platform's Engine Room

Complete Kubernetes Cluster Architecture

│  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │

3.2 Control Plane Components — The Platform Engineer's Perspective

a) kube-apiserver — "The Front Door"

Every kubectl command, every CI/CD deploy, every platform API call goes through the API Server. It handles authentication (who are you?), authorization (what can you do?), and admission control (should we allow this?). This is where your IDP's API integration happens!

b) etcd — "The Brain's Memory"

etcd is a distributed key-value store that holds ALL cluster state: every Pod, Service, ConfigMap, and Secret. It uses the Raft consensus algorithm and needs an odd number of nodes (3, 5, or 7). If you lose etcd, you lose everything — BACK IT UP!

c) kube-scheduler — "The Tetris Master"

Watches for new pods with no assigned node and decides where to place them. Uses a 2-step process: Filtering (which nodes can run this pod?) and Scoring (which is the best fit?). Considers resource requests, affinity rules, and taints/tolerations.

d) kube-controller-manager — "The Reconciliation Loop"

The Reconciliation Loop
         ┌──────────────┐
    ┌───▶│  OBSERVE     │  "What's the current state?"
    │    │  (Watch)     │
    │    └──────┬───────┘
    │           ▼
    │    ┌──────────────┐
    │    │  COMPARE      │  "Does current = desired?"
    │    │  (Diff)       │  "I want 3 pods, but only 2 running"
    │    └──────┬───────┘
    │       ┌───┴───┐
    │       ▼       ▼
    │  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
    │  │ MATCH! │ │DIFFERENT│
    │  │Do nothing│ │  ACT!  │  "Create pod #3"
    │  └───┬────┘ └───┬────┘
    │      │          │
    └──────┴──────────┘

3.3 Worker Node Components — Where the Work Happens

a) kubelet — "The Node Manager"

Runs on every worker node as a systemd service. Takes PodSpecs from the API server and ensures containers are running and healthy. Reports node/pod health back to the control plane.

b) kube-proxy — "The Traffic Cop"

Network proxy on every node that maintains network rules (iptables/IPVS). Implements the Kubernetes Service abstraction — routes traffic to the correct backend pod.

c) Container Runtime — "The Engine"

Actually runs containers. containerd is the default since K8s 1.24 (Docker deprecated). Kubernetes is runtime-agnostic via the CRI (Container Runtime Interface).

3.4 The Pod Creation Journey — From kubectl to Running Container

1
kubectl apply

User runs kubectl apply -f deploy.yaml

2
API Server

AuthN → AuthZ → Admission → Write to etcd

3
etcd

"Deployment anihpj: desired=3 replicas"

4
Deploy Controller

"New deploy → create ReplicaSet"

5
RS Controller

"0 pods, need 3 → create Pod specs"

6
Scheduler

"3 unscheduled pods → assign to nodes 1,2,3"

7
kubelet

"I see pod → pull image → start container"

8
Running!

"Pod anihpj-abc123 is Running!" 🎉

⏱️ Total Time

~2-5 seconds for the entire journey! (vs ~30 minutes for manual deployment)

3.5 Kubernetes API — The Platform Engineer's Interface

The K8s API is organized into API groups (core, apps, networking, rbac, storage), each with versions (v1 for stable, v1beta1 for beta) and resources (pods, deployments, services). Your IDP will talk to K8s via its API — understanding this is crucial for Platform Engineering.

API GroupStable VersionKey Resources
core (empty)v1Pod, Service, Node, Namespace, ConfigMap, Secret
appsv1Deployment, StatefulSet, DaemonSet, ReplicaSet
networking.k8s.iov1Ingress, NetworkPolicy, IngressClass
rbac.authorization.k8s.iov1Role, RoleBinding, ClusterRole, ClusterRoleBinding
storage.k8s.iov1StorageClass, PersistentVolume, PersistentVolumeClaim

3.6 Kubernetes Objects Every Platform Engineer Must Master

📦
Pod

Smallest deployable unit. One or more containers sharing network/storage. anihpj runs in a Pod with one Django container.

🔄
Deployment

Declarative updates for Pods and ReplicaSets. "I want 3 replicas of anihpj running at all times" — K8s makes it happen.

🌐
Service

Stable network endpoint for pods. Types: ClusterIP (internal), NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName.

📝
ConfigMap

Decouple configuration from container images. Store Django settings, database URLs, feature flags.

🔐
Secret

Store sensitive data: passwords, tokens, API keys. Base64-encoded (configure encryption at rest!).

📁
Namespace

Virtual cluster partitioning. Separate dev, staging, prod environments for anihpj within one cluster.

         └────────┘   └────────┘    └────────┘
📖 How These Objects Connect

An Ingress routes external traffic to a Service, which load-balances to Pods. A Deployment manages a ReplicaSet, which maintains the desired number of Pods. ConfigMaps and Secrets provide configuration to Pods as environment variables or mounted files.

✅ Chapter 3 Summary — Kubernetes Architecture Deep Dive

You've explored the engine room of cloud native: the Kubernetes architecture. The Control Plane (kube-apiserver → etcd → scheduler → controller-manager) acts as the cluster's brain, while Worker Nodes (kubelet → kube-proxy → container runtime) execute the work. You traced the complete Pod creation journey from `kubectl apply` through API server validation, etcd persistence, scheduler assignment, and kubelet container startup. You now understand that K8s is a declarative system — you declare desired state, and controllers continuously reconcile reality to match. The 12 key K8s objects (Pod, Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, Secret, Namespace, HPA, PVC, NetworkPolicy, ServiceAccount, RBAC) are your platform engineering toolbox.

☸️ Key Takeaway 1

Control Plane = Brain (API, etcd, scheduler, controllers). Worker Nodes = Muscles (kubelet, kube-proxy, container runtime). Both communicate via API server only.

☸️ Key Takeaway 2

Declarative model: You say "I want 3 replicas of anihpj" → controllers reconcile. No step-by-step instructions needed. This is the core K8s philosophy.

☸️ Key Takeaway 3

etcd is the single source of truth. If etcd loses data, the cluster loses its memory. Always back up etcd. Always. (This WILL be on the CNPA exam.)

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — K8s Architecture & Objects (Domain 1, 36%)

The K8s architecture is the #1 tested topic on the CNPA exam. Memorize: (1) Control Plane components and their ONE job — API server = front door (ALL communication flows through it), etcd = brain's memory (ALL cluster state), scheduler = pod placement (decides WHERE, not HOW), controller-manager = reconciliation engine (makes reality match desired state). (2) Worker Node components — kubelet = node manager (starts pods per scheduler), kube-proxy = network rules (iptables/IPVS), container runtime = runs containers (containerd, CRI-O). (3) The Pod creation journey (kubectl → API → etcd → controller → scheduler → kubelet → running) is a favorite exam scenario question. (4) Declarative model = you say WHAT (3 replicas), controllers figure out HOW. This philosophy underpins ALL of K8s and is tested in multiple domains.

📝 Chapter 3 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
Name the four core Control Plane components in Kubernetes and describe each one's single primary responsibility. Where are they deployed on a kubeadm cluster?
Show Answer
Answer

① kube-apiserver — Front door. ALL communication (kubectl, controllers, kubelet) goes through it. RESTful API, authentication, authorization, admission control.

② etcd — Brain's memory. Distributed key-value store holding ALL cluster state (pods, nodes, configs, secrets). Single source of truth.

③ kube-scheduler — Pod placement. Watches for unscheduled pods and assigns them to the best node based on resources, affinity, taints.

④ kube-controller-manager — Reconciliation engine. Runs controllers (Deployment, ReplicaSet, Node, etc.) that continuously reconcile desired state with actual state.

On kubeadm clusters, all four run as static pods in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

Explanation

This is the #1 most tested CNPA question pattern. You must know each component's ONE job. Common exam trap: saying "scheduler runs pods" — NO, the scheduler ASSIGNS pods to nodes; kubelet runs them. "Controller-manager deploys apps" — NO, it RECONCILES state. Static pods = pods managed directly by kubelet from manifest files, not via API server.

Q2
Trace the complete Pod creation journey from kubectl apply -f deployment.yaml to a running container. List all 8 steps in order.
Show Answer
Answer

① kubectl → API Server (AuthN, AuthZ, Admission). ② API Server → etcd (persist desired state). ③ Deployment Controller detects new Deployment → creates ReplicaSet. ④ ReplicaSet Controller detects desired=3, actual=0 → creates 3 Pod specs in etcd. ⑤ Scheduler detects unscheduled Pods → assigns each to a suitable node (writes binding to etcd). ⑥ kubelet on each node detects assigned Pod → pulls image. ⑦ kubelet starts container via CRI (containerd). ⑧ kubelet reports Pod status back to API Server → Pod shows "Running."

Explanation

The Pod creation journey tests your understanding of the full K8s control flow. Key insight: everything flows through etcd (write → watch → reconcile). Controllers WATCH etcd for changes, then ACT, then WRITE back. This is the reconciliation loop in action. The entire journey takes ~2-5 seconds.

Q3
What is the reconciliation loop (control loop) in Kubernetes? A Deployment specifies 3 replicas, but you manually delete one pod. Explain exactly what happens and which component is responsible.
Show Answer
Answer

The ReplicaSet controller (running inside kube-controller-manager) WATCHES pod state via the API server. When you delete a pod, the controller OBSERVES: current=2, desired=3 (from ReplicaSet spec). DIFF = -1. ACT: creates a new Pod to restore count to 3. The loop: Observe → Diff → Act → Repeat — runs continuously, never stops.

Explanation

The reconciliation loop is the fundamental K8s design pattern. Every controller implements it: watch for changes, compare desired vs actual, take action to converge. This self-healing behavior means you never need to manually fix pod counts — K8s does it automatically. The CNPA exam may ask: "Which K8s design pattern ensures self-healing?" → Reconciliation loop.

Q4
What are the three Worker Node components and what does each do? Which one actually runs the containers?
Show Answer
Answer

① kubelet — Node manager. Registers node with API server, watches for Pods assigned to its node, ensures containers are running. Creates/runs static pods from /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.

② kube-proxy — Network rules. Maintains iptables/IPVS rules so Services can reach Pods. Handles load balancing for ClusterIP Services.

③ Container Runtime — Runs containers. containerd (or CRI-O) actually pulls images and starts/stops containers. Kubelet talks to the runtime via the CRI (Container Runtime Interface).

Explanation

Worker nodes execute user workloads. kubelet is the "foreman" — it tells the runtime what to run. kube-proxy is the "traffic cop" — it ensures network rules route traffic correctly. The container runtime (containerd) is the actual engine. Exam trap: "Docker runs containers in K8s" — NO, containerd runs them since K8s v1.24.

Q5
What is etcd and why is it the most critical component to back up? What happens if etcd loses its data?
Show Answer
Answer

etcd is a distributed, consistent key-value store. It stores ALL Kubernetes cluster state: every Pod, Node, Service, ConfigMap, Secret, Deployment — everything. It uses the Raft consensus algorithm to maintain consistency across multiple members.

If etcd loses data: The cluster loses ALL memory of what exists. Control plane components have no state to reconcile. The cluster is effectively dead. Pods may keep running (they're processes on nodes), but you can't create, update, or delete anything. Recovery requires etcd restore from backup.

Explanation

etcd backup is a critical platform engineering task. The exam emphasizes: (1) etcd = single source of truth, (2) ALWAYS back up etcd before upgrades, (3) etcd snapshots are taken with etcdctl snapshot save. This is tested in both CNPA and CKA exams. The backup should be stored OFF the cluster (S3, external storage).

Q6
What does it mean that Kubernetes is declarative? Contrast with an imperative approach and give an example using the anihpj Deployment.
Show Answer
Answer

Declarative: You declare the DESIRED state. "I want 3 replicas of anihpj running, each with 200m CPU and 256Mi memory." You write a YAML manifest + kubectl apply. K8s figures out HOW — it creates pods, schedules them, monitors them, replaces failed ones.

Imperative: You specify every STEP. "SSH to node1, run docker pull, docker run, SSH to node2, docker run, SSH to node3, docker run, set up nginx, configure health checks..." You manage every detail.

K8s is declarative: you write YAML, K8s reconciles continuously to maintain that state forever.

Explanation

The declarative model is the core K8s philosophy and appears across multiple CNPA domains. K8s, Terraform, and GitOps are all declarative. The exam contrasts declarative (K8s) with imperative (traditional scripts). Key phrase: "declare desired state; system reconciles to match it" — this exact phrasing may appear in answer choices.

Q7
What is the difference between a Deployment and a ReplicaSet? Why would you create a Deployment instead of directly creating a ReplicaSet?
Show Answer
Answer

ReplicaSet: Ensures a specified number of identical Pods are running at all times. If a Pod dies, the RS creates a new one. That's ALL it does.

Deployment: Manages ReplicaSets to provide declarative updates (rolling updates, rollbacks, pause/resume). When you change the image tag in a Deployment, it creates a new ReplicaSet and gradually shifts traffic (rolling update). If something goes wrong, you can kubectl rollout undo to the previous ReplicaSet.

You use Deployment because you need update management — ReplicaSet alone doesn't support zero-downtime updates or rollbacks.

Explanation

The Deployment → ReplicaSet → Pod hierarchy is tested on the CNPA. Deployment = update strategy (rolling, recreate). ReplicaSet = pod count guarantee. Pod = running containers. You almost never create ReplicaSets directly — Deployments create and manage them. This abstraction is key to K8s' update capabilities.

Q8
What are ConfigMaps and Secrets? How are they different and when should you use each for the anihpj Django app?
Show Answer
Answer

ConfigMap: Stores non-sensitive configuration data as key-value pairs. Use for: Django settings (DEBUG=False), database URL (postgres://...), feature flags, environment names. Mounted as environment variables or files in Pods.

Secret: Stores sensitive data. Use for: SECRET_KEY, database passwords, API tokens. Base64-encoded by default (NOT encrypted — enable encryption at rest!). Can be mounted as environment variables or files.

Key difference: ConfigMaps = non-sensitive config. Secrets = passwords/tokens. Secrets have additional protections: they're only sent to nodes that need them, stored in tmpfs (not disk), and can be encrypted at rest.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests ConfigMap vs Secret. Common trap: thinking Secrets are encrypted — they're only base64-encoded (easily decoded). Always enable etcd encryption for Secrets in production. For anihpj: Django SECRET_KEY → Secret. Database URL, DEBUG flag → ConfigMap. This separation is a platform security best practice.

Q9
What is a Service in Kubernetes and what problem does it solve? Describe the four Service types.
Show Answer
Answer

A Service provides a stable network endpoint for a set of Pods. Pods are ephemeral — they get new IPs when restarted. A Service gives you a permanent IP/DNS name that load-balances to healthy Pods.

ClusterIP (default): Internal-only, reachable within cluster. Used for service-to-service communication.

NodePort: Opens a port (30000-32767) on EVERY node. For development/testing.

LoadBalancer: Provisions a cloud load balancer (AWS NLB, GCP LB). External access in production.

ExternalName: DNS CNAME alias to an external service (e.g., external-db.anihpj.com).

Explanation

Services are a K8s fundamental. Key insight: Service selects Pods by LABELS (app: anihpj), not by IP. When a Pod restarts with a new IP, the Service automatically updates its endpoint list. For anihpj: ClusterIP for internal Django ↔ DB communication, LoadBalancer or Ingress for external HTTP access.

Q10
You need to deploy the anihpj Django app on K8s. List ALL Kubernetes objects you need (minimum 8), in the order you'd create them, and explain each one's purpose.
Show Answer
Answer

① Namespace — Logical isolation (anihpj-prod). ② Secret — Database password, Django SECRET_KEY. ③ ConfigMap — Database URL, DEBUG flag, allowed hosts. ④ PersistentVolumeClaim — Storage for media uploads (if needed). ⑤ Deployment — 3 replicas of anihpj with resource limits + probes. ⑥ Service — ClusterIP to expose anihpj internally on port 80. ⑦ Ingress — External HTTP access via api.anihpj.com with TLS. ⑧ HPA — Auto-scale between 3-20 replicas based on CPU.

Explanation

This question tests your ability to think in K8s objects — the core platform engineering skill. The order matters: create namespace first (resources are namespaced), then secrets/configs (needed by Deployment), then Deployment (references them), then expose it (Service/Ingress). This is the minimum production setup for any service on your platform.

Chapter 4: Infrastructure as Code (IaC) Fundamentals

Infrastructure as Code is the practice of managing infrastructure through machine-readable definition files rather than manual processes. For Platform Engineers, IaC is the foundation of reproducible, version-controlled infrastructure. We'll explore Terraform, compare IaC tools, and provision an EKS cluster for anihpj.

🟢 Beginner ⏱️ ~2 hours 🏗️ Hands-On 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What IaC is and why it eliminates "snowflake servers"
  • ✅ Declarative vs Imperative approaches to infrastructure
  • ✅ Terraform: init → plan → apply → destroy lifecycle
  • ✅ How Terraform compares to Pulumi, Crossplane, and Ansible
  • ✅ 10 IaC best practices every Platform Engineer should follow
  • ✅ Hands-on: Provision an EKS cluster with Terraform for anihpj

4.1 What is Infrastructure as Code?

Manual vs IaC
  ❌ MANUAL (Click-Ops)                    ✅ INFRASTRUCTURE AS CODE
  ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐    ┌─────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
  │🖥️Server1│ │🖥️Server2│ │🖥️Server3│    │ 📁 Git Repo │────▶│ 🖥️🖥️🖥️        │
  │ Python  │ │ Python  │ │ Python  │    │  main.tf    │     │ Identical    │
  │ 3.8?!   │ │ 3.11 ✓ │ │ 3.10?!  │    │  vars.tf    │     │ infra on all │
  │No nginx │ │ nginx ✓ │ │ Apache?!│    └─────────────┘     └──────────────┘
  └─────────┘ └─────────┘ └─────────┘
  "Snowflake" servers — each unique!     Version-controlled, reproducible!

4.2 Declarative vs Imperative Approaches

🗣️ Imperative (HOW)

"Go to server, install Python 3.11, install Django, configure nginx on port 80, restart service" — YOU specify every step.

📜 Declarative (WHAT)

"I want: Python 3.11 installed, Django 5.2 running, nginx on port 80" — the TOOL figures out HOW. Kubernetes YAML and Terraform HCL are declarative.

4.3 Terraform — The Industry Standard for IaC

Terraform Workflow
  ① WRITE              ② INIT               ③ PLAN              ④ APPLY
  ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐
  │ main.tf  │───────▶│terraform │───────▶│terraform │───────▶│terraform │
  │ vars.tf  │        │  init    │        │  plan    │        │  apply   │
  │outputs.tf│        │          │        │          │        │          │
  └──────────┘        └────┬─────┘        └────┬─────┘        └────┬─────┘
                           │                   │                    │
                           ▼                   ▼                    ▼
                      ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐
                      │Downloads │        │  Shows   │        │ ☁️ Cloud  │
                      │providers │        │ changes  │        │Resources │
                      └──────────┘        └──────────┘        │Created!  │
                                                              └──────────┘

4.5 IaC Best Practices for Platform Engineers

✅ 10 IaC Best Practices Checklist
  1. Store ALL IaC in Git — no manual changes, ever!
  2. Remote state storage — S3 + DynamoDB lock (never local)
  3. Use modules — DRY, reusable, tested components
  4. CI/CD for IaC — plan on PR, apply on merge to main
  5. Drift detection — run plan weekly to catch configuration drift
  6. NEVER hardcode secrets — use variables + secret management
  7. Tag EVERYTHING — cost allocation, ownership tracking
  8. Version pin providers — prevent unexpected upgrades
  9. Use .gitignore — exclude .terraform/, state files, credentials
  10. Document modules — README with inputs, outputs, examples

✅ Chapter 4 Summary — Infrastructure as Code Foundations

You've mastered the IaC fundamentals that every Platform Engineer needs: the difference between declarative (WHAT) and imperative (HOW), the Terraform lifecycle (write → init → plan → apply → destroy), and how Terraform compares to Pulumi, Crossplane, and Ansible. You've learned 10 IaC best practices — from remote state storage to drift detection — that separate production-grade infrastructure from hobby projects. The hands-on EKS provisioning exercise shows how a complete platform infrastructure (VPC → EKS → node groups) can be defined in code, versioned in Git, and deployed repeatably. This is the foundation for Domain 4 (Platform APIs & Infra Provisioning).

🏗️ Key Takeaway 1

Declarative = "I want 3 servers" (tool figures out how). Imperative = "Create server1, then server2, then server3" (you specify every step). K8s and Terraform are declarative.

🏗️ Key Takeaway 2

Terraform state (.tfstate) is the source of truth — store it remotely (S3+DynamoDB), never locally. Losing state means losing the mapping between code and real infra.

🏗️ Key Takeaway 3

Crossplane runs inside K8s and uses CRDs to represent cloud resources. Terraform runs outside K8s in CI/CD. Together they cover both initial provisioning and ongoing self-service.

📝 Chapter 4 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is Infrastructure as Code (IaC) and what problem does it solve? How would anihpj benefit from IaC?
Show Answer
Answer

IaC manages infrastructure through machine-readable definition files rather than manual processes (click-ops) or scripts. Instead of clicking through AWS console to create a server, you write a main.tf file and run terraform apply.

Problems solved: ① Snowflake servers — every server identical, defined by code. ② Configuration drift — Terraform detects and can fix drift. ③ Version control — infrastructure in Git, with history, reviews, rollbacks. ④ Reproducibility — same code = same infra in dev, staging, prod.

Explanation

IaC is a foundational CNPA concept (Domain 4, 12%). The exam tests WHY IaC matters: reproducibility, version control, drift detection, collaboration. Key phrase: "eliminates snowflake servers" — every server provisioned by IaC is identical. For anihpj: the entire EKS cluster, RDS database, and VPC are defined in Terraform — fully reproducible.

Q2
Explain the difference between declarative and imperative approaches. Is Terraform declarative or imperative?
Show Answer
Answer

Declarative (WHAT): "I want: an EKS cluster with 3 t3.large nodes in us-east-1, PostgreSQL RDS, and a VPC." The tool figures out HOW to create them, in what order, handling dependencies.

Imperative (HOW): "Step 1: create VPC. Step 2: create subnets. Step 3: create EKS cluster. Step 4: create RDS. Step 5: configure security groups." YOU specify every step and order.

Terraform is declarative — you write HCL describing desired resources, Terraform builds a dependency graph and executes in the correct order. Similarly, Kubernetes YAML is declarative.

Explanation

Declarative vs Imperative is a core CNPA concept across multiple domains. Remember: Kubernetes YAML, Terraform HCL, GitOps manifests = declarative. Bash scripts, Ansible playbooks = imperative. The exam asks: "Which approach does Terraform use?" → Declarative. "What does declarative mean?" → State desired outcome; tool determines execution steps.

Q3
Describe the Terraform lifecycle: init → plan → apply → destroy. What happens at each stage?
Show Answer
Answer

① init — Downloads provider plugins (AWS, GCP, K8s). Initializes backend for state storage. Creates .terraform/ directory.

② plan — Reads current state + config. Computes diff: what needs to be created, updated, or destroyed. Shows you the changes BEFORE applying. Safe to run anytime — makes no changes.

③ apply — Executes the plan. Creates/updates/destroys real infrastructure. Writes new state to backend. Prompts for confirmation (unless -auto-approve).

④ destroy — Removes ALL resources managed by the configuration. Reads state, deletes everything. Use with caution!

Explanation

The Terraform lifecycle is tested on CNPA Domain 4. Key exam points: (1) ALWAYS run plan before apply — it's your safety net. (2) plan is read-only — zero risk. (3) apply requires confirmation. (4) State file (.tfstate) is the bridge between your code and real infrastructure — store it remotely!

Q4
What is Terraform state (.tfstate)? Why should it be stored remotely (S3 + DynamoDB) and NEVER committed to Git?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform state is a JSON file that maps your Terraform configuration to real-world resources. It tracks resource IDs, attributes, and dependencies. When you run plan, Terraform compares your config (.tf files) against state (.tfstate) to compute changes.

Remote storage (S3+DynamoDB): ① Team collaboration — multiple engineers can work on same infra (DynamoDB locks prevent concurrent modifications). ② No local state sync issues. ③ State is backed up automatically (S3 versioning).

NEVER in Git: State contains secrets (resource outputs, connection strings, potentially passwords). It's also machine-generated — not human-readable source code.

Explanation

State management is a critical production concern. Losing state = losing the mapping between code and real infra. You'd have to manually import every resource. The CNPA exam tests: (1) Where to store state? Remote backend (S3, GCS, Azure Storage). (2) How to prevent concurrent modifications? State locking (DynamoDB). (3) Why not in Git? Security + not source code.

Q5
Compare Terraform, Pulumi, Crossplane, and Ansible. For each: what language does it use, where does it run, and what is its primary strength?
Show Answer
Answer
ToolLanguageRunsStrength
TerraformHCLOutside K8s (CI/CD)Multi-cloud, largest ecosystem
PulumiPython/TS/GoOutside K8s (CI/CD)Real programming languages
CrossplaneYAML (K8s API)INSIDE K8sSelf-service dev APIs
AnsibleYAMLOutside (SSH)Config mgmt, not provisioning
Explanation

This comparison is essential for CNPA Domain 4. Key distinction: Terraform + Pulumi = provision infrastructure from OUTSIDE K8s. Crossplane = provision infrastructure from INSIDE K8s via CRDs. Ansible = configure existing servers, NOT provision new ones. The exam asks: "Which tool runs inside Kubernetes?" → Crossplane.

Q6
What are Terraform modules and why should you use them? Give an example for anihpj infrastructure.
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform modules are reusable, composable units of Terraform configuration. Like functions in programming — they encapsulate a set of resources with inputs and outputs.

anihpj example: A postgresql-database module that takes inputs (name, size, engine version) and creates: RDS instance, security group, subnet group, and outputs the connection string. Used in dev, staging, and prod — same module, different inputs.

Why modules: ① DRY — don't copy-paste RDS config. ② Tested once, used everywhere. ③ Teams share modules via registry. ④ Version modules for safe upgrades.

Explanation

Modules are a Terraform best practice. The CNPA exam expects you to understand the concept — reusable infrastructure components. Think of them as "infrastructure libraries." The Terraform Registry (registry.terraform.io) hosts thousands of community modules. AWS EKS module used in anihpj is a popular example.

Q7
What is configuration drift in IaC and how does Terraform detect and handle it?
Show Answer
Answer

Configuration drift is when the actual state of infrastructure diverges from the IaC-defined state. Example: someone manually changes the RDS instance class from db.t4g.medium to db.t4g.large via AWS console. Your Terraform config still says db.t4g.medium.

Detection: Run terraform plan — it compares state (from last apply) against current real-world state. Drift shows as changes. terraform plan -refresh-only updates state to match reality without making changes.

Handling: Either: (1) Update config to match reality (if change was intentional), or (2) Run terraform apply to revert to desired state.

Explanation

Drift detection is why IaC is superior to scripts. A bash script doesn't know if someone manually changed a server after it ran. Terraform detects drift every time you run plan. Best practice: run terraform plan weekly (automated) to catch drift early. For platform engineering: NEVER make manual changes — always go through IaC.

Q8
List 5 IaC best practices for production infrastructure. Why is each important?
Show Answer
Answer

① Remote state storage (S3+DynamoDB) — enables team collaboration, prevents concurrent modifications, backs up state.

② Version pin providers (version = "~> 5.0") — prevents unexpected provider updates breaking your infra.

③ Use modules — DRY, tested, reusable infrastructure components.

④ CI/CD for IaC — terraform plan on PR, terraform apply on merge to main. No local applies.

⑤ NEVER hardcode secrets — use variables + external secret management (Vault, AWS Secrets Manager).

Explanation

Production IaC requires discipline. These 5 practices separate hobby projects from enterprise-grade infrastructure. The CNPA exam tests IaC maturity: manual terraform apply from laptop = immature. Automated plan on PR + apply on merge = mature. Secrets in code = vulnerability. Secrets from Vault = secure.

Q9
What is the difference between Terraform and Crossplane for provisioning infrastructure? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform: Runs OUTSIDE K8s (in CI/CD pipelines). Uses HCL language. Maintains state file (.tfstate). Best for: initial cluster provisioning (EKS, VPC, IAM), infrastructure that doesn't change frequently, multi-cloud setups.

Crossplane: Runs INSIDE K8s. Uses K8s CRDs/YAML. State stored in etcd (no separate state file). Best for: ongoing developer self-service (databases, buckets, queues), infrastructure managed via GitOps, platform APIs.

Together: Terraform provisions EKS + core infra → Crossplane lets developers self-serve databases and services on top.

Explanation

Terraform vs Crossplane is a key CNPA Domain 4 comparison. Remember: Terraform = outside K8s, CI/CD-driven, initial provisioning. Crossplane = inside K8s, developer-facing, ongoing self-service. They complement each other — most mature platforms use BOTH. The exam may ask: "Which tool runs as a Kubernetes operator?" → Crossplane.

Q10
You run terraform apply and it fails halfway through — some resources were created, some weren't. What happens to the state? What should you do next?
Show Answer
Answer

Terraform saves state only for successfully created resources. Failed resources are NOT recorded in state. The state file reflects partial success: 3 resources created, 2 failed.

Next steps: ① Fix the error (read error message — usually permissions, quotas, or naming conflicts). ② Run terraform plan — it sees: 3 resources already exist (in state), 2 still need creation. ③ Run terraform apply again — it only creates the remaining 2, skipping the 3 already created. This is idempotent and safe.

Explanation

Terraform is designed for partial failure recovery. The state file is the key: it tracks what was successfully created, so re-running apply only addresses what's missing. This idempotency is a core IaC property — running the same configuration multiple times produces the same result. Unlike bash scripts, which might create duplicate resources on re-run.

5.1 The anihpj Application — Understanding What We're Deploying

anihpj Application — Component View
  ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐     ┌──────────────┐
  │   👤 Users   │────▶│   📋 JobPost │◀────│  🏷️ Skills  │
  │  username    │     │  title       │     │  name        │
  │  email       │     │  description │     │  category    │
  └──────┬───────┘     │  salary      │     └──────────────┘
         │             │  location    │
         │             └──────┬───────┘
         │                    │
         │             ┌──────┴───────┐
