5.2 Creating Kubernetes Manifests for anihpj

We need 7 manifests to fully deploy anihpj to Kubernetes. Here's each one with explanations:

01-namespace.yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod
  labels:
    environment: production
    team: platform-engineering
02-deployment.yaml
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
      - name: django
        image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.0.0
        ports:
        - containerPort: 8000
        envFrom:
        - configMapRef:
            name: anihpj-config
        - secretRef:
            name: anihpj-secret
        resources:
          requests: { memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m" }
          limits: { memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m" }
        livenessProbe:
          httpGet: { path: /health/, port: 8000 }
          initialDelaySeconds: 30
        readinessProbe:
          httpGet: { path: /health/, port: 8000 }
          initialDelaySeconds: 10
📖 Manifest Anatomy

Every manifest has 4 required fields: apiVersion, kind, metadata, and spec. The apiVersion depends on the resource kind — use kubectl explain <resource> to find the right version.

Deployment Manifest — Field-by-Field Anatomy
  apiVersion: apps/v1          ← API group & version
  kind: Deployment             ← Resource type
  metadata:
    name: anihpj               ← Unique name in namespace
    namespace: anihpj-prod     ← Which namespace
    labels:                    ← Identifiers for organization
      app: anihpj
  spec:
    replicas: 3                ← How many Pods to run
    selector:                  ← How to find managed Pods
      matchLabels:
        app: anihpj
    template:                  ← Pod blueprint
      metadata:
        labels:                ← Must match selector!
          app: anihpj
      spec:
        containers:            ← Container definitions
        - name: django
          image: ghcr.io/anihpj:v1.0.0
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8000 }]
          envFrom:             ← Inject ConfigMaps/Secrets
          - configMapRef: { name: anihpj-config }
          - secretRef: { name: anihpj-secret }
          resources:           ← CPU/Memory requests & limits
            requests: { memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m" }
            limits: { memory: "512Mi", cpu: "500m" }
          livenessProbe:       ← Is the app alive?
            httpGet: { path: /health/, port: 8000 }
          readinessProbe:      ← Ready to serve traffic?
            httpGet: { path: /health/, port: 8000 }

5.4 Verifying and Debugging the Deployment

CommandWhat It ShowsWhen to Use
kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prodPod status (Running/Pending/CrashLoop)First check after deploy
kubectl describe pod <name>Detailed pod info, events, conditionsPod not starting? Check here
kubectl logs <pod>Application stdout/stderrApp errors, Django startup logs
kubectl exec -it <pod> -- bashShell inside containerDebug inside the container
kubectl port-forward svc/anihpj 8000:8000Local access to serviceTest without Ingress
Pod Debugging Decision Tree
  kubectl get pods
       │
       ▼
  ┌──────────────┐
  │ Pod STATUS?  │
  └──────┬───────┘
         │
    ┌────┼────────────┬───────────────┐
    ▼    ▼            ▼               ▼
  Pend-  ImagePull-  CrashLoop-    Running
  ing    BackOff     BackOff       but not
    │       │           │          working
    ▼       ▼           ▼               │
  Check  Check        Check             ▼
  node   image name,  logs:           Check
  resource registry   kubectl         Service
  limits  auth,       logs <pod>      endpoints,
          network                     Ingress

  💡 Common Fixes:
  • Pending → Check node resources: kubectl describe node
  • ImagePullBackOff → Check registry auth & image name
  • CrashLoopBackOff → Check logs: kubectl logs <pod>
  • Running but unreachable → Check Service & Ingress

5.5 The anihpj Dockerfile — Production-Ready

Dockerfile
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends gcc libpq-dev && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY . .
RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput

FROM python:3.11-slim
RUN addgroup --system anihpj && adduser --system --group anihpj
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends libpq5 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
COPY --from=builder /app/static /app/static
COPY --from=builder /app /app
USER anihpj
EXPOSE 8000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health/ || exit 1
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "4"]

✅ Chapter 5 Summary — Deploying anihpj to Kubernetes

You've taken the anihpj Django app from source code to running on Kubernetes. You wrote every manifest needed: Namespace → ConfigMap → Secret → Deployment → Service → Ingress. You learned the critical manifest structure pattern (apiVersion + kind + metadata + spec) that applies to ALL K8s resources. You configured probes (liveness = "is it alive?", readiness = "can it serve?") and set resource requests/limits. Most importantly, you experienced the declarative workflow: write YAML → kubectl apply → K8s reconciles to match. This is the exact workflow you'll use on the CNPA exam and every day as a Platform Engineer.

☸️ Key Takeaway 1

Every K8s manifest follows the same pattern: apiVersion + kind + metadata + spec. Once you learn one, you can read them all.

☸️ Key Takeaway 2

Probes are critical: livenessProbe restarts dead containers, readinessProbe stops traffic to unhealthy ones. Without them, K8s can't manage your app.

☸️ Key Takeaway 3

kubectl apply is declarative — you tell K8s WHAT you want, not HOW. K8s controllers continuously reconcile reality to match your desired state.

📝 Chapter 5 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the standard structure of every Kubernetes YAML manifest? List the four required top-level fields.
Show Answer
Answer

Every K8s manifest has: apiVersion (API group/version, e.g., apps/v1), kind (resource type, e.g., Deployment), metadata (name, namespace, labels), spec (desired state — what varies per resource). Some resources also have data (ConfigMaps, Secrets) or rules (RBAC).

Explanation

This is the most fundamental K8s skill. Every resource — Pod, Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, Secret — follows this pattern. The CNPA exam expects you to recognize it. Once you learn the pattern, you can read ANY K8s manifest and understand it. The exam may show a malformed YAML and ask you to identify what's missing — usually apiVersion or kind.

Q2
What is the difference between livenessProbe and readinessProbe? What happens when each fails for the anihpj container?
Show Answer
Answer

livenessProbe: "Is the container ALIVE?" If it fails → K8s RESTARTS the container. Use when your app can deadlock or hang. Be careful: a misconfigured liveness probe can cause restart loops.

readinessProbe: "Can the container SERVE traffic?" If it fails → K8s REMOVES the Pod from Service endpoints. The container keeps running but receives no traffic. Use for: app still starting up, dependency not ready, temporary overload.

Explanation

Probes are critical for production K8s. Common exam trap: confusing "restart" (liveness) with "stop traffic" (readiness). Key rule: readinessProbe should be more forgiving (longer initialDelaySeconds, higher failureThreshold) than livenessProbe — you want to stop traffic before you kill the container.

Q3
Write the minimum Deployment YAML for anihpj with 3 replicas, resource requests (200m CPU, 256Mi memory), and limits (500m CPU, 512Mi memory).
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: anihpj
          image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8000
          resources:
            requests:
              cpu: 200m
              memory: 256Mi
            limits:
              cpu: 500m
              memory: 512Mi
Explanation

The selector MUST match the template labels — this is the #1 YAML error beginners make. If spec.selector.matchLabels doesn't match spec.template.metadata.labels, the Deployment creation fails. Resource requests = guaranteed minimum (scheduler uses this). Limits = maximum allowed (container throttled if exceeded).

Q4
What command applies a YAML file to Kubernetes? What's the difference between kubectl apply and kubectl create?
Show Answer
Answer

kubectl apply -f manifest.yaml — Declarative. Creates OR updates resources. If resource exists, patches it to match the YAML. Safe to run repeatedly (idempotent). Uses last-applied-configuration annotation to track changes.

kubectl create -f manifest.yaml — Imperative. Creates resources ONLY. Fails with AlreadyExists error if resource exists. Good for one-time setup, bad for updates.

Rule: Always use kubectl apply for deploying applications. Use kubectl create only when you're sure the resource doesn't exist.

Explanation

apply vs create is a core K8s distinction that maps to the declarative vs imperative philosophy. apply is declarative — "make it look like this YAML." create is imperative — "create this one thing once." Platform engineers should default to apply for all deployments; it's what GitOps tools (ArgoCD, Flux) use internally.

Q5
You deploy anihpj but can't access it. List the top 5 debugging steps in order to diagnose the issue.
Show Answer
Answer

① kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod — Are pods Running? If Pending → resource issues or scheduling. If CrashLoopBackOff → app error.

② kubectl describe pod <pod> -n anihpj-prod — Check Events section. See why a pod is failing (image pull error, OOMKilled, probe failure).

③ kubectl logs <pod> -n anihpj-prod — Check application logs. Django traceback, database connection error, missing config.

④ kubectl get svc -n anihpj-prod — Service exists? Endpoints populated? If no endpoints, selector doesn't match pods.

⑤ kubectl get ingress -n anihpj-prod — Ingress exists? Host matches? TLS configured? Ingress controller running?

Explanation

This 5-step debugging flow is EXACTLY what you do as a platform engineer. The order matters: pods first (is it running?), then service (is it reachable internally?), then ingress (is it exposed externally?). Common issues: missing namespace in kubectl commands, selector mismatch between Service and Pods, Ingress controller not installed.

Q6
What is a Namespace in Kubernetes? Why should you use separate namespaces for dev, staging, and production of anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

A Namespace is a virtual cluster partition within a physical K8s cluster. It provides: scope for resource names (anihpj-dev vs anihpj-prod), RBAC isolation (team-A can't access team-B's namespace), resource quotas (limit CPU/memory per namespace), and logical organization.

Why separate: ① Isolation — a bug in dev won't affect prod. ② RBAC — devs can deploy to dev, only platform team can deploy to prod. ③ Resource quotas — dev gets smaller limits than prod. ④ Clean separation — kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod shows only production.

Explanation

Namespaces are a fundamental platform engineering concept. Best practice: one namespace per environment per application. anihpj-dev, anihpj-staging, anihpj-prod. Some orgs also use separate CLUSTERS for prod (stronger isolation), but namespaces are the minimum. The CNPA exam may ask about namespace isolation vs cluster isolation.

Q7
How do you expose anihpj's port 8000 to external HTTP traffic? Walk through Ingress, Service, and Deployment configuration.
Show Answer
Answer

① Deployment exposes containerPort: 8000 (the port Django/gunicorn listens on inside the container). ② Service (ClusterIP) maps port: 80 → targetPort: 8000 — Service listens on :80, forwards to Pod's :8000. ③ Ingress defines routing rules: host api.anihpj.com, path / → backend Service anihpj:80. Ingress Controller (NGINX) implements these rules and terminates TLS.

Traffic flow: Browser → api.anihpj.com:443 → Ingress Controller → Service anihpj:80 → Pod anihpj:8000.

Explanation

This is the standard K8s networking stack. Key insight: containerPort, targetPort, and Service port are three DIFFERENT things. containerPort = inside container. targetPort = Service → Pod. Service port = what other services see. The Ingress abstracts all of this behind a DNS name + TLS. This pattern appears in every production K8s deployment.

Q8
What are resource requests and limits? What happens when a container exceeds its CPU limit? Memory limit?
Show Answer
Answer

Requests: GUARANTEED minimum. Scheduler uses this to decide which node has enough capacity. Pod won't be scheduled if no node has requested resources available.

Limits: MAXIMUM allowed. Container can burst up to this but not beyond.

CPU limit exceeded: Container is THROTTLED — it runs slower but is NOT killed. K8s uses CFS (Completely Fair Scheduler) to cap CPU time.

Memory limit exceeded: Container is OOMKilled — immediately terminated and restarted. Memory cannot be throttled; the process must be killed.

Explanation

Resource management is a critical platform engineering skill. Key exam fact: CPU is compressible (throttle), memory is NOT (OOMKill). Always set memory limits slightly above normal usage to prevent OOMKills. CPU limits can be generous — throttling is annoying but not fatal. Best practice: set requests = typical usage, limits = 2-3x requests.

Q9
What is kubectl port-forward and when would you use it for debugging anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

kubectl port-forward pod/anihpj-xxxxx 8000:8000 -n anihpj-prod creates a temporary TCP tunnel from your local machine → K8s API server → target Pod. You access http://localhost:8000 as if you were inside the cluster.

Use cases: ① Debug a pod that has no Service/Ingress yet. ② Access internal-only services (ClusterIP) from your laptop. ③ Test a pod directly without going through load balancers. ④ Quick database access: kubectl port-forward svc/postgres 5432:5432.

NOT for production: Port-forward is a debugging tool — it goes through the API server (limited bandwidth, single connection). Use Services + Ingress for production traffic.

Explanation

Port-forward is a platform engineer's debugging superpower. It creates an ephemeral tunnel without modifying any K8s resources — no Service, no Ingress, no firewall rules. The CNPA exam may ask about debugging tools — port-forward is one of the most useful. It's safe because it doesn't change cluster state.

Q10
A developer says "I deployed anihpj but it doesn't work." Walk through your systematic debugging approach as a Platform Engineer. Include all kubectl commands.
Show Answer
Answer
# 1. Check deployment status
kubectl get deploy anihpj -n anihpj-prod
# Look at: DESIRED vs CURRENT vs READY. If READY < DESIRED, investigate pods.

# 2. Check pods
kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod -l app=anihpj
# Are they Running? Pending? CrashLoopBackOff? ErrImagePull?

# 3. Describe problematic pod
kubectl describe pod anihpj-xxxxx -n anihpj-prod
# Check Events at bottom: image pull error? OOMKilled? Mount failure?

# 4. Check logs
kubectl logs deployment/anihpj -n anihpj-prod --tail=50
# Django errors? Database connection refused? Missing SECRET_KEY?

# 5. Check service endpoints
kubectl get endpoints anihpj -n anihpj-prod
# Empty endpoints = selector mismatch between Service and Pods

# 6. Check ingress
kubectl describe ingress anihpj -n anihpj-prod
# Host configured? TLS secret exists? Backend service exists?
Explanation

This is the real daily work of a Platform Engineer. The systematic approach: Deployment → Pods → Describe → Logs → Service → Ingress. Always check Events in kubectl describe — it tells you EXACTLY what K8s thinks is wrong. The CNPA exam values this debugging methodology — knowing WHAT to check and in WHAT ORDER.

Chapter 6: Kubernetes Services, Ingress, and Networking for Platform Engineers

Networking is the backbone of any cloud native platform. This chapter covers the Kubernetes networking model, the four Service types, Ingress for HTTP routing, DNS, and Network Policies — everything you need to connect your anihpj application securely and reliably.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🌐 Networking 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The 4 fundamental rules of the Kubernetes networking model
  • ✅ All 4 Service types: ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName
  • ✅ Ingress for HTTP/HTTPS path-based and host-based routing
  • ✅ DNS-based service discovery with CoreDNS
  • ✅ Zero-trust networking with Network Policies
🎓 CNPA Relevance
Domain 1: PE Core Fundamentals 36% Domain 4: Platform APIs & Infra 12%
Networking on the CNPA Exam

The CNPA expects you to understand which Service type to use in which scenario, how Ingress differs from Services (L7 vs L4), and how Network Policies implement zero-trust. Know the DNS naming pattern: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local.

6.1 Kubernetes Networking Model — The 4 Rules

K8s Networking Model
  Node 1 (10.0.1.0/24)          Node 2 (10.0.2.0/24)
  ┌──────────────────┐          ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Pod A  Pod B     │          │ Pod C  Pod D     │
  │10.0.1.1 10.0.1.2 │◀────────▶│10.0.2.1 10.0.2.2 │
  │  │       │       │  Direct  │  │       │       │
  │  └───┬───┘       │  Pod-to- │  └───┬───┘       │
  │      │           │  Pod     │      │           │
  └──────┼───────────┘          └──────┼───────────┘
         │                             │
    ┌────┴────┐                   ┌────┴────┐
    │  cni0   │                   │  cni0   │
    │ bridge  │                   │ bridge  │
    └────┬────┘                   └────┬────┘
         │                             │
    ┌────┴────┐                   ┌────┴────┐
    │ eth0    │◀═════════════════▶│ eth0    │
    │10.0.0.1 │     Underlay     │10.0.0.2 │
    └─────────┘     Network      └─────────┘

  4 RULES:
  ① All pods can communicate with all other pods without NAT
  ② All nodes can communicate with all pods without NAT
  ③ A pod sees its own IP as the same IP other pods see
  ④ Containers within a pod share the network namespace

6.2 Services Deep Dive — The 4 Types

a) ClusterIP (Default)

Exposes the service on a cluster-internal IP. Only reachable from within the cluster. This is the default type and is used for pod-to-pod communication. The anihpj Django service uses ClusterIP internally.

b) NodePort

Exposes the service on each Node's IP at a static port (range: 30000-32767). You can access the service from outside the cluster using <NodeIP>:<NodePort>. Good for development and debugging.

c) LoadBalancer

Exposes the service externally using a cloud provider's load balancer. AWS creates an NLB/ALB, GCP creates a Cloud Load Balancer. This is the standard way to expose services in production on managed K8s.

d) ExternalName

Maps the service to a DNS name (CNAME). No proxying — just a DNS alias. Useful for accessing external services (like an RDS database) through a consistent internal name.

TypeAccessPortUse Case
ClusterIPInternal onlyAnyPod-to-pod communication
NodePortExternal (Node IP)30000-32767Dev/testing, debugging
LoadBalancerExternal (Cloud LB)AnyProduction traffic
ExternalNameDNS aliasN/AExternal service access

6.3 Ingress — HTTP/HTTPS Smart Routing

Ingress provides Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing to Services. Unlike Services (L4), Ingress can route based on hostname and URL path — enabling path-based and host-based routing.

ingress.yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: anihpj-ingress
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  rules:
  - host: anihpj.example.com
    http:
      paths:
      - path: /jobs/
        pathType: Prefix
        backend:
          service:
            name: anihpj-service
            port:
              number: 8000

6.4 DNS in Kubernetes — Service Discovery

Kubernetes runs CoreDNS as the cluster DNS server. Every Service gets a DNS record: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local. Pods can discover services by name without hardcoding IPs.

📖 DNS Pattern

anihpj pods can reach the database service at postgres-service.anihpj-prod.svc.cluster.local. This is how the Django settings.py configures DATABASE_HOST — by service name, not IP!

6.5 Network Policies — Firewall for Pods

By default, all pods can communicate with all other pods. Network Policies implement zero-trust networking — deny everything, then explicitly allow required traffic.

network-policy.yaml
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: anihpj-network-policy
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  policyTypes:
  - Ingress
  - Egress
  ingress:
  - from:
    - podSelector:
        matchLabels:
          role: frontend
    ports:
    - port: 8000
  egress:
  - to:
    - podSelector:
        matchLabels:
          app: postgres
    ports:
    - port: 5432

✅ Chapter 6 Summary — Kubernetes Services, Ingress & Networking

You now understand the complete Kubernetes networking stack: the flat network model (every Pod gets an IP, all Pods can communicate), the four Service types (ClusterIP for internal, NodePort for dev, LoadBalancer for cloud, ExternalName for DNS aliasing), Ingress for HTTP routing with TLS termination, CoreDNS for service discovery, and NetworkPolicy for zero-trust security. The anihpj networking path — Ingress (api.anihpj.com:443) → Service (:80) → Pods (:8000) with NetworkPolicy restricting traffic — is your reference architecture for Domain 1.

🌐 Key Takeaway 1

ClusterIP = internal-only (default). NodePort = opens port on every node. LoadBalancer = cloud LB provisioned. ExternalName = CNAME alias in CoreDNS.

🌐 Key Takeaway 2

Ingress = HTTP routing rules (hosts, paths, TLS). Ingress Controller (NGINX, Traefik) = the actual proxy that implements those rules.

🌐 Key Takeaway 3

NetworkPolicy = K8s firewall. Default: allow all. Best practice: deny all, then allow specific. Label-based selectors = zero-trust networking.

📝 Chapter 6 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the Kubernetes networking model? State the key rules for Pod-to-Pod communication.
Show Answer
Answer

The K8s networking model has fundamental rules: ① Every Pod gets its own IP address. ② Pods on any node can communicate with Pods on any other node WITHOUT NAT (Network Address Translation). ③ Containers within a Pod share the Pod's IP and can communicate via localhost. ④ Agents (kubelet) on a node can communicate with all Pods on that node.

This is a "flat" network — no NAT between Pods. Network plugins (CNI: Calico, Cilium, Flannel) implement this model.

Explanation

The flat networking model is what makes K8s powerful: any Pod can reach any other Pod by IP, without port mapping or NAT. This is implemented by CNI plugins, not by K8s itself. The CNPA exam tests: "Can Pod A on Node 1 reach Pod B on Node 2 directly?" → Yes, via the CNI overlay network. No NAT, no port mapping needed.

Q2
Describe all four Service types: ClusterIP, NodePort, LoadBalancer, ExternalName. When would you use each for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

① ClusterIP (default): Internal IP, reachable only within cluster. Use: Django ↔ PostgreSQL, microservice-to-microservice. anihpj app → anihpj-db.

② NodePort: Opens static port (30000-32767) on EVERY node's IP. Use: Development, quick testing. NOT for production (limited port range, no load balancing across nodes).

③ LoadBalancer: Provisions a cloud load balancer (AWS NLB, GCP LB). Use: External production access to anihpj API. Each Service gets its own LB (costly at scale).

④ ExternalName: Creates a CNAME DNS record pointing to an external FQDN. Use: Connecting to external services (AWS RDS endpoint, external API).

Explanation

Service types are a core CNPA concept. For platform engineering: prefer ClusterIP + Ingress over LoadBalancer for HTTP services (one Ingress can route to many Services, saving cloud LB costs). NodePort is for development only. The exam may ask: "Which Service type exposes a port on every node?" → NodePort.

Q3
What is an Ingress and how is it different from a Service? What is an Ingress Controller?
Show Answer
Answer

Ingress: A K8s resource that defines HTTP/HTTPS routing rules (host-based, path-based) and TLS configuration. It's a DECLARATION of routing intent.

Ingress Controller: The actual proxy (NGINX, Traefik, HAProxy) that IMPLEMENTS those rules. It watches Ingress resources and configures itself accordingly. You must INSTALL an Ingress Controller — K8s doesn't come with one.

Service vs Ingress: Service = Layer 4 (TCP/UDP), single backend set. Ingress = Layer 7 (HTTP/HTTPS), can route to different Services based on host/path. One Ingress → many Services.

Explanation

A critical CNPA distinction: Ingress is the RULES, Ingress Controller is the IMPLEMENTATION. Common exam trap: thinking K8s comes with an Ingress Controller — it DOESN'T. You must install one. This is different from Services, which work out of the box via kube-proxy. Ingress = declarative routing for HTTP.

Q4
What is CoreDNS and how does service discovery work in Kubernetes? How does anihpj-prod.svc.cluster.local resolve?
Show Answer
Answer

CoreDNS is the cluster DNS server (runs as a Deployment in kube-system). It resolves K8s Service names to ClusterIPs. Every Pod has /etc/resolv.conf pointing to CoreDNS.

DNS resolution: anihpj-prod.svc.cluster.local → <service-name>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local. CoreDNS resolves this to the Service's ClusterIP. Within the same namespace, just anihpj-prod works.

Service discovery: Pods discover Services via DNS (CoreDNS) or environment variables (less reliable). When a Pod starts, CoreDNS records are already available.

Explanation

CoreDNS is how all service-to-service communication works in K8s. Pods don't use IP addresses — they use DNS names. The CNPA exam tests: "How does Pod A find Service B?" → DNS via CoreDNS. The FQDN pattern is: <service>.<namespace>.svc.cluster.local. Within the same namespace, just the service name works.

Q5
What is a NetworkPolicy? Write a policy that allows only frontend pods to access anihpj pods on port 8000, and anihpj pods to access PostgreSQL on port 5432.
Show Answer
Answer
# Default deny all ingress to anihpj
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata:
  name: anihpj-policy
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  podSelector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  policyTypes:
    - Ingress
    - Egress
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              role: frontend
      ports:
        - port: 8000
  egress:
    - to:
        - podSelector:
            matchLabels:
              app: postgres
      ports:
        - port: 5432
Explanation

NetworkPolicy = K8s firewall. By default, all traffic is allowed. Once you create ANY NetworkPolicy selecting a Pod, that Pod becomes isolated — only traffic explicitly allowed by policies is permitted. Best practice: start with a default-deny policy, then add allows. This implements zero-trust networking. Requires a CNI that supports NetworkPolicy (Calico, Cilium).

Q6
What is the difference between north-south and east-west traffic in Kubernetes? Which components handle each?
Show Answer
Answer

North-South: Traffic entering/exiting the cluster (external client → cluster). Handled by: Ingress Controller, LoadBalancer Service, API Gateway. Example: User's browser → api.anihpj.com → Ingress → anihpj Service.

East-West: Traffic between services INSIDE the cluster. Handled by: ClusterIP Service, Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd), kube-proxy. Example: anihpj Django app → PostgreSQL database within the cluster.

Key insight: Ingress = north-south (entry point). Service Mesh = east-west (internal communication with mTLS, retries, circuit breaking).

Explanation

This traffic direction concept is fundamental to cloud native networking. The CNPA exam tests: "Which handles external-to-cluster traffic?" → Ingress/API Gateway. "Which handles service-to-service?" → Service Mesh. A complete platform strategy covers BOTH directions: Ingress for external access, Service Mesh for internal reliability + security.

Q7
How does a Service know which Pods to send traffic to? What happens when a Pod is added or removed?
Show Answer
Answer

A Service selects Pods via label selectors. The Service's spec.selector (e.g., app: anihpj) must match Pod labels. K8s maintains an Endpoints object (or EndpointSlice) listing all Pod IPs matching the selector.

When a Pod is added: Its IP is added to Endpoints → kube-proxy updates iptables/IPVS rules → traffic flows to the new Pod.

When a Pod is removed: Its IP is removed from Endpoints → kube-proxy removes the iptables rule → no more traffic to that Pod.

This is continuous and automatic — no manual intervention needed.

Explanation

Label-based service discovery is core to K8s' dynamic nature. Services don't track Pod IPs directly — they use labels. This is why labels are so important: they're the glue between Services and Pods. Check Endpoints with kubectl get endpoints — if empty, the Service selector doesn't match any Pod labels. This is the #1 Service debugging issue.

Q8
What is TLS termination and where should it happen for anihpj — at the Ingress, Service, or Pod level?
Show Answer
Answer

TLS termination = decrypting HTTPS traffic at a specific point in the network path, then forwarding as plain HTTP internally. The question is WHERE to terminate.

For anihpj, terminate at the Ingress: Browser → HTTPS → Ingress Controller (decrypts) → HTTP → Service → HTTP → Pod. The Ingress holds the TLS certificate (via cert-manager). Internal traffic is HTTP — simpler, faster, and the cluster network is trusted.

End-to-end TLS (mTLS): For high-security environments, use a Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd) to encrypt ALL pod-to-pod traffic with mTLS automatically. No application changes needed.

Explanation

TLS strategy is a platform engineering decision. Most platforms terminate TLS at Ingress (external encryption, internal plain). Highly regulated environments may require end-to-end mTLS via Service Mesh. The CNPA exam may ask: "Where is TLS typically terminated?" → At the Ingress/API Gateway. "What provides automatic pod-to-pod encryption?" → Service Mesh (mTLS).

Q9
Explain how cert-manager works with Kubernetes Ingress to automatically provision and renew TLS certificates for api.anihpj.com.
Show Answer
Answer

cert-manager is a K8s add-on that automates TLS certificate lifecycle. Flow: ① You create an Ingress with annotation cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod. ② cert-manager detects the annotation → requests certificate from Let's Encrypt via ACME challenge. ③ Let's Encrypt validates domain ownership (HTTP-01 or DNS-01 challenge). ④ cert-manager stores the issued certificate as a K8s Secret. ⑤ Ingress Controller reads the Secret and serves HTTPS. ⑥ cert-manager auto-renews before expiration (30 days before).

Explanation

cert-manager is a platform essential — manual certificate management doesn't scale. It's part of the CNCF ecosystem (Sandbox project). The CNPA exam tests platform automation concepts: certificate management should be automated, not manual. cert-manager + Let's Encrypt = free, automated TLS for all platform services.

Q10
A developer reports: "I can curl anihpj from within the cluster, but can't access api.anihpj.com from my browser." What are the top 3 causes and how do you fix each?
Show Answer
Answer

① No Ingress Controller: Ingress resource exists but no controller is installed. Fix: kubectl get pods -n ingress-nginx — if nothing, install NGINX Ingress Controller.

② DNS not pointing to Ingress: api.anihpj.com doesn't resolve to the Ingress Controller's external IP. Fix: kubectl get svc -n ingress-nginx → get EXTERNAL-IP → configure DNS A record.

③ Ingress misconfiguration: Host doesn't match, TLS secret missing, or backend Service name wrong. Fix: kubectl describe ingress anihpj -n anihpj-prod → check Rules section, verify backend Service exists.

Explanation

This debugging scenario is daily platform engineering work. Internal access works (Service + Pods fine) but external doesn't → the issue is between Ingress and the outside world. Check: Ingress Controller running? DNS correct? Ingress config correct? The systematic approach: Controller → DNS → Config.

Chapter 7: Helm — The Kubernetes Package Manager

Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes — think apt, yum, or brew, but for K8s applications. It lets you define, install, and upgrade complex applications using Charts — templated, reusable Kubernetes manifests. Every Platform Engineer should master Helm.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours ⛵ Package Mgmt 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Helm's role as the Kubernetes package manager (apt/yum/brew for K8s)
  • ✅ Chart structure: Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/, charts/
  • ✅ Creating and customizing a Helm chart for anihpj
  • ✅ Essential Helm commands: install, upgrade, rollback, history, template
  • ✅ Helm hooks for lifecycle management (Django migrations example)
  • ✅ When to use Helm vs Kustomize — and when to use BOTH
🎓 CNPA Relevance
Domain 1: PE Core Fundamentals 36% Domain 3: CD & PE 16%

7.1 What is Helm and Why Platform Engineers Love It

Helm Architecture
  ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐     ┌─────────────┐
  │ 📁 Chart/   │     │ 📦 Release  │     │ ☸️ Cluster  │
  │ Chart.yaml  │     │ anihpj-v1   │     │ ┌────────┐  │
  │ values.yaml │────▶│ (installed) │────▶│ │Deploy  │  │
  │ templates/  │     │             │     │ │Service │  │
  │   deploy.yaml│    │ Managed by  │     │ │Config  │  │
  │   svc.yaml  │     │ Helm        │     │ │Map     │  │
  └─────────────┘     └─────────────┘     │ └────────┘  │
                                          └─────────────┘

7.2 Helm Chart Structure — Anatomy of a Chart

Helm Chart Directory Tree
  anihpj/
  ├── 📄 Chart.yaml        ← Metadata (name, version)
  ├── 📄 values.yaml       ← Default config values
  ├── 📂 templates/
