7.5 Helm Hooks and Lifecycle Management

Helm hooks execute actions at specific points in a release lifecycle. Common hooks: pre-install, post-install, pre-upgrade, post-upgrade, pre-delete.

💡 Example: Django Migrations Hook

Create a Job with helm.sh/hook: post-install,post-upgrade annotation. This runs python manage.py migrate automatically after every Helm install/upgrade — ensuring your database schema is always in sync.

7.6 Helm vs Kustomize — When to Use Which

Feature⛵ Helm🎨 Kustomize
ApproachTemplating (Go templates)Overlay/Patching
Built into kubectl❌ Needs CLI✅ kubectl kustomize
Reusability✅ Charts distributable⚠️ Overlays local
Learning CurveMediumLow (plain YAML)
Hooks/Lifecycle✅ Pre/post hooks❌ No hooks
Best ForDistributing appsEnvironment variants
💡 Platform Tip

Use BOTH: Helm for packaging and distributing platform components; Kustomize for per-environment customizations on top. They complement each other, not compete.

✅ Chapter 7 Summary — Helm Package Manager

You've mastered Helm, the Kubernetes package manager that transforms raw YAML into reusable, configurable charts. You understand the chart structure (Chart.yaml + values.yaml + templates/), the Go template syntax ({{ .Values.x }}) that separates configuration from infrastructure, and the Helm lifecycle (install → upgrade → rollback → uninstall). You learned when to use Helm (distributing apps to others, complex deploys with dependencies) vs Kustomize (managing environment variants within your org). For the CNPA exam, remember: Helm = package manager, Charts = packages, Repositories = where charts are stored, Releases = installed instances.

⛵ Key Takeaway 1

helm install = deploy. helm upgrade = update. helm rollback = undo. Every Helm operation creates a new Release revision, enabling instant rollbacks.

⛵ Key Takeaway 2

values.yaml separates WHAT (template structure) from HOW (configuration). values-dev.yaml overrides for dev, values-prod.yaml for production — same chart, different configs.

⛵ Key Takeaway 3

Helm vs Kustomize: Helm uses Go templates ({{ .Values.x }}), Kustomize uses YAML overlays. Both are CNCF projects. Both are valid for CNPA Domain 1.

📝 Chapter 7 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is Helm and what problem does it solve for platform engineering?
Show Answer
Answer

Helm is the package manager for Kubernetes. It solves the problem of managing complex, multi-resource K8s applications. Instead of maintaining 10 separate YAML files per environment, you create one Chart with templates and override values per environment.

Problem solved: Raw K8s YAML is repetitive (same Deployment structure, different image tags, different replica counts). Helm templatizes the YAML with Go templates ({{ .Values.replicaCount }}), making it reusable across dev/staging/prod with different values files.

Explanation

Helm is a CNCF Graduated project and essential platform engineering tool. Think: apt/yum/brew for K8s. Charts = packages, Repositories = where charts live, Releases = installed instances. The CNPA exam tests: "What is the K8s package manager?" → Helm. "What is a Chart?" → A packaged K8s application with templates.

Q2
What is the structure of a Helm Chart? List the key files and directories.
Show Answer
Answer
anihpj/
├── Chart.yaml          # Metadata: name, version, description, dependencies
├── values.yaml         # Default configuration values
├── values-dev.yaml     # Dev environment overrides (optional)
├── values-prod.yaml    # Production overrides (optional)
├── charts/             # Sub-charts (dependencies)
├── templates/          # Go-templated K8s manifests
│   ├── deployment.yaml
│   ├── service.yaml
│   ├── ingress.yaml
│   ├── configmap.yaml
│   ├── secret.yaml
│   ├── hpa.yaml
│   ├── _helpers.tpl    # Template helpers (reusable snippets)
│   └── NOTES.txt       # Post-install instructions
└── .helmignore         # Files to exclude from chart package
Explanation

Chart structure is a key CNPA concept. Chart.yaml = metadata, values.yaml = defaults, templates/ = Go-templated YAML, charts/ = dependencies. The _helpers.tpl file is important for reusable template snippets. The exam may ask: "Which file contains default configuration?" → values.yaml.

Q3
How does values.yaml work with Helm templates? Show an example using {{ .Values.replicaCount }}.
Show Answer
Answer

values.yaml provides configuration that gets injected into templates via Go template syntax. The template accesses values with {{ .Values.key }}.

# values-prod.yaml
replicaCount: 3
image:
  repository: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj
  tag: v1.2.3
resources:
  limits:
    cpu: 500m
    memory: 512Mi

# templates/deployment.yaml (excerpt)
spec:
  replicas: {{ .Values.replicaCount }}
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: {{ .Chart.Name }}
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          resources:
            {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }}

helm install anihpj ./anihpj -f values-prod.yaml renders the template with values-prod.yaml, producing replicas: 3.

Explanation

The values → template separation is Helm's core innovation. Same templates, different values per environment. {{ .Values.replicaCount }} is the most common pattern. {{- toYaml .Values.resources | nindent 12 }} is for complex nested objects. The CNPA exam tests: "How does Helm separate configuration from infrastructure definition?" → values.yaml + templates.

Q4
What is the Helm lifecycle: install → upgrade → rollback → uninstall? What happens to K8s resources at each stage?
Show Answer
Answer

① helm install: Creates a new Release (revision 1). Renders templates → applies all K8s resources. Release name + namespace uniquely identify the deployment.

② helm upgrade: Creates a NEW revision (revision 2, 3, ...). Compares new chart/values against previous revision. Updates only changed resources. Keeps release history for rollback.

③ helm rollback: Reverts to a previous revision. helm rollback anihpj 1 goes back to revision 1. All revisions are stored as Secrets (default) or ConfigMaps.

④ helm uninstall: Removes ALL K8s resources created by the release. Deletes release history. Irreversible — use with caution.

Explanation

Helm's revision history is a powerful feature. Every upgrade creates a new revision, and you can rollback to any previous one. By default, Helm stores 10 revisions. This enables safe deployments: if something breaks, helm rollback restores the previous state in seconds. The CNPA exam tests: "How does Helm support rollbacks?" → Release revision history.

Q5
Compare Helm and Kustomize. When should a platform engineer choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

Helm: Uses Go templates ({{ .Values.x }}). Charts are packages distributed via Helm repositories. Best for: distributing applications to others (public charts), complex deploys with dependencies, conditional logic in templates ({{ if .Values.ingress.enabled }}).

Kustomize: Uses YAML overlays/patches (no templates). Built into kubectl (kubectl apply -k). Best for: managing environment variants within your org (dev/staging/prod), simple overlays where you only change a few fields per environment.

Both are CNCF projects. Many platforms use BOTH: Helm for packaging, Kustomize for last-mile environment customizations.

Explanation

Helm vs Kustomize is a classic platform engineering decision. Helm = template-driven, good for distribution. Kustomize = patch-driven, good for variants. The CNPA exam may present a scenario: "A team needs to share their deployment with other orgs. Which tool?" → Helm (charts + repos). "A team needs minor env-specific changes. Which tool?" → Kustomize (overlays).

Q6
What is a Helm Repository? How do you add one and search for charts?
Show Answer
Answer

A Helm Repository is an HTTP server hosting packaged charts (.tgz files) and an index.yaml that lists available charts and versions. Like PyPI for Python or npm for JavaScript.

# Add a repository
helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
helm repo add ingress-nginx https://kubernetes.github.io/ingress-nginx

# Update local repo cache
helm repo update

# Search for charts
helm search repo postgresql
helm search repo ingress

# List added repos
helm repo list
Explanation

Helm repositories are how charts are distributed. Popular repos: Bitnami (databases, infrastructure), Ingress-nginx, Jetstack (cert-manager), Grafana, Prometheus-community. You can also host your own repo (ChartMuseum, Harbor, OCI registries). The CNPA exam tests: "How are Helm charts distributed?" → Repositories.

Q7
What does helm template do and why is it useful for CI/CD pipelines?
Show Answer
Answer

helm template anihpj ./anihpj -f values-prod.yaml renders the chart templates with the specified values and outputs the full K8s YAML to stdout — WITHOUT installing anything on the cluster. It's purely a local rendering operation.

CI/CD use: ① Validate templates before applying — catch syntax errors in CI. ② Pipe output to kubectl apply -f - (Helm-free deployment). ③ Diff against previous render to see what changed. ④ Integrate with GitOps tools that prefer raw YAML over Helm releases. ⑤ Generate documentation of what will be deployed.

Explanation

helm template is a CI/CD essential. It decouples chart rendering from cluster installation. You can lint the output, store it as an artifact, or pipe it to other tools. Some GitOps setups use helm template in CI, commit the rendered YAML to a config repo, and let ArgoCD apply it. This gives you the benefits of Helm templating with GitOps plain-YAML deployment.

Q8
You run helm upgrade anihpj ./anihpj and it fails. What happens to the running application? How do you recover using helm rollback?
Show Answer
Answer

What happens: If helm upgrade fails (template error, resource conflict, timeout), the PREVIOUS revision continues running. Helm does NOT delete or modify existing resources until the new ones are successfully created. This is by design — Helm upgrades are atomic at the resource level.

Recovery: ① helm history anihpj — see all revisions. ② helm rollback anihpj — rollback to the last successful revision (default: previous). ③ helm rollback anihpj 2 — rollback to revision 2 specifically.

Explanation

Helm's failure behavior is a safety feature: failed upgrades don't break running applications. The app keeps running on the last good revision. helm rollback is your emergency undo button. This is why Helm stores revision history — it enables instant recovery. Platform engineers should always verify helm history and know the rollback command.

Q9
What is a Helm hook? Give an example using a pre-install job for running Django database migrations before deploying anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

Helm hooks are K8s resources annotated to run at specific points in the release lifecycle. They run as Jobs and execute before/after install, upgrade, delete, or rollback.

# templates/db-migrate.yaml
apiVersion: batch/v1
kind: Job
metadata:
  name: {{ .Release.Name }}-db-migrate
  annotations:
    "helm.sh/hook": pre-install,pre-upgrade
    "helm.sh/hook-delete-policy": hook-succeeded
    "helm.sh/hook-weight": "5"
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      restartPolicy: Never
      containers:
        - name: migrate
          image: "{{ .Values.image.repository }}:{{ .Values.image.tag }}"
          command: ["python", "manage.py", "migrate"]

This runs python manage.py migrate BEFORE the new Deployment is created. If migration fails, the install/upgrade stops — preventing broken deploys.

Explanation

Helm hooks enable deployment orchestration: run migrations before deploy, run tests after deploy, clean up resources on delete. Hook weights control execution order. Common hooks: pre-install (migrations, secrets), post-install (tests, notifications), pre-delete (cleanup). The CNPA exam tests Helm's extensibility features — hooks show Helm is more than just a template engine.

Q10
A platform team needs to deploy the same anihpj application to dev, staging, and production with different configurations. Design the Helm-based deployment strategy — what files, commands, and CI/CD integration would you use?
Show Answer
Answer

Configuration: One chart (anihpj/) with three values files:
values-dev.yaml — 1 replica, debug=true, small resources
values-staging.yaml — 2 replicas, medium resources, staging DB
values-prod.yaml — 3 replicas, large resources, autoscaling, TLS, monitoring

CI/CD pipeline:
① PR merged to main → CI builds image, pushes to registry
② helm lint ./anihpj -f values-dev.yaml — validate
③ helm upgrade --install anihpj-dev ./anihpj -f values-dev.yaml --namespace anihpj-dev --wait
④ Run integration tests against dev
⑤ helm upgrade --install anihpj-staging ./anihpj -f values-staging.yaml --namespace anihpj-staging --wait
⑥ Manual approval gate
⑦ helm upgrade --install anihpj-prod ./anihpj -f values-prod.yaml --namespace anihpj-prod --wait --timeout 5m

Explanation

This is a real-world platform deployment strategy. Key principles: one chart, multiple values files, environment promotion (dev → staging → prod), automated validation (helm lint), --wait flag (don't return until pods are ready), --timeout (fail fast if pods don't start). The CNPA exam values this structured approach to multi-environment deployment.

🟡 Part 3: CI/CD & GitOps Chapters 8-10

Chapter 8: Continuous Integration (CI) — Building Quality Into Every Commit

CI is the practice of automatically building, testing, and validating every code change. For Platform Engineers, CI is the first quality gate in the delivery pipeline — ensuring that every commit to anihpj is linted, tested, and scanned before it reaches production.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔄 CI/CD 🎯 Domain 3: 16%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What CI is and why it's the foundation of modern software delivery
  • ✅ GitHub Actions: event-driven CI workflows for anihpj
  • ✅ GitLab CI/CD and Jenkins alternatives
  • ✅ CI best practices: fast feedback, caching, parallelization
  • ✅ Hands-on: Set up a complete CI pipeline for anihpj

8.1 What is CI and Why Does It Matter?

Continuous Integration means automatically building, testing, and validating every code change — ideally on every push and every pull request. It's the foundational practice that enables teams to ship software reliably at high velocity.

📖 The Problem CI Solves

"It works on my machine" — the most dreaded phrase in software engineering. CI eliminates this by running your entire test suite in a clean, reproducible environment on every commit. If it passes CI, it works everywhere.

🔄 CI Pipeline Stages for anihpj

📝Git Pushpush to main/PR
→
🔍Lintflake8, black
→
🧪Testpytest, Django
→
🛡️SecuritySAST, Image scan
→
🏗️BuildDocker image
→
⬆️RegistryPush image

⚡ Fail-Fast: The CI Philosophy

anihpj CI Pipeline — Pass/Fail Gates
  Git Push ──▶ ┌──────────┐ ──▶ ┌──────────┐ ──▶ ┌──────────┐
               │ 1️⃣ LINT  │     │ 2️⃣ TEST  │     │3️⃣SECURITY│
               │flake8    │     │pytest    │     │Bandit    │
               │black     │     │Django    │     │Trivy     │
               └────┬─────┘     └────┬─────┘     └────┬─────┘
                    │                │                │
               ❌ Fail = STOP   ❌ Fail = STOP   ❌ Fail = STOP
                    │                │                │
                    └────────────────┴────────────────┘
                                     │
                                     ▼
                            ┌────────────────┐
                            │ 4️⃣ BUILD IMAGE │
                            │ docker build   │
                            └───────┬────────┘
                                    │
                                    ▼
                            ┌────────────────┐
                            │ 5️⃣ PUSH TO     │
                            │ REGISTRY       │
                            │ ghcr.io/anihpj │
                            └────────────────┘

  💡 KEY PRINCIPLE: Each stage is a gate. If linting fails, tests don't run.
     If tests fail, the image isn't built. This saves compute time and gives
     developers immediate, actionable feedback.

📊 Before vs After CI

AspectWithout CIWith CI
IntegrationManual, painful "merge days"Automatic, every commit
Bug DiscoveryDays/weeks after writing codeMinutes after pushing
Deploy Confidence"Fingers crossed""CI passed, we're good"
Code QualityInconsistent styleEnforced by linters
SecurityScanned before release (if at all)Scanned on every commit
Time to MarketSlow, unpredictableFast, predictable
🟡 CNPA Exam Relevance

Domain 3: Continuous Delivery & Platform Engineering (16%). You must understand how CI fits into the broader platform engineering lifecycle — it's the first quality gate in your Internal Developer Platform (IDP).

8.2 GitHub Actions — CI for the GitHub Ecosystem

GitHub Actions is event-driven automation built into every GitHub repository. When you push code, open a PR, create an issue, or trigger a schedule — GitHub Actions can respond with automated workflows. It's the most popular CI system for open-source and is deeply integrated with the GitHub ecosystem.

🏗️ Architecture: Event → Workflow → Job → Step

GitHub Actions Hierarchy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 📌 EVENT (Trigger)                                       │
  │ push to main, pull_request, schedule, workflow_dispatch  │
  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🔄 WORKFLOW                                              │
  │ .github/workflows/ci.yml — the automation definition      │
  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 📦 JOBS (run in parallel by default)                      │
  │                                                          │
  │  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐      │
  │  │ Job: Lint   │  │ Job: Test   │  │Job: Security│      │
  │  │             │  │             │  │             │      │
  │  │ STEP 1:     │  │ STEP 1:     │  │ STEP 1:     │      │
  │  │ actions/    │  │ actions/    │  │ actions/    │      │
  │  │ checkout@v4 │  │ checkout@v4 │  │ checkout@v4 │      │
  │  │ STEP 2:     │  │ STEP 2:     │  │ STEP 2:     │      │
  │  │ setup-      │  │ setup-      │  │ Run Bandit  │      │
  │  │ python@v5   │  │ python@v5   │  │ STEP 3:     │      │
  │  │ STEP 3:     │  │ STEP 3:     │  │ Run Trivy   │      │
  │  │ Run flake8  │  │ Run pytest  │  │             │      │
  │  │ STEP 4:     │  │             │  │             │      │
  │  │ Run black   │  │             │  │             │      │
  │  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                         │
                         ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 📦 Job: build-and-push                                   │
  │ needs: [lint, test, security]  ← Only runs if ALL pass!  │
  │ STEP 1: docker/login-action@v3                           │
  │ STEP 2: docker/build-push-action@v5                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📝 Complete CI Workflow for anihpj

.github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI — anihpj
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

jobs:
  lint:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: '3.11' }
      - run: pip install flake8 black
      - run: flake8 anihpj/ jobpost/ --max-line-length=100
      - run: black --check anihpj/ jobpost/

  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    services:
      postgres:
        image: postgres:15
        env: { POSTGRES_PASSWORD: testpass }
        options: >-
          --health-cmd pg_isready --health-interval 10s
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: '3.11' }
      - run: pip install -r requirements.txt
      - run: pytest anihpj/ --cov --cov-report=xml

  security:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pip install bandit && bandit -r anihpj/
      - uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
        with:
          scan-type: 'fs'
          scan-ref: '.'
          severity: 'CRITICAL,HIGH'

  build-and-push:
    needs: [lint, test, security]   # Wait for ALL to pass!
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          registry: ghcr.io
          username: ${{ github.actor }}
          password: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with:
          push: true
          tags: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.sha }}

🔑 Key Patterns in This Workflow

PatternWhat It DoesWhy It Matters
needs: [lint, test, security]Sequential dependency — build only runs if all three passSaves compute; avoids building broken code
Service containersSpin up PostgreSQL for testsTests run against real DB, not SQLite
GITHUB_TOKENAuto-generated auth tokenNo secret setup needed for GHCR push
Git SHA tagginganihpj:${{ github.sha }}Every image traceable to exact commit
Marketplace actionsactions/checkout@v4, etc.Reuse community-built, maintained actions
💡 Matrix Builds

Test against multiple Python versions simultaneously by adding a strategy.matrix to your job. This ensures anihpj works on Python 3.9, 3.10, 3.11, and 3.12 — all in parallel!

8.3 GitLab CI/CD — Self-Hosted CI Alternative

GitLab CI/CD is tightly integrated into GitLab's platform. Unlike GitHub Actions (SaaS-first), GitLab excels in self-hosted environments where you control the entire pipeline infrastructure — from Git repos to runners to container registries.

📝 .gitlab-ci.yml Structure

.gitlab-ci.yml
stages:
  - lint
  - test
  - security
  - build

variables:
  IMAGE_TAG: $CI_REGISTRY_IMAGE:$CI_COMMIT_SHORT_SHA

lint:flake8:
  stage: lint
  image: python:3.11
  script:
    - pip install flake8 black
    - flake8 anihpj/ jobpost/ --max-line-length=100
    - black --check anihpj/ jobpost/

test:pytest:
  stage: test
  image: python:3.11
  services:
    - postgres:15
  variables:
    POSTGRES_PASSWORD: testpass
  script:
    - pip install -r requirements.txt
    - pytest anihpj/ --cov --cov-report=xml

security:bandit:
  stage: security
  image: python:3.11
  script:
    - pip install bandit
    - bandit -r anihpj/

build:docker:
  stage: build
  image: docker:latest
  services:
    - docker:dind
  script:
    - docker login -u $CI_REGISTRY_USER -p $CI_REGISTRY_PASSWORD $CI_REGISTRY
    - docker build -t $IMAGE_TAG .
    - docker push $IMAGE_TAG

🔄 GitLab vs GitHub Actions — Key Differences

FeatureGitLab CI/CDGitHub Actions
Configuration.gitlab-ci.yml.github/workflows/*.yml
ExecutionGitLab Runners (self-hosted or SaaS)GitHub-hosted or self-hosted runners
Container RegistryBuilt-in per project (free)GHCR (GitHub Container Registry)
Auto DevOps✅ Pre-built CI/CD templates❌ No equivalent
Self-Hosted🥇 Excellent (mature self-hosted)🟡 Good (Actions Runner Controller on K8s)
MarketplaceSmaller ecosystem🥇 Massive ecosystem
📖 Platform Engineer's Choice

As a platform engineer, GitLab's Auto DevOps is powerful — it provides pre-built CI/CD templates that automatically detect your language (Python/Django for anihpj) and configure build, test, and deploy stages without writing YAML from scratch.

8.4 Jenkins — The Grandfather of CI

Jenkins is the original CI/CD automation server (created 2011, forked from Hudson). While newer tools like GitHub Actions have emerged, Jenkins remains widely used in enterprises due to its extreme flexibility, massive plugin ecosystem (1,800+ plugins), and ability to self-host everything.

📝 Jenkinsfile — Pipeline as Code

Jenkinsfile (Declarative)
pipeline {
    agent { kubernetes { yaml '''
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
spec:
  containers:
  - name: python
    image: python:3.11
    command: ['sleep', 'infinity']
  - name: docker
    image: docker:dind
    securityContext: { privileged: true }
''' } }

    stages {
        stage('Lint') {
            steps {
                container('python') {
                    sh 'pip install flake8 black'
                    sh 'flake8 anihpj/ jobpost/ --max-line-length=100'
                    sh 'black --check anihpj/ jobpost/'
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                container('python') {
                    sh 'pip install -r requirements.txt'
                    sh 'pytest anihpj/ --cov --cov-report=xml'
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Security Scan') {
            steps {
                container('python') {
                    sh 'pip install bandit && bandit -r anihpj/'
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Build & Push') {
            steps {
                container('docker') {
                    sh 'docker build -t anihpj:${BUILD_NUMBER} .'
                    sh 'docker push anihpj:${BUILD_NUMBER}'
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        success { echo '✅ CI Pipeline PASSED!' }
        failure { echo '❌ CI Pipeline FAILED!' }
    }
}

☸️ Jenkins on Kubernetes — Dynamic Agent Provisioning

The Kubernetes plugin is Jenkins' killer feature for platform engineers. Instead of maintaining static build servers, Jenkins dynamically creates a new Pod for each build — with exactly the tools needed — and deletes it when done. This means:

  • 🧹 Clean environment every build — no "works on my machine" drift
  • 📈 Auto-scaling — handle 100 concurrent builds without idle servers
  • 🔧 Per-pipeline pods — Python 3.11 for anihpj, Node 20 for frontend
  • 💸 Cost efficient — pods only exist during builds
📜
Declarative Pipeline

Simpler, structured syntax. Easier to read and write. Supports blue ocean visual editor. Recommended for most teams.

⚡
Scripted Pipeline

Full Groovy power — loops, conditionals, shared libraries. More flexible but more complex. For advanced use cases like dynamic parallel stages.

⚠️ Jenkins Complexity Warning

Jenkins' strength (flexibility) is also its weakness. It requires more maintenance than GitHub Actions or GitLab CI. For new platforms, consider whether you truly need Jenkins' power before accepting its complexity.

8.5 CI Best Practices for Platform Engineers

As a platform engineer, you're not just writing CI pipelines — you're designing the CI experience for all your development teams. These best practices ensure your CI platform is fast, reliable, and developer-friendly.

✅ CI Best Practices Checklist
  1. Fast feedback — Keep CI under 10 minutes. Developers lose focus after 10 min. Use parallel jobs and smart test selection.
  2. Deterministic builds — Same input → same output, always. Pin all dependency versions. Use lockfiles (poetry.lock, Pipfile.lock).
  3. Caching — Cache pip dependencies, Docker layers, and build artifacts. Don't download the internet on every run.
  4. Parallelization — Run lint, test, and security scans simultaneously. Use matrix builds for multi-version testing.
  5. Flaky test management — Quarantine flaky tests; don't ignore them. They erode trust in CI. Track flakiness metrics.
  6. Branch protection — Require CI to pass before merge. Block direct pushes to main. Enforce via GitHub/GitLab settings.
  7. Test isolation — Each test should be independent. No shared state. Use service containers for databases.
  8. Security scanning — SAST (Bandit), dependency scanning, container scanning (Trivy) on every PR.
  9. Artifact management — Store test reports, coverage data, and build logs. Don't lose evidence.
  10. Pipeline as code — CI config lives in the repo. Version controlled. Reviewable. Auditable.

📊 CI Maturity Model

LevelCharacteristicsTime to Feedback
🟤 L0: NoneManual builds on developer machineDays/Weeks
🔴 L1: NightlyScheduled build overnight~12 hours
🟠 L2: On PushCI triggers on every push10-30 minutes
🟡 L3: Fast CICI completes in under 10 min< 10 minutes
🟢 L4: Pre-mergeCI runs on PRs, blocks merge if failing< 5 minutes
🔵 L5: InstantLocal pre-push hooks + CI in under 2 min< 2 minutes
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

The exam expects you to know how to design a CI pipeline — not just use one. You should be able to: choose the right CI tool for a given scenario, explain why parallelization matters, describe caching strategies, and identify CI anti-patterns (e.g., CI that takes 45+ minutes).

8.6 Hands-On: Set Up CI for anihpj with GitHub Actions

In this hands-on lab, you'll create a complete CI pipeline for anihpj from scratch. Follow each step and understand WHY each piece exists.

1
Create Workflow Directory

mkdir -p .github/workflows/ in your anihpj repo root

2
Create ci.yml

Create .github/workflows/ci.yml with the trigger configuration

3
Add Lint Job

flake8 for code quality + black for formatting consistency

4
Add Test Job

pytest with PostgreSQL service container — real DB testing!

5
Add Security Job

Bandit (Python SAST) + Trivy (filesystem and container scanning)

6
Add Build & Push

Docker build → push to GitHub Container Registry (GHCR)

7
Push & Watch

git push origin main → GitHub Actions dashboard → see it run!

8
Fix & Iterate

Read failure logs, fix issues, commit, push again. CI tells you what's wrong.

.github/workflows/ci.yml — Complete Working File
name: CI — anihpj
on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

jobs:
  lint:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: '3.11' }
      - run: pip install flake8 black
      - run: flake8 anihpj/ jobpost/ --max-line-length=100
      - run: black --check anihpj/ jobpost/

  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    services:
      postgres:
        image: postgres:15
        env: { POSTGRES_PASSWORD: testpass }
        options: >-
          --health-cmd pg_isready --health-interval 10s
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-python@v5
        with: { python-version: '3.11' }
      - run: pip install -r requirements.txt
      - run: pytest anihpj/ --cov --cov-report=xml

  security:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - run: pip install bandit && bandit -r anihpj/
      - uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
        with:
          scan-type: 'fs'
          scan-ref: '.'
          severity: 'CRITICAL,HIGH'

  build-and-push:
    needs: [lint, test, security]
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          registry: ghcr.io
          username: ${{ github.actor }}
          password: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v5
        with:
          push: true
          tags: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.sha }}
📖 What You'll See in GitHub Actions UI

After pushing: Go to your GitHub repo → Actions tab → You'll see all 4 jobs running (lint, test, security in parallel; build-and-push waiting). Green ✅ means pass, Red ❌ means fix. Click any job to see step-by-step logs!

🟡 Platform Engineering Context

This CI pipeline is the first quality gate in your Internal Developer Platform (IDP). When you integrate it with Backstage or Port, developers see a green/red CI badge right on the service catalog — without ever leaving their developer portal.

📋 Chapter 8 Summary — Continuous Integration

🔑
Key Concept

CI = automatically build, test, and validate every commit. Fail-fast means if linting fails, tests don't run — saving time and compute.

🛠️
Tools Covered

GitHub Actions (event-driven CI), GitLab CI/CD (self-hosted), Jenkins (enterprise-grade with K8s plugin).

✅
Best Practices

Keep CI under 10 min, use caching, run jobs in parallel, block merges if CI fails, never ignore flaky tests.

🎯 What CNPA Expects You to Know

You should be able to: (1) Design a CI pipeline for a given application stack, (2) Choose between CI tools based on organizational needs, (3) Explain CI best practices (caching, parallelization, fail-fast), (4) Identify CI anti-patterns (slow CI, no branch protection, ignoring flaky tests).

📝 Chapter 8 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is Continuous Integration (CI) and what are its three core practices? How would CI benefit the anihpj project?
Show Answer
Answer

CI is the practice of automatically building, testing, and validating every code change. Three core practices: (1) All developers merge to a shared main branch multiple times daily, (2) Every commit triggers automated build + test, (3) Failures are fixed immediately (within minutes, not days). For anihpj: every PR runs lint (flake8), unit tests (pytest), security scan (Bandit), and a Docker build — all in parallel, completing in under 10 minutes.

Explanation

CI is a Domain 3 fundamental. Key exam points: CI ≠ CD — CI is about integrating and testing code; CD is about deploying it. The fail-fast principle means faster failures = faster fixes. The CNPA exam tests the CI/CD distinction: "Which practice automatically builds and tests every commit?" → CI.

Q2
Compare GitHub Actions, GitLab CI, and Jenkins. When would a platform engineer choose each?
Show Answer
Answer

GitHub Actions: Best when code is on GitHub. Event-driven (push, PR, schedule). 2,000+ pre-built actions. Free for public repos. Easiest setup. GitLab CI: Best for self-hosted GitLab. Single YAML file (.gitlab-ci.yml). Built-in container registry. Strong for enterprise with compliance needs. Jenkins: Best for complex, legacy pipelines. Massive plugin ecosystem (1,800+). Requires dedicated infrastructure. Most flexible but highest maintenance burden.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests CI tool selection based on scenario. Key differentiators: GitHub Actions = GitHub-native, easiest. GitLab CI = self-hosted, enterprise. Jenkins = most flexible, highest ops cost. The exam may ask: "A team uses GitHub and wants the simplest CI setup." → GitHub Actions.

Q3
What is the fail-fast principle in CI? Design a pipeline that demonstrates this for the anihpj Django app.
Show Answer
Answer

Fail-fast means ordering CI jobs so the fastest, most-likely-to-fail checks run FIRST. If linting fails, don't waste time running tests or building images. Pipeline order for anihpj: (1) Lint (flake8) — 30s, catches syntax/style. If fails → stop. (2) Security scan (Bandit) — 45s. (3) Unit tests (pytest) — 2min. (4) Build (Docker) — 3min. (5) Integration tests — 5min. Total: ~10min if all pass, ~30s if lint fails.

Explanation

Fail-fast is a CI best practice that saves compute time and developer waiting time. The CNPA exam may ask: "Why run linting before tests?" → Fail-fast — catch trivial errors early rather than after 10 minutes of test execution. This is especially important at scale where 100+ developers are pushing code daily.

Q4
What are CI anti-patterns? List 5 things a platform engineer should NEVER do in CI.
Show Answer
Answer

① Slow CI (>10 min): Developers context-switch and lose flow. Parallelize, cache, optimize. ② No branch protection: Allowing merges without passing CI defeats the purpose. Require status checks before merge. ③ Ignoring flaky tests: Flaky tests erode trust in CI. Fix or quarantine them. ④ Hardcoded secrets in pipeline: Use GitHub Secrets, Vault, or external secret managers. ⑤ Manual CI triggers only: CI should run automatically on every push and PR — not require a manual button click.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests CI best practices and anti-patterns. Key rule: CI should be AUTOMATIC (triggered by Git events), FAST (under 10 min), and RELIABLE (no flaky tests). If developers skip CI because it's slow or unreliable, you've failed as a platform engineer. The platform's CI pipeline IS the first quality gate.

Q5
Explain parallelization and caching in CI pipelines. How would you parallelize the anihpj pipeline?
Show Answer
Answer

Parallelization: Run independent jobs simultaneously. anihpj example: lint, security-scan, and unit-tests run in parallel (all independent). Only build-and-push waits for all three to pass. Cuts total time from 8min sequential to ~3min parallel.

Caching: Store dependencies between runs. Cache ~/.cache/pip for Python packages, Docker layers via registry cache or BuildKit cache mounts. First run: 5min (download deps). Subsequent runs: 30s (cache hit).

Explanation

Pipeline optimization is a platform engineering concern. Key insight: identify which jobs have NO dependencies on each other → run them in parallel. Identify which files change rarely → cache them. The CNPA exam values this efficiency mindset: the faster the pipeline, the faster developers get feedback.

Q6
A developer pushes code and CI fails with a linting error. What happens next in a properly configured CI pipeline?
Show Answer
Answer

① CI marks the commit as FAILED (red ❌ on GitHub). ② The developer receives a notification (GitHub email/Slack). ③ No downstream jobs run — tests, build, and push are skipped (fail-fast). ④ The PR is blocked from merging (branch protection rules require CI to pass). ⑤ The developer fixes the linting error, pushes a fix, CI re-runs — this time passing. ⑥ Only then can the PR be merged.

Explanation

This is the CI feedback loop — the heart of CI. The CNPA exam tests understanding of the full flow: code → CI → feedback → fix → CI passes → merge. The key principle: no code reaches production without passing CI. Branch protection rules in GitHub/GitLab enforce this automatically.

Q7
What should a CI pipeline include for a Python/Django application like anihpj? List all quality gates.
Show Answer
Answer

① Linting: flake8/ruff for code style, mypy for type checking. ② Security scan: Bandit (code), safety/pip-audit (dependencies) for known CVEs. ③ Unit tests: pytest with coverage (minimum 80%). ④ Docker build: Build image, verify it starts. ⑤ Image scan: Trivy scan for OS and package vulnerabilities. ⑥ Integration tests: Spin up Docker Compose (app + DB), run API tests. ⑦ Migration check: python manage.py makemigrations --check (no missing migrations).

Explanation

A production CI pipeline has multiple quality gates — each catches different issues. The CNPA exam tests pipeline design: "Which tools would you include in a Python CI pipeline?" The order matters: fast checks first (lint), then unit tests, then slow checks (build, integration). Security scanning should be in EVERY pipeline — it's a Domain 2 requirement.

Q8
What is the difference between CI and CD? Where does CI end and CD begin?
Show Answer
Answer

CI ends when the code is merged to main AND a validated artifact (container image) is produced and pushed to the registry. CD begins when that artifact is deployed to an environment (staging, then production). The hand-off point: CI produces an image → CD deploys it.

CI = Build + Test + Integrate. CD = Deploy + Verify + Release. CI answers "Does this code work?" CD answers "Is this code in production safely?"

Explanation

The CI/CD boundary is a key CNPA concept. The artifact (container image) is the hand-off point. CI produces it, CD consumes it. The exam may ask: "Which phase produces the deployable artifact?" → CI. "Which phase deploys it to production?" → CD. The registry (GHCR, ECR, Docker Hub) is the bridge between CI and CD.

Q9
A team's CI pipeline takes 25 minutes. Developers are bypassing CI by pushing directly to main. As a platform engineer, how do you fix this?
Show Answer
Answer

① Diagnose bottlenecks: Profile the pipeline — which jobs take longest? (Often: Docker build without caching, integration tests on slow infra.) ② Optimize: Add layer caching, use BuildKit, run tests in parallel, use faster runners. Target: under 10 min. ③ Enforce branch protection: Block direct pushes to main (only via PR). Require CI to pass before merge. ④ Communicate: Show developers the optimized CI time. Developers bypass CI because it's slow — make it fast and they'll WANT to use it.

Explanation

This is a real platform engineering scenario. The root cause is NOT "lazy developers" — it's a slow pipeline. Fix the platform, don't blame the users. This is "Platform as a Product" thinking: if your customers (developers) are bypassing your product (CI), your product has a problem. The CNPA exam values this user-centric approach.

Q10
Design the complete CI pipeline architecture for anihpj covering: event triggers, job ordering, quality gates, artifact management, and notification strategy.
Show Answer
Answer

Trigger: Push to any branch or PR opened → GitHub Actions workflow starts. Jobs (parallel): lint (flake8, 30s) + security (Bandit+safety, 45s) + unit-tests (pytest, 2min). Sequential after parallel success: build-image (Docker multi-stage, 3min) → scan-image (Trivy, 1min) → push-image (ghcr.io, 30s). Branch-specific: On main branch merge → tag as :latest and :v1.x.x. On PR → tag as :pr-123. Notifications: Slack (#ci-cd channel) on failure. GitHub status check on PR. Artifact: Container image in GHCR with SBOM attestation.

Explanation

This end-to-end CI design tests your platform engineering thinking. The CNPA exam values holistic pipeline design: triggers → parallel jobs → sequential gates → artifact → notification. Every decision should be justified: parallel for speed, sequential for dependencies (build must finish before scan), branch-specific tags for traceability.

Chapter 9: Continuous Delivery (CD) — Deploying Safely to Production

CD extends CI by automatically deploying validated changes to staging and production. This chapter covers deployment strategies, GitOps with ArgoCD, and progressive delivery — ensuring every anihpj release reaches users safely.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🚀 GitOps 🎯 Domain 3: 16%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ CD vs Continuous Deployment — the approval gate difference
  • ✅ 4 deployment strategies: Rolling Update, Blue-Green, Canary, A/B Testing
  • ✅ ArgoCD — GitOps continuous delivery for Kubernetes
  • ✅ Progressive delivery with Argo Rollouts
  • ✅ Hands-on: Set up GitOps with ArgoCD for anihpj

9.1 What is Continuous Delivery vs Continuous Deployment?

These terms are often confused, but they have distinct meanings. Understanding the difference is critical for the CNPA exam and for designing your platform's delivery strategy.

📦
Continuous Delivery

Every change is deployable — but deployment is manual (approval gate). The anihpj team reviews and approves each production deployment. "We COULD deploy anytime; we CHOOSE when."

🚀
Continuous Deployment

Every change that passes CI is automatically deployed to production. No human approval — the pipeline decides. "If CI passes, it ships." Requires extremely strong test coverage and monitoring.

🔗 The Relationship: CI → CDelivery → CDeployment

CI/CD Spectrum — Where Does anihpj Fit?
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐      ┌───────────────┐      ┌──────────────────┐      │
  │  │    CI    │ ───▶ │ Continuous    │ ───▶ │ Continuous       │      │
  │  │ Build,   │      │ Delivery      │      │ Deployment       │      │
  │  │ Test,    │      │ (Manual Deploy│      │ (Auto Deploy     │      │
  │  │ Scan     │      │  Approval)    │      │  to Production)  │      │
  │  └──────────┘      └───────────────┘      └──────────────────┘      │
  │       ✅                  ✅                       ❓                 │
  │  Every commit         Every build is           Every build is        │
  │  is validated         deployable               deployed              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  anihpj STRATEGY:                                                    │
  │  • Staging: Continuous Deployment (auto-deploy on merge to main)     │
  │  • Production: Continuous Delivery (approval gate before deploy)     │
  │  • Feature Branches: Deploy to ephemeral preview environments        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
AspectContinuous DeliveryContinuous Deployment
Deployment TriggerManual (human or automated approval)Automatic (on CI pass)
Human InvolvementYes — approval gateNo — fully automated
Test RequirementsStrong test suiteExceptional test suite + monitoring
RollbackRarely needed (caught before deploy)Must be instantaneous
Best ForRegulated industries, critical appsSaaS, fast-moving startups
anihpj Use✅ Production deployments✅ Staging deployments

9.2 Deployment Strategies — The Complete Guide

Choosing the right deployment strategy is one of the most impactful decisions a platform engineer makes. Each strategy balances risk, speed, complexity, and cost differently.

a) Rolling Update (Kubernetes Default)

Gradually replaces old pods with new ones — zero downtime, but no environment isolation during deployment. Controlled by maxSurge (how many extra pods to create) and maxUnavailable (how many pods can be down).

Rolling Update Visualization
  Before:   [P1-v1] [P2-v1] [P3-v1]  ← All running v1
  Step 1:   [P1-v1] [P2-v1] [P3-v1] [P4-v2]  ← maxSurge=1, new v2 pod created
  Step 2:   [P1-v1] [P2-v1]         [P4-v2] [P5-v2]  ← One v1 removed, one v2 added
  Step 3:   [P1-v1]                 [P4-v2] [P5-v2] [P6-v2]  ← Continue...
  Final:                        [P4-v2] [P5-v2] [P6-v2]  ← All v2, zero downtime!

b) Blue-Green Deployment

Two identical environments: Blue (current/production) and Green (new version). Switch traffic all at once via Service selector. Instant rollback — just switch the Service back to Blue.

Blue-Green Deployment Pattern
  ┌─────────────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────────────┐
  │  🔵 BLUE (v1 — LIVE)    │     │  🟢 GREEN (v2 — IDLE)   │
  │  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐     │     │  ┌───┐ ┌───┐ ┌───┐     │
  │  │ P │ │ P │ │ P │     │     │  │ P │ │ P │ │ P │     │
  │  │v1 │ │v1 │ │v1 │     │     │  │v2 │ │v2 │ │v2 │     │
  │  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘     │     │  └───┘ └───┘ └───┘     │
  └───────────┬─────────────┘     └───────────┬─────────────┘
              │                               │
              └───────────┬───────────────────┘
                          │
                    ┌─────┴─────┐
                    │  Service  │  ← selector: version=v1
                    │  anihpj   │     (switch to v2 when ready)
                    └───────────┘

  ✅ Rollback: Just change Service selector back to version=v1 — instant!

c) Canary Deployment

Route a small percentage of traffic to the new version, monitor metrics, and gradually increase the percentage. Example: 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%. Requires traffic splitting via Ingress controller or Service Mesh (Istio/Linkerd).

Canary Deployment — Gradual Traffic Shift
  Time 0:   [v1: 100%] [v2:   0%]  ████████████████████████░░░░░░░░
  Time 5m:  [v1:  95%] [v2:   5%]  ███████████████████████░░░░░░░░░
  Time 10m: [v1:  75%] [v2:  25%]  ██████████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░
  Time 20m: [v1:  50%] [v2:  50%]  ████████████░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░
  Time 30m: [v1:   0%] [v2: 100%]  ░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░░██████████

  At each step: Monitor error rate, latency, CPU. If metrics > threshold → ROLLBACK!

d) A/B Testing

Route specific users to different versions based on headers, cookies, or user IDs. Unlike canary (infrastructure-focused), A/B testing is feature-focused — you're testing which version users prefer, not just whether it runs.

📊 Complete Strategy Comparison

StrategyDowntimeRollbackComplexityBest For
Rolling UpdateZeroSlow (revert)LowStandard web apps, APIs
Blue-GreenZeroInstant (switch)MediumCritical apps, pre-release tests
CanaryZeroFast (reduce %)HighHigh-traffic, risk-averse orgs
A/B TestingZeroFastHighFeature experimentation
RecreateYES (downtime)SlowLowDev/test only, DB migrations

🌳 Which Strategy Should You Choose?

Deployment Strategy Decision Tree
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Can you tolerate ANY downtime?       │
  └──────────┬─────────────┬────────────┘
             │ YES         │ NO
             ▼             ▼
  ┌──────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────┐
  │ Need instant     │  │ Is this for dev/ │
  │ rollback?        │  │ testing only?    │
  └───┬─────────┬────┘  └───┬─────────┬────┘
      │ YES     │ NO        │ YES     │ NO
      ▼         ▼           ▼         ▼
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │ Blue-  │ │Do you  │ │Recreate│ │ Rolling  │
  │ Green  │ │need    │ │(Accept │ │ Update   │
  │Deploy  │ │gradual │ │downtime│ │(Default) │
  └────────┘ │rollout?│ └────────┘ └──────────┘
              └───┬─┬──┘
                  │ │
             YES  │ │ NO
              ┌───┘ └───┐
              ▼         ▼
          ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐
          │Canary  │ │A/B Test  │
          │Deploy  │ │Deploy    │
          │5%→100% │ │(Features)│
          └────────┘ └──────────┘

  💡 anihpj RECOMMENDATION:
  Staging → Blue-Green (instant rollback while testing)
  Production → Canary (5%→50%→100% over 30 minutes)
  Feature flags → A/B Testing for UI experiments

9.3 ArgoCD — GitOps Continuous Delivery

GitOps is a paradigm where Git is the single source of truth for your infrastructure and application configuration. ArgoCD is the leading GitOps tool for Kubernetes — it continuously monitors your Git repo and ensures your cluster matches the desired state.

🏗️ ArgoCD Architecture

ArgoCD consists of three main components:

  • API Server — Serves the Web UI, CLI, and API; handles auth (SSO, RBAC)
  • Repository Server — Clones Git repos, generates Kubernetes manifests (Kustomize, Helm, plain YAML)
  • Application Controller — The core reconciliation loop: compares desired state (Git) with live state (cluster), syncs if different
ArgoCD GitOps Reconciliation Loop
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                     │
  │  ┌─────────────┐                                                    │
  │  │ 👨‍💻 Developer │  ① Git Push: Change replicas 3→5                  │
  │  │ Changes     │─────▶  ┌──────────┐                               │
  │  │ replicas:5  │       │ 📁 Git    │                               │
  │  └─────────────┘       │ Config    │                               │
  │                        │ Repo      │                               │
  │                        └─────┬─────┘                               │
  │                              │  ② Every 3 min (default):            │
  │                              │     ArgoCD polls Git for changes     │
  │                              ▼                                      │
  │                        ┌──────────┐                                │
  │                        │ 🔄 ArgoCD│                                │
  │                        │ ┌──────┐ │                                │
  │                        │ │ Diff │ │  Git=5 pods  ← Desired         │
  │                        │ │Engine│ │  Live=3 pods ← Actual          │
  │                        │ └──┬───┘ │  → OUT OF SYNC!                │
  │                        │    │     │                                │
  │                        │    │ ③ Sync: Apply manifests to cluster   │
  │                        └────┼─────┘                                │
  │                             │                                       │
  │                             ▼                                       │
  │                        ┌──────────┐                                │
  │                        │ ☸️ K8s   │                                │
  │                        │ Cluster  │                                │
  │                        │ ┌─┐┌─┐┌─┐│  ← Was 3 pods                  │
  │                        │ │P││P││P││                                │
  │                        │ └─┘└─┘└─┘│                                │
  │                        │ ┌─┐┌─┐   │  ← ArgoCD adds 2 pods          │
  │                        │ │P││P│   │                                │
  │                        │ └─┘└─┘   │  Now = 5 pods ✓                │
  │                        └──────────┘                                │
  │                                                                     │
  │  💡 GOLDEN RULE: Git is ALWAYS right. If K8s ≠ Git, Git wins.      │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔄 Auto-Sync vs Manual Sync

ModeBehaviorUse Case
Manual SyncArgoCD detects drift but waits for human to click "Sync"Production with change control
Auto-SyncArgoCD automatically syncs when Git changesStaging/dev environments
Auto-Sync + PruneAuto-deploy AND auto-delete resources removed from GitFull GitOps automation
Auto-Sync + Self-HealAuto-correct manual kubectl changes back to Git statePrevent configuration drift

📦 ApplicationSets — Manage Apps at Scale

For platform engineers managing dozens of services, ApplicationSets let you template ArgoCD Applications. Instead of writing 100 Application YAMLs, you write one template that generates them from a list of services, git repos, or directories.

📖 ArgoCD for anihpj

Your anihpj platform would have an ArgoCD Application pointing to anihpj's K8s manifests repo. When you want to scale from 3 to 5 replicas, you change the number in Git — ArgoCD handles the rest. No kubectl needed!

9.4 Progressive Delivery with Argo Rollouts

Argo Rollouts is a Kubernetes controller and CRD that replaces the standard Deployment resource. It gives you Blue-Green and Canary deployment strategies natively in Kubernetes, plus automatic analysis and rollback.

📝 Argo Rollout for anihpj — Canary Deployment

anihpj-rollout.yaml
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Rollout
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 5
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
        - setWeight: 5      # 5% traffic to new version
        - pause: {duration: 5m}  # Wait 5 minutes, monitor
        - setWeight: 25     # 25% traffic
        - pause: {duration: 10m}
        - setWeight: 50     # 50% traffic
        - pause: {duration: 10m}
        - setWeight: 100    # 100% — fully promoted!
      analysis:
        templates:
          - templateName: error-rate-check
          - templateName: latency-check
        startingStep: 1     # Start analysis after step 1
  template:
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: anihpj
          image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v2
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8000

🔬 Analysis Templates — Automated Validation

Analysis templates query metrics (Prometheus, Datadog, New Relic) during the rollout. If error rate exceeds threshold or latency spikes — automatic rollback!

analysis-template.yaml
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: AnalysisTemplate
metadata:
  name: error-rate-check
spec:
  metrics:
    - name: error-rate
      interval: 30s
      successCondition: result[0] <= 0.01  # Error rate must be ≤ 1%
      failureLimit: 3        # 3 failures → auto rollback
      provider:
        prometheus:
          address: http://prometheus.monitoring:9090
          query: |
            rate(http_requests_total{app="anihpj",status=~"5.."}[1m]) /
            rate(http_requests_total{app="anihpj"}[1m])
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know the difference: Deployment (K8s built-in, Rolling Update only) vs Argo Rollout (CRD, Blue-Green + Canary + Analysis). The CNPA exam tests your ability to choose the right tool for the right deployment scenario.

9.5 Hands-On: Set Up GitOps with ArgoCD for anihpj

This hands-on lab walks you through setting up a complete GitOps workflow with ArgoCD for the anihpj project.

1
Install ArgoCD

kubectl create namespace argocd
kubectl apply -n argocd -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/argoproj/argo-cd/stable/manifests/install.yaml

2
Access ArgoCD UI

kubectl port-forward svc/argocd-server -n argocd 8080:443
Get admin password: kubectl -n argocd get secret argocd-initial-admin-secret -o jsonpath="{.data.password}" | base64 -d

3
Create Git Repo for Manifests

Create a repo (e.g., anihpj-k8s-manifests) and push Deployment, Service, ConfigMap YAMLs for anihpj

4
Create ArgoCD Application

Deploy the ArgoCD Application CRD pointing to your Git repo and cluster

5
Configure Auto-Sync

Enable automated sync with pruning so Git always matches the cluster

6
Test GitOps Flow

Change replicas from 3 to 5 in Git → wait 3 min → ArgoCD syncs → 5 pods running!

7
Test Self-Healing

Simulate drift: kubectl scale deployment anihpj --replicas=2 → ArgoCD auto-corrects back to 5

📝 ArgoCD Application YAML

anihpj-argocd-app.yaml
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  project: default
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/anihpj/anihpj-k8s-manifests
    targetRevision: main
    path: overlays/production
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: anihpj
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true        # Delete resources removed from Git
      selfHeal: true     # Auto-correct manual kubectl changes
    syncOptions:
      - CreateNamespace=true
📖 GitOps in Practice

After this setup: Git is your control plane. No more kubectl for deployments. Every change is version-controlled, reviewable via PR, and auditable. This is the essence of platform engineering — making operations safe, repeatable, and scalable.

📋 Chapter 9 Summary — Continuous Delivery & GitOps

🔑
Key Concept

CD = every change is deployable. Continuous Deployment = auto-deploy to prod. GitOps = Git is the single source of truth for infra AND app config.

🔄
Deployment Strategies

Rolling Update (default), Blue-Green (instant rollback), Canary (gradual % shift), A/B Testing (user-focused). Choose based on risk tolerance.

🛠️
Tools Covered

ArgoCD (GitOps reconciliation), Argo Rollouts (Blue-Green + Canary + Analysis), ApplicationSets (manage apps at scale).

🎯 What CNPA Expects You to Know

You should be able to: (1) Explain the difference between Continuous Delivery and Continuous Deployment, (2) Choose the right deployment strategy for a given scenario, (3) Describe GitOps principles and ArgoCD architecture, (4) Configure a canary deployment with Argo Rollouts and analysis templates.

📝 Chapter 9 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is the difference between Continuous Delivery and Continuous Deployment? Which one requires manual approval?
Show Answer
Answer

Continuous Delivery: Every change is automatically built, tested, and made READY for production. But the final deploy to production requires MANUAL approval (a human clicks "deploy"). Continuous Deployment: Every change that passes CI/CD is automatically deployed to production with NO manual approval. This requires extremely high confidence in automated tests and monitoring.

Explanation

This is a classic CNPA question. The key distinction: Delivery = manual deploy button exists. Deployment = fully automated. Most platforms use Continuous Delivery with a manual approval gate for production. Continuous Deployment requires mature testing, canary analysis, and auto-rollback — which Argo Rollouts provides.

Q2
Compare Rolling Update, Blue-Green, Canary, and A/B Testing deployment strategies. When would you use each for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

Rolling Update: Replace pods one-by-one. Default K8s strategy. No downtime. Rollback by reversing the update. Best for: stateless apps, standard deploys.

Blue-Green: Run old (blue) and new (green) side-by-side. Switch 100% traffic to green instantly. Instant rollback (switch back to blue). Requires 2x resources. Best for: critical apps needing instant rollback.

Canary: Gradually shift traffic (5% → 25% → 50% → 100%) while monitoring metrics. Auto-rollback if metrics degrade. Best for: high-risk changes, progressive delivery.

A/B Testing: Route based on user attributes (headers, region, device). Tests user behavior against variant. Best for: UX experiments, feature comparison.

Explanation

Deployment strategy selection is a core CNPA skill. Key exam rule: Rolling Update = standard, Blue-Green = instant rollback (cost: 2x resources), Canary = gradual + automated analysis, A/B = user-focused experimentation. The exam may ask: "Which strategy allows gradual traffic shift with automated rollback?" → Canary.

Q3
What is GitOps? Explain the Pull-based vs Push-based models and which ArgoCD uses.
Show Answer
Answer

GitOps uses Git as the single source of truth for declarative infrastructure and applications. Two models: Push-based: CI pipeline pushes changes to the cluster (kubectl apply or helm upgrade from CI). Pull-based: An agent INSIDE the cluster (ArgoCD, Flux) continuously watches Git and pulls changes automatically. ArgoCD uses Pull-based GitOps — it watches the Git repo and reconciles the cluster to match.

Explanation

Pull-based GitOps is the CNCF-recommended model. Advantages: (1) No CI needs cluster credentials (security win), (2) Git becomes the audit log, (3) Self-healing — if someone manually changes the cluster, ArgoCD reverts it. The CNPA exam tests: "ArgoCD uses which GitOps model?" → Pull-based. "Where is the source of truth in GitOps?" → Git repository.

Q4
Explain the ArgoCD architecture and how it reconciles cluster state with Git.
Show Answer
Answer

ArgoCD runs inside the cluster with these components: API Server (gRPC/REST API + Web UI), Repository Server (clones Git repos, renders manifests), Application Controller (the reconciliation loop — continuously compares Git state vs cluster state, applies diffs). The flow: Git push → Repository Server detects change → Application Controller compares desired (Git) vs actual (cluster) → syncs to converge. Default polling: every 3 minutes. Webhooks available for instant sync.

Explanation

ArgoCD's architecture mirrors K8s itself — a reconciliation loop watching Git instead of etcd. Key exam facts: (1) ArgoCD uses a Pull model, (2) The Application Controller is the reconciliation engine, (3) selfHeal: true automatically reverts manual cluster changes, (4) prune: true deletes resources removed from Git.

Q5
What is Progressive Delivery? How does Argo Rollouts implement it for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

Progressive Delivery gradually exposes a new version to users while continuously measuring its health via metrics. If metrics degrade, it automatically rolls back — before all users are affected. Argo Rollouts implements this via: (1) A Rollout CRD (superset of Deployment), (2) Canary steps (setWeight: 20% → pause → 50% → pause → 100%), (3) AnalysisTemplate that queries Prometheus for error rate/latency, (4) Auto-promotion if healthy, auto-rollback if unhealthy.

Explanation

Progressive Delivery is the evolution beyond basic CD. Key CNPA concept: traditional CD deploys and hopes; Progressive Delivery deploys, VERIFIES, and automatically rolls back on failure. This closes the feedback loop that manual approval gates previously provided. Argo Rollouts + Prometheus analysis = automated verification.

Q6
What is the difference between an ArgoCD Application and an ApplicationSet? When would you use each?
Show Answer
Answer

Application: Deploys ONE app from ONE Git repo to ONE destination cluster/namespace. Manual creation. Good for: single applications.

ApplicationSet: Generates MULTIPLE Applications from a template. Uses generators: List (static list of clusters), Cluster (auto-discover all ArgoCD-managed clusters), Git (directories in a repo), Matrix (combine generators). Good for: deploying the same app to dev/staging/prod, or to multiple clusters.

Explanation

ApplicationSets solve the "many clusters, many apps" problem. Instead of creating 30 individual Applications (dev+staging+prod × 10 services), you create one ApplicationSet template. This is essential for platform engineering — it enables centralized management of multi-environment, multi-cluster deployments. The CNPA exam tests scalability patterns.

Q7
A manual kubectl scale deployment anihpj --replicas=5 was run directly on the cluster. ArgoCD shows the app as "OutOfSync." What happens next with selfHeal: true?
Show Answer
Answer

With selfHeal: true, ArgoCD's Application Controller detects the drift (actual=5 replicas, desired=3 replicas from Git). It automatically reverts the change — scaling the deployment back to 3 replicas. The sync status returns to "Synced." This happens on ArgoCD's reconciliation interval (default: every 3 minutes). Without selfHeal, the app stays "OutOfSync" until manually synced.

Explanation

Self-healing is a key GitOps feature. It enforces the principle that Git is the single source of truth — ANY manual change, even well-intentioned ones, are reverted. This prevents configuration drift. The CNPA exam tests: "What prevents manual cluster changes from persisting in GitOps?" → selfHeal in ArgoCD (or the reconciliation loop in Flux).

Q8
Compare ArgoCD and Flux CD. What are the key differences?
Show Answer
Answer

ArgoCD: Rich Web UI, CLI, and API. Application CRD with sync policies. Multi-cluster management built-in. ApplicationSets for template-based deployments. Better for: teams wanting a UI-driven experience, multi-cluster deployments.

Flux CD: Helm-controller native (HelmRelease CRD). Image automation (auto-update image tags). Kustomize-controller built-in. GitRepository and OCIRepository sources. Better for: Helm-heavy workflows, teams preferring CLI-only, automated image updates.

Both are CNCF Graduated projects. Both implement Pull-based GitOps. Choose based on UX preference and Helm/Kustomize needs.

Explanation

ArgoCD vs Flux is a platform engineering decision. The CNPA exam may ask: "Which GitOps tool has a Web UI?" → ArgoCD. "Which has native HelmRelease support?" → Flux. Both are valid, both are CNCF graduated, both implement the same GitOps principles. Many organizations use BOTH for different use cases.

Q9
What is a canary analysis and what metrics should it check before promoting a new version of anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

Canary analysis continuously queries metrics during the canary rollout to detect problems. For anihpj, the AnalysisTemplate checks: (1) Error Rate: rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[2m]) — must stay below 1%. (2) P99 Latency: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[2m])) — must be less than 500ms. (3) Pod Restarts: rate(kube_pod_container_status_restarts_total[2m]) — must be 0. (4) CPU/Memory: Resource usage within limits. If ANY metric exceeds threshold → auto-rollback.

Explanation

Canary analysis is what makes Progressive Delivery work. The key insight: don't check just ONE metric (a new version might have low errors but high latency). Check Rate, Errors, Duration (RED method) + resource usage. The CNPA exam tests: "What metrics should a canary analysis check?" → Error rate, latency, resource usage, and crash rate.

Q10
Design the full GitOps workflow for anihpj: from developer pushing code to production deployment. Include all tools and their roles.
Show Answer
Answer

① Developer pushes code → GitHub Actions (CI) builds, tests, scans, pushes image to GHCR. ② CI updates the config repo (anihpj-config) with the new image tag and commits. ③ ArgoCD watches the config repo → detects the change. ④ Argo Rollouts (via Rollout CRD) executes canary strategy: 20% traffic to new pods → Prometheus analysis checks error rate/latency for 2 minutes → 50% → analyze → 100%. ⑤ If analysis passes → full promotion. If analysis fails → auto-rollback to previous version. ⑥ Throughout: Grafana dashboards show canary vs stable metrics, Loki shows logs, OTel traces show request flow. ⑦ Alertmanager sends notifications on rollback or deployment success.

Explanation

This end-to-end workflow integrates all CD concepts: CI → GitOps → Progressive Delivery → Observability → Alerting. The CNPA exam values this holistic view: each component has a specific role, and together they form a complete delivery pipeline. The key insight: Git is the control plane, and every change flows through it.

Chapter 10: Container Image Pipelines — Build, Scan, Sign, Deploy

Container images are the artifacts that flow through your platform. This chapter covers the complete image lifecycle — from optimized builds to vulnerability scanning, signing, and registry best practices.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1 hour 🐳 Images 🎯 Domain 1: 36%

10.1 The Complete Container Image Lifecycle

Container images are the artifacts that flow through your platform. Every commit that passes CI produces a container image. As a platform engineer, you're responsible for ensuring that every image that reaches production is secure, optimized, and traceable.

🔄 The Image Lifecycle — 7 Stages, 7 Quality Gates

Complete Container Image Pipeline for anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐             │
  │  │ 1️⃣ WRITE │──▶│ 2️⃣ BUILD │──▶│ 3️⃣ SCAN  │──▶│ 4️⃣ SIGN  │             │
  │  │Dockerfile│   │docker bld│   │  Trivy   │   │ Cosign   │             │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘             │
  │       │              │              │              │                     │
  │    Git push      BuildKit      Block on      Keyless                     │
  │    triggers      multi-arch    CRITICAL      signing                     │
  │    CI pipeline   caching       vulns         via OIDC                    │
  │                                     │                                   │
  │                                     ▼                                    │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐                            │
  │  │ 7️⃣ MONITOR│◀──│ 6️⃣ DEPLOY│◀──│ 5️⃣ PUSH  │                            │
  │  │ Runtime  │   │ArgoCD/   │   │ GHCR /   │                            │
  │  │ Security │   │Flux      │   │ DockerHub│                            │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘                            │
  │                                                                          │
  │  💡 Each arrow is a QUALITY GATE. If any stage fails, the image never    │
  │     reaches production. 7 stages = 7 opportunities to catch problems.    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🛡️ Platform Engineer's Role in the Image Pipeline

🏗️
Build

Provide optimized base images, BuildKit configuration, multi-stage build templates

🔍

✍️
Sign
