↓ The platform team builds the pipeline; developers just push code ↓

10.2 Docker Build Optimization for Platform Engineers

The default Docker build works — but as a platform engineer, you can dramatically improve build speed, image size, and security. Here are the techniques that matter:

📦 Multi-Stage Builds — Separate Build from Runtime

Dockerfile (Optimized Multi-Stage)
# ──── STAGE 1: BUILD ────
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends \
    gcc libpq-dev && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# ──── STAGE 2: RUNTIME (Distroless-like) ────
FROM python:3.11-slim
WORKDIR /app
# Copy ONLY the installed packages from builder (not gcc, not dev libs)
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
# Copy application code LAST (changes most frequently)
COPY . .
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
USER 1000:1000
EXPOSE 8000
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000"]

🪜 Layer Caching — Order Matters!

Docker Layer Caching Strategy
  ❌ BAD (Cache busted every code change):
  COPY . .                    ← Changes every commit → cache MISS
  RUN pip install -r req.txt  ← Runs EVERY time → SLOW

  ✅ GOOD (Dependencies cached):
  COPY requirements.txt .     ← Only changes when deps change
  RUN pip install -r req.txt  ← Cached 95% of the time → FAST!
  COPY . .                    ← Only this layer is rebuilt

  ⚡ BUILD TIME: Bad ~5 min → Good ~30 sec (10x faster!)

📊 Image Size Optimization — Results

TechniqueImage SizeReduction
python:3.11 (full)~920 MB—
python:3.11-slim~150 MB-84%
+ Multi-stage build~120 MB-87%
+ Remove cache (--no-cache-dir)~100 MB-89%
Distroless (gcr.io/distroless/python3)~55 MB-94% !
💡 BuildKit & Multi-Arch

Use docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64 for multi-architecture images. Run anihpj on cheaper ARM (Graviton) instances — saving 20-40% on compute costs. BuildKit also enables --mount=type=cache for pip cache and --mount=type=secret for passing credentials without baking them into the image.

10.3 Image Scanning — Shift Left Security

Shift Left means moving security checks earlier in the development lifecycle — catching vulnerabilities before they reach production, not after. Image scanning should happen on every commit, every PR, not just before release.

🔍 Scanner Comparison

ToolSpeedAccuracyBest ForIntegration
Trivy (Aqua)⚡ Very Fast⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐CI pipelines, comprehensive scanning (OS + lang pkgs + IaC)GitHub Actions, CLI, K8s operator
grype (Anchore)⚡ Very Fast⭐⭐⭐⭐Lightweight CI integration, quick local scansGitHub Actions, CLI
Snyk🟡 Medium⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐Developer-focused with auto-fix PRsGitHub/GitLab/Bitbucket native
Docker Scout🟡 Medium⭐⭐⭐⭐Built-in Docker Desktop, SBOM generationDocker Hub, Docker Desktop

🛡️ Vulnerability Severity Classification

SeverityActionExample
🔴 CRITICALBLOCK deployment immediatelyRCE in Django, Log4Shell
🟠 HIGHBlock deployment, fix within 24hSQL injection, auth bypass
🟡 MEDIUMCreate ticket, fix within sprintDOS potential, info disclosure
🔵 LOWLog and track, fix when possibleTheoretical attack vectors

📝 CI Integration — Trivy in GitHub Actions

CI Trivy Scanning Step
# Scan filesystem for vulnerabilities BEFORE building the image
- name: Trivy FS Scan
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
  with:
    scan-type: 'fs'
    scan-ref: '.'
    severity: 'CRITICAL,HIGH'
    exit-code: 1          # FAIL the build on findings
    format: 'sarif'
    output: 'trivy-results.sarif'

# Scan the BUILT image
- name: Trivy Image Scan
  uses: aquasecurity/trivy-action@master
  with:
    image-ref: 'ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.sha }}'
    severity: 'CRITICAL,HIGH'
    exit-code: 1
⚠️ Critical Policy

Block deployments on CRITICAL and HIGH vulnerabilities. Your CI pipeline should FAIL if Trivy finds any critical or high CVEs. This is non-negotiable for production images. Configure exit-code: 1 and severity: 'CRITICAL,HIGH'.

10.4 Image Signing and Supply Chain Security

How do you know the image running in production is the exact same image that passed your CI pipeline — and not one swapped by an attacker? Image signing answers this question cryptographically.

🔐 Cosign — Sign and Verify Container Images

Cosign (part of the Sigstore project) signs container images and stores the signature in the same registry. It supports keyless signing using OIDC (OpenID Connect) — your CI provider's identity (e.g., GitHub Actions) becomes the signer. No keys to manage, rotate, or lose!

Cosign in CI Pipeline
# Step 1: Install Cosign
- uses: sigstore/cosign-installer@v3

# Step 2: Sign the image (keyless, via OIDC)
- name: Sign Image
  run: |
    cosign sign \
      --yes \
      ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj@${{ steps.build.outputs.digest }}
  env:
    COSIGN_EXPERIMENTAL: "true"  # Enable keyless signing

# Step 3: Verify BEFORE deployment (in CD pipeline)
- name: Verify Signature
  run: |
    cosign verify \
      --certificate-identity https://github.com/anihpj/anihpj/.github/workflows/ci.yml@refs/heads/main \
      --certificate-oidc-issuer https://token.actions.githubusercontent.com \
      ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.sha }}

🏭 SLSA Framework — Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts

SLSA (pronounced "salsa") is a security framework from Google/OpenSSF that defines increasing levels of supply chain integrity:

LevelNameRequirements
🔴 L0NoneNo guarantees
🟠 L1ProvenanceBuild is scripted; provenance shows how artifact was built
🟡 L2Hosted BuildBuild runs on hosted platform (GitHub Actions); signed provenance
🟢 L3Hardened BuildIsolated, ephemeral, hermetic builds; non-falsifiable provenance

📋 SBOM — Software Bill of Materials

An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of every component inside your image — like a nutrition label for software. Generated with Syft or Trivy. Critical for vulnerability management: when Log4Shell hits, you can instantly query SBOMs to find affected images.

🔗 End-to-End Supply Chain Security

Supply Chain Security Pipeline — Code to Production
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SUPPLY CHAIN SECURITY — Every Link Must Be Verified                     │
  │                                                                         │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐          │
  │  │ 1️⃣ CODE  │───▶│ 2️⃣ BUILD │───▶│ 3️⃣ SIGN  │───▶│ 4️⃣ VERIFY│          │
  │  │ Git Repo │    │ CI/CD    │    │ Cosign   │    │ Kyverno  │          │
  │  │          │    │ Pipeline │    │ Keyless  │    │ or OPA   │          │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘          │
  │       │               │               │               │                 │
  │   Code Review     Build              Sign          Verify               │
  │   + SAST          Provenance         Image         Signature            │
  │   (Bandit)        (SLSA L2+)         Digest        at Deploy            │
  │       │               │               │               │                 │
  │       ▼               ▼               ▼               ▼                 │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                     🔒 TRUSTED ARTIFACT                          │  │
  │  │  ✓ Code reviewed      ✓ Build provenance recorded                │  │
  │  │  ✓ Passed all tests   ✓ Signed by CI identity (OIDC)            │  │
  │  │  ✓ No CRITICAL vulns  ✓ SBOM generated & attached               │  │
  │  │  ✓ Signature verified ✓ Deployed via GitOps (ArgoCD)            │  │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                         │
  │  💡 PLATFORM GOAL: Developers just push code. The platform handles     │
  │     everything else — building securely, signing, scanning, and        │
  │     deploying with verified artifacts only.                             │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 Platform Engineer's Role

You configure the platform to: (1) Auto-sign every image in CI with Cosign, (2) Auto-generate SBOMs for every image, (3) Enforce signature verification at deploy time with Kyverno or OPA. Developers don't need to know about signing — it just works.

10.5 Container Registry Best Practices

Your container registry is the distribution hub for your platform. Treat it as critical infrastructure — because if your registry is down, your platform can't deploy anything.

🏷️ Tagging Strategy — Never Use :latest in Production!

StrategyExampleProsCons
Git SHAanihpj:abc1234Immutable, traceable to commitHard to know which is latest
Semantic Versionanihpj:v1.2.3Human-readable, follows semverTags can be overwritten (use with SHA)
Hybrid (BEST)anihpj:v1.2.3-prod-abc1234Readable + immutable + traceableSlightly verbose
Environmentanihpj:staging, anihpj:prodEasy for CD pipelinesMutable (not recommended alone)
:latestanihpj:latestNone❌ NEVER in production!

📝 CI Job — Generate Smart Tags

CI Tag Generation
- name: Generate Image Tags
  id: tags
  run: |
    echo "sha_tag=ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.sha }}" >> $GITHUB_OUTPUT
    echo "version_tag=ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v$(cat VERSION)-${{ github.sha }}" >> $GITHUB_OUTPUT
    echo "env_tag=ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:${{ github.ref_name }}" >> $GITHUB_OUTPUT

✅ Registry Best Practices Checklist

✅ Registry Best Practices
  1. Immutable tags — Never overwrite; use unique version tags. Once pushed, a tag should be permanent.
  2. Hybrid tag strategy — anihpj:v1.2.3-prod-abc1234 (semver + env + git SHA)
  3. Retention policies — Auto-delete images older than 90 days or keep only last N versions
  4. Vulnerability scanning — Scan at registry level continuously, not just during CI
  5. NEVER use :latest in production manifests! It's mutable, unpredictable, and dangerous.
  6. Proxy cache — Cache Docker Hub images locally to reduce external pulls and avoid rate limits
  7. Access control — Use least privilege: devs can push, CD system can pull, nobody else
  8. Geo-replication — Replicate registries across regions for disaster recovery and faster pulls
🟡 Platform Engineering Context

As a platform engineer, you'll configure the registry as part of your IDP's infrastructure. Key decisions: public vs private registry, retention policies, geo-replication, and integration with your CI/CD and security scanning pipelines. The registry is the bridge between CI (build) and CD (deploy).

Multi-stage builds separate build deps from runtime (920MB→55MB). Layer caching (COPY requirements.txt BEFORE code). BuildKit for multi-arch and cache mounts.

🔐
Security

📝 Chapter 10 — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What are the 7 stages of the container image lifecycle? Describe each stage and its purpose for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

① Write: Create Dockerfile + application code. ② Build: docker build — produces an image. Multi-stage for optimization. ③ Scan: Trivy scans for vulnerabilities (OS + language packages). Block on CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs. ④ Sign: Cosign signs the image with a digital signature (keyless via OIDC). ⑤ Push: Upload to registry (GHCR, ECR). ⑥ Deploy: K8s pulls the image and runs containers. ⑦ Monitor: Watch for runtime vulnerabilities (Falco), new CVEs in deployed images.

Explanation

The 7-stage lifecycle is your platform's image supply chain. Each stage is a quality/security gate. The CNPA exam tests: "At which stage should vulnerability scanning occur?" → Scan (stage 3, before pushing to registry). "At which stage does signing occur?" → Sign (stage 4, after scanning, before pushing).

Q2
What is Trivy and how should it be integrated into the anihpj CI pipeline? What severity level should block a deploy?
Show Answer
Answer

Trivy is a comprehensive vulnerability scanner for container images, file systems, and Git repositories. In CI: trivy image --severity CRITICAL,HIGH ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0 — if CRITICAL or HIGH CVEs are found, the pipeline FAILS and the image is NOT pushed. Blocking rule: CRITICAL = always block. HIGH = block by default (with an exception process). MEDIUM/LOW = warn but don't block. Unknown = investigate. Trivy scans: OS packages (apt/rpm), language packages (pip, npm, gems), and misconfigurations.

Explanation

Trivy is a CNCF project and the industry standard for container scanning. The CNPA exam tests: "Which tool scans container images for vulnerabilities?" → Trivy. Key policy decision: what severity blocks the pipeline? CRITICAL always, HIGH usually. This is a platform-wide policy — enforced in CI, not left to individual teams.

Q3
What is image signing with Cosign? How does it prevent supply chain attacks on the anihpj platform?
Show Answer
Answer

Cosign signs container images with a digital signature, proving the image came from a trusted source and hasn't been tampered with. Flow: CI builds + scans image → cosign sign --key cosign.key ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0 → signature stored alongside the image. At deploy time, Kyverno (or native Cosign verification) checks: is this image signed with the platform's trusted key? If not → BLOCK deployment.

Keyless signing: Cosign can also use OIDC (GitHub Actions token) — no key management needed.

Explanation

Image signing is a critical supply chain security control. The CNPA exam tests Domain 2 (Security): "How do you ensure only trusted images run in your cluster?" → Image signing + admission verification. Cosign is the CNCF standard. Keyless signing (OIDC) is the modern approach — no private keys to manage.

Q4
What is an SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) and why does every platform need them? How does Syft generate one?
Show Answer
Answer

An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of all components, libraries, and dependencies in a software artifact. Like a nutrition label for software. Why: (1) When a CVE is announced (e.g., Log4Shell), you can instantly query "which of our images use this vulnerable library?" (2) Compliance (US Executive Order 14028 mandates SBOMs for federal software). Syft: syft ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0 -o spdx-json → generates an SPDX-format SBOM listing every OS package, Python package, and binary in the image.

Explanation

SBOMs are a growing CNPA topic (Domain 2). The exam tests supply chain security practices: "What document lists all software components in a container image?" → SBOM. Syft (from Anchore) is the leading tool. SBOMs should be generated in CI, attached to the image, and stored for auditing. Formats: SPDX, CycloneDX.

Q5
What is the SLSA framework? What does SLSA Level 2 require for the anihpj build pipeline?
Show Answer
Answer

SLSA (Supply chain Levels for Software Artifacts, pronounced "salsa") is a security framework that defines 4 levels of supply chain integrity. Level 2 requirements: (1) Version-controlled build config (Dockerfile in Git), (2) Build service (GitHub Actions with ephemeral runners), (3) Build-as-code (pipeline defined in YAML, auditable), (4) Provenance — a signed attestation of how, when, and where the image was built. Generated with: cosign attest --predicate slsa-provenance.json ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0.

Explanation

SLSA is the industry standard for supply chain integrity. The CNPA exam may test: "What framework measures supply chain security maturity?" → SLSA. Level 1 = basic (version controlled). Level 2 = provenance (signed build attestation). Level 3 = hardened build platform. Level 4 = hermetic, reproducible builds. Most platforms target Level 2-3.

Q6
What is the best image tagging strategy for production? Compare :latest, :v1.2.3, and :sha-abc1234.
Show Answer
Answer

❌ :latest — NEVER use in production. It's a moving target — what "latest" means changes every build. Impossible to know which version is running. Debugging nightmare.

✅ :v1.2.3 (Semantic version) — The minimum. Human-readable, traceable to a specific release. Use for all production deployments.

✅ :sha-abc1234 (Git SHA) — The gold standard. Immutable, traceable to exact commit. Combined with semver tag on the same image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 AND :sha-abc1234 point to the same digest.

Explanation

Tagging strategy is a platform policy decision. The CNPA exam tests: "Why avoid :latest in production?" → Not reproducible, unknown version, debugging impossible. Best practice: tag with BOTH semver (human-readable) AND git SHA (immutable, traceable). Kyverno policies should BLOCK :latest tags at admission.

Q7
What is BuildKit and how does it improve Docker builds for the anihpj platform?
Show Answer
Answer

BuildKit is Docker's next-generation build engine with significant improvements: (1) Parallel builds: Build independent stages simultaneously. (2) Cache mounts: Mount persistent cache directories (--mount=type=cache,target=/root/.cache/pip) — pip cache persists between builds, even in CI ephemeral runners. (3) Secrets: Mount secrets during build without them ending up in image layers. (4) Multi-arch: Build for amd64 AND arm64 simultaneously. (5) Better layer caching: Only rebuild what changed — smarter than legacy Docker builder.

Explanation

BuildKit is Docker's default builder since Docker 23.x. The CNPA exam tests build optimization: "What enables parallel Docker builds?" → BuildKit. "How do you cache pip downloads across CI builds?" → BuildKit cache mounts. Key command: DOCKER_BUILDKIT=1 docker build or set in daemon config.

Q8
What is a container registry and what features should a production registry for anihpj have?
Show Answer
Answer

A production registry should have: (1) Access control: RBAC — who can push/pull which repos. (2) Image scanning: Built-in vulnerability scanning (GHCR/ECR have this, Harbor is the CNCF self-hosted option). (3) Retention policies: Auto-delete old/untagged images to control costs. (4) Geo-replication: Images available in multiple regions for faster pulls. (5) Immutability: Tags cannot be overwritten (prevents :v1.2.3 from changing). (6) Signature storage: Store Cosign signatures alongside images. (7) SBOM attachment: Store SBOMs with images.

Explanation

The registry is platform infrastructure. The CNPA exam tests: "Which CNCF project provides a self-hosted container registry?" → Harbor. Public cloud: GHCR (GitHub), ECR (AWS), GAR (GCP), ACR (Azure). Key policy: enable immutability — once a tag is pushed, it can NEVER change. This prevents supply chain attacks where an attacker overwrites a known-good tag.

Q9
A new CVE (CVE-2024-1234) is announced affecting Python 3.11. How do you identify which anihpj services are vulnerable?
Show Answer
Answer

① Query SBOMs: Search all stored SBOMs for the affected package/version. ② Trivy scan all deployed images: trivy image --severity CRITICAL,HIGH ghcr.io/anihpj/* — filters for the new CVE. ③ Kubectl check running images: kubectl get pods -A -o jsonpath="{.items[*].spec.containers[*].image}" — list all images in the cluster. ④ Cross-reference: which images use the vulnerable version? ⑤ Remediate: rebuild images with patched Python, re-deploy via GitOps. ⑥ Update Trivy DB: trivy image --download-db-only (CVE databases update continuously).

Explanation

Vulnerability response is a critical platform engineering capability. The CNPA exam tests supply chain security operations: "How do you respond to a new CVE?" → SBOM query → scan → identify → patch → redeploy. Platforms with SBOMs answer this in minutes. Platforms without SBOMs spend days manually checking every service.

Q10
Design the complete image pipeline for anihpj covering: build optimization, security scanning, signing, SBOM generation, tagging strategy, and registry configuration.
Show Answer
Answer

Build (GitHub Actions): BuildKit with cache mounts for pip. Multi-stage: builder compiles wheels, production copies only runtime. Scan: Trivy scans for CRITICAL/HIGH CVEs → fail pipeline if found. SBOM: Syft generates SPDX SBOM, attached to image. Sign: Cosign keyless signing via GitHub OIDC. Tag: Three tags on same image: :v1.3.0 (semver), :sha-abc1234 (git SHA), :latest (only for dev). Push: To GHCR with immutability enabled. Verify at deploy: Kyverno verifies Cosign signature at admission. Monitor: Falco watches for runtime anomalies. Trivy rescans images daily for new CVEs.

Explanation

This is the complete secure image supply chain. The CNPA exam values this end-to-end thinking: every stage is a security gate, every artifact is signed and traceable, and every vulnerability is catchable at multiple points (CI, registry, admission, runtime). This is Domain 2 (Security) in practice.

🟡 Part 4: Internal Developer Platforms (IDPs) — Building the Paved Road Chapters 11-13

Chapter 11: Internal Developer Platforms — Concepts and Design

An IDP is the product you build as a platform engineer. It's the self-service layer between developers and infrastructure — reducing cognitive load, enforcing best practices, and making delivery predictable. This chapter teaches you how to think about, design, and build your IDP.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🏗️ IDP 🎯 Domain 5: 8%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What an IDP is and its layered architecture
  • ✅ Platform as a Product mindset — treating developers as customers
  • ✅ How to start with an MVP platform and evolve iteratively
  • ✅ Golden Path templates — the "paved road" for developers
  • ✅ Platform APIs as the interface layer

11.1 What Exactly is an Internal Developer Platform (IDP)?

An Internal Developer Platform is a self-service layer on top of infrastructure that enables developers to build, deploy, and operate their applications — without needing to be infrastructure experts. It's the sum of tools, APIs, templates, and processes that make developers productive.

📖 IDP ≠ Just a Portal

Backstage is a developer portal — it's the UI layer. The IDP is the entire system: the templates that scaffold services, the CI/CD pipelines that build and deploy, the GitOps controllers that sync infrastructure, the monitoring that alerts. Backstage is the window; the IDP is the house.

🏗️ The IDP — Layered Architecture

IDP Layers — Developer Experience to Infrastructure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  👨‍💻 DEVELOPER EXPERIENCE LAYER (What devs interact with)              │
  │  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐  ┌───────────┐                       │
  │  │ Developer │  │   CLI     │  │    API    │                       │
  │  │  Portal   │  │ (anictl)  │  │ Endpoints │                       │
  │  │(Backstage)│  │           │  │           │                       │
  │  └───────────┘  └───────────┘  └───────────┘                       │
  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │
  ┌──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🔧 PLATFORM ENGINEERING LAYER (What you build)                       │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐  │
  │  │Templates │ │  CI/CD   │ │  GitOps  │ │ Secrets  │ │Monitor.  │  │
  │  │(Scaffold)│ │Pipelines │ │ (ArgoCD) │ │ (Vault)  │ │(Grafana) │  │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────┘
                             │
  ┌──────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ☸️ INFRASTRUCTURE LAYER (The raw resources)                          │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐               │
  │  │Kubernetes│ │ Database │ │  Cloud   │ │ Network  │               │
  │  │ Cluster  │ │  (RDS)   │ │  (AWS)   │ │  (VPC)   │               │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 KEY INSIGHT: Developers only touch the TOP layer. The platform team
     owns the middle layer and abstracts the bottom layer.

🔑 Key IDP Capabilities

CapabilityWhat It ProvidesTool Example
Self-Service Provisioning"Click to create a new service" — automated scaffoldingBackstage Scaffolder
CI/CD PipelinesBuild, test, deploy — pre-configured per languageGitHub Actions, Argo Workflows
ObservabilityLogs, metrics, traces — accessible without SSHGrafana, Loki, Tempo
Secrets ManagementSecure storage and injection of credentialsHashiCorp Vault, External Secrets
Environment ManagementCreate/destroy dev, staging, prod environmentsTerraform, Crossplane
Service CatalogRegistry of all services, owners, and metadataBackstage Catalog

11.2 Platform as a Product — The Mindset Shift

This is the most important mindset shift in platform engineering: your platform is not an IT project — it's a product. Your "customers" are your internal developers. If they don't adopt it, your platform has failed — no matter how technically impressive it is.

🏗️
Platform as a PROJECT (❌)

Build it once, hand it over, move on. No feedback loop. No iteration. Developers are forced to use it. This is how platforms fail.

📦
Platform as a PRODUCT (✅)

Continuous discovery, iterative delivery, measured by adoption. Developers CHOOSE to use it because it's better than the alternative. This is how platforms succeed.

🔄 Platform Product Lifecycle

🔍DiscoverTalk to devs
→
🏗️BuildMVP features
→
📊MeasureAdoption, NPS
→
📝LearnIterate

📊 Platform KPIs — How to Measure Success

MetricWhat It MeasuresTarget
Developer NPS"How likely are you to recommend our platform?"> 50 (Excellent)
Time to First DeployFrom idea → code running in production< 1 day
Platform Adoption Rate% of teams using the platform> 80%
Deploy FrequencyDeployments per week (DORA)> 5/week (Elite)
Ticket Avoidance Rate% of requests handled via self-service (not tickets)> 60%
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

The exam WILL test your understanding of Platform as a Product. Be prepared to explain: (1) Why treating the platform as a product matters, (2) How to measure platform success, (3) The difference between a platform built as a project vs. a product.

11.3 Minimum Viable Platform (MVP) — Where to Start

Don't build everything at once. Start with the biggest pain point your developers face. For most teams, it's the journey from "code written" to "code running in production."

🚀 MVP for the anihpj Platform

1
Git Repo Template

Cookiecutter template with Django + Dockerfile + K8s manifests + CI config — pre-configured

2
CI Pipeline

GitHub Actions that lints, tests, security-scans, and builds Docker image automatically

3
One-Click Deploy

ArgoCD configured to auto-deploy from Git. Developer pushes → auto-deploys to staging

4
Logs & Monitoring

Grafana dashboards + Loki logs accessible from Backstage. No SSH or kubectl needed.

🛤️ Platform Evolution Roadmap

MVP → Full Platform Journey
  ┌─────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │   🥚 MVP    │───▶│  🐣 v1.0    │───▶│  🐥 v2.0    │───▶│  🦅 v3.0    │
  │             │    │              │    │              │    │              │
  │• Templates  │    │+ CI/CD      │    │+ Backstage   │    │+ Full IDP    │
  │• Git repos  │    │+ ArgoCD     │    │+ Catalog     │    │  experience  │
  │• Dockerfile │    │+ Monitoring │    │+ Scaffolder  │    │+ Scorecards  │
  │  1 month    │    │  +3 months  │    │  +6 months   │    │  +12 months  │
  └─────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘

11.4 Golden Path Templates — The Paved Road

A Golden Path template is a pre-configured, opinionated scaffold that embodies your organization's best practices. It's the "paved road" — developers stay on it because it's smooth, fast, and safe. They CAN leave it (escape hatches), but most won't want to.

🛣️ Golden Path vs Free Path

The Paved Road Concept
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  🌿 FREE PATH (Bushwhacking)                                         │
  │  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ │
  │  Developer figures everything out alone:                             │
  │  "What base image? How do I configure CI? Where do I put K8s YAML?   │
  │   How do I set up monitoring? What's the secret management strategy? │
  │   Which libraries are approved? How do I configure the database?"    │
  │  → ❌ Takes WEEKS. Inconsistent. Error-prone. Frustrating.           │
  │                                                                      │
  │  🛣️ GOLDEN PATH (Paved Road)                                         │
  │  ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════ │
  │  anictl create service jobpost-api                                   │
  │  → ✅ Template creates: Git repo, Dockerfile, K8s manifests,          │
  │     CI/CD config, monitoring setup, database config, secrets wiring. │
  │  → Developer starts coding in MINUTES, not weeks.                    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 PLATFORM PRINCIPLE: Make the right way the EASY way.              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📦 Cookiecutter Template Structure for anihpj

📖 Cookiecutter vs Copier

Cookiecutter (Python) is the original project templating tool — simple, proven, widely used. Copier (also Python) is a modern alternative with a key advantage: it supports template evolution. When your Golden Path changes (e.g., you add a new monitoring config), Copier can update existing projects to match the new template — like git merge for scaffolding. For anihpj, start with Cookiecutter; graduate to Copier when you need to manage template updates across dozens of services.

cookiecutter-anihpj/ template structure
cookiecutter-anihpj/
├── cookiecutter.json          # Variables: {{service_name}}, {{port}}
├── {{cookiecutter.service_name}}/
│   ├── Dockerfile              # Multi-stage, optimized
│   ├── .github/workflows/
│   │   └── ci.yml              # Pre-configured CI pipeline
│   ├── k8s/
│   │   ├── deployment.yaml     # With {{service_name}} and {{port}}
│   │   ├── service.yaml
│   │   └── argocd-app.yaml    # Auto-register with ArgoCD
│   ├── monitoring/
│   │   └── service-monitor.yaml # Prometheus ServiceMonitor
│   └── README.md               # Auto-generated with setup instructions
└── hooks/
    └── post_gen_project.py     # Post-scaffold: create K8s namespace, register in catalog

11.5 Platform APIs — The Interface Layer

Everything in your platform should be API-driven. Your developer portal, CLI, and any automation all talk to the platform through APIs. This is what makes the platform programmable and composable.

  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘            │

  │         │          │          │          │          │               │

📋 API Types and When to Use Each

API TypeBest Foranihpj Example
RESTCRUD operations, simple integrationsPOST /api/v1/services — create a new service
GraphQLFlexible queries, Backstage backendquery { service(name:"jobpost") { owner, status } }
gRPCHigh-performance internal commsDeployService(deployRequest) → deployResponse stream

🔄 API Versioning — Backward Compatibility is Critical

Platform APIs MUST be versioned. When you change an API, existing clients (CLI, Backstage, CI pipelines) must continue to work. Common strategies:

StrategyExampleProsCons
URL Path Versioning/api/v1/services → /api/v2/servicesSimple, explicit, easy to routeURL proliferation over time
Header VersioningAccept: application/vnd.anihpj.v2+jsonClean URLs, granular per-resourceHarder to discover, cache issues
Query Parameter/api/services?version=2Easy to test, can have defaultsPollutes query params, less RESTful

Platform rule: Support N-1 version for at least 6 months. Announce deprecation via Sunset HTTP headers and developer communication channels. Never silently break an API your developers depend on — that's how you lose platform trust.

Chapter 11b: Backstage — Building a Developer Portal

Spotify Backstage is the leading open-source framework for building developer portals. It unifies all your tools, services, and documentation into a single interface — the "front door" to your IDP.

11b.1 What is Spotify Backstage?

Backstage is an open-source CNCF incubating project (originally created by Spotify) for building developer portals. It provides a unified view of all your services, documentation, CI/CD status, and tooling — reducing the cognitive load of navigating a fragmented toolchain.

🏗️ Backstage Architecture

Backstage Architecture — Frontend + Backend + Plugins
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐         │ │
  │  │  │ Software │ │ TechDocs │ │  Plugins │ │  Search  │         │ │
  │  │  │ Catalog  │ │          │ │  (100+)  │ │          │         │ │
  │  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘         │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘ │
  │                               │                                      │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┐ │
  │  │  ⚙️ BACKEND (Node.js + Express)                                 │ │

  │  │  │ Service  │ │ Service  │ │ Service  │ │ Manager  │         │ │
  │  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘         │ │

  │  │  🗄️ DATABASE (PostgreSQL) + External Integrations              │ │
  │  │  GitHub, GitLab, K8s API, ArgoCD, PagerDuty, Grafana, etc.    │ │
  │  └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

11b.2 Backstage Software Catalog

The Software Catalog is Backstage's central registry of all your services, APIs, websites, and resources. Each entity is defined by a catalog-info.yaml file stored in the service's Git repository. The catalog discovers these files and keeps them in sync.

📦 Component Types (Kinds)

⚙️
Service

Backend service, microservice, or web app (e.g., jobpost)

🌐
Website

Frontend application or static site

📚
Library

Shared code library or SDK

🔌
API

REST, GraphQL, or gRPC endpoints

🗄️
Resource

Infrastructure: databases, queues, buckets

📝 catalog-info.yaml for anihpj-jobpost

catalog-info.yaml
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: jobpost
  description: Job posting service for anihpj platform
  annotations:
    github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
    backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
    argocd/app-name: jobpost
  tags:
    - django
    - python
    - api
  links:
    - url: https://jobpost.anihpj.com
      title: Production URL
spec:
  type: service
  lifecycle: production
  owner: platform-team
  system: anihpj
  providesApis:
    - jobpost-api
  dependsOn:
    - component:postgres
    - resource:anihpj-cluster

📊 Catalog Hierarchy

Domain → System → Component → API/Resource
  Domain: "Hiring Platform"
    └── System: "anihpj"
          ├── Component: "jobpost" (service) → lifecycle: production
          │     ├── API: "jobpost-api" (REST API)
          │     └── Resource: "jobpost-db" (PostgreSQL)
          ├── Component: "skills-service" (service) → lifecycle: experimental
          └── Component: "anihpj-frontend" (website) → lifecycle: production

  💡 Every component has: owner, lifecycle stage, dependencies, and metadata.
     The catalog answers "Who owns this? Is it production? What does it depend on?"

Add team widgets, quick links, and service overview

📖 What You'll See

After setup: One URL where developers see ALL their services, deployment status, documentation, on-call schedules, and CI status. No more "which dashboard has the logs?" or "where's the runbook for jobpost?" — it's all in Backstage.

📝 app-config.yaml — Key Configuration Snippets

app-config.yaml
# Database configuration (PostgreSQL for production)
backend:
  database:
    client: pg
    connection:
      host: ${POSTGRES_HOST}
      port: ${POSTGRES_PORT}
      user: ${POSTGRES_USER}
      password: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}

# GitHub integration (auto-discover catalog-info.yaml)
integrations:
  github:
    - host: github.com
      token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}

# Kubernetes plugin
kubernetes:
  serviceLocatorMethod:
    type: 'multiTenant'
  clusterLocatorMethods:
    - type: 'config'
      clusters:
        - url: https://k8s.anihpj.com
          name: anihpj-cluster
          authProvider: 'serviceAccount'

# ArgoCD plugin
argocd:
  appLocatorMethods:
    - type: 'config'
      instances:
        - name: argocd
          url: https://argocd.anihpj.com
          username: ${ARGOCD_USERNAME}
          password: ${ARGOCD_PASSWORD}

# TechDocs (publish docs from your repos)
techdocs:
  builder: 'local'
  generator:
    runIn: 'docker'
  publisher:
    type: 'local'

📋 Chapter 11 & 11b Summary — IDP Concepts & Backstage

🔑
Key Concept

An IDP is a self-service platform layer. Platform as a Product means treating devs as customers. Golden Paths make the right way the easy way. Backstage is the front door to your IDP.

🏗️
IDP Architecture

3 layers: Developer Experience (Backstage/CLI), Platform Engineering (templates/CI/CD/GitOps), Infrastructure (K8s/DB/Cloud). APIs connect everything.

🎸
Backstage

Software Catalog = central registry. Scaffolder = self-service creation. Plugins = K8s, ArgoCD, TechDocs, Grafana. catalog-info.yaml registers services.

📝 Chapters 11 & 11b — CNPA Practice Questions

Q1
What is an Internal Developer Platform (IDP)? How does it differ from a traditional PaaS?
Show Answer
Answer

An IDP is a self-service layer built by a platform team that abstracts infrastructure complexity for developers. Unlike a traditional PaaS (Heroku, Cloud Foundry) which is a pre-built product, an IDP is composed of multiple tools (Backstage + ArgoCD + Crossplane + Prometheus) tailored to the organization's specific needs. Key differences: IDP is customizable (you choose the tools), PaaS is opinionated (vendor chooses). IDP evolves with your org, PaaS is what the vendor offers.

Explanation

The CNPA exam tests the distinction: an IDP is NOT a product you buy — it's a platform you BUILD. The key IDP components are: developer portal (Backstage), GitOps (ArgoCD), CI/CD (GitHub Actions), observability (Prometheus/Grafana), security (Kyverno), and infrastructure APIs (Crossplane). Any single-vendor "IDP product" is really just a PaaS.

Q2
Explain "Platform as a Product." What practices does this imply for the anihpj platform team?
Show Answer
Answer

"Platform as a Product" treats the IDP with product management discipline — developers are customers. Practices: (1) Product Manager for the platform — prioritizes features based on developer needs. (2) User research — interview developers, measure pain points. (3) Roadmap — publicly visible, prioritized by impact. (4) Developer NPS surveys — measure satisfaction quarterly. (5) Adoption metrics — % teams onboarded, time-to-10th-deploy. (6) SLAs for platform services — 99.9% availability for CI/CD.

Explanation

This is a core CNPA concept (Domain 5). The old model: infra team builds what THEY think developers need. The new model: platform team asks developers what THEY need, builds that, measures satisfaction. The exam asks: "What is Platform as a Product?" → Treating the IDP with product management discipline, treating developers as customers.

Q3
What are the 5 levels of the IDP Maturity Model? Where should a startup with 15 developers start?
Show Answer
Answer

L1 — Documentation: Wiki/confluence with guides. No automation. L2 — Templates: Golden Path templates (Cookiecutter, GitHub templates). 80% reduction in deploy time. L3 — Self-Service: Developer portal + automated provisioning. "Click to deploy." L4 — Fully Automated: Everything automated — CI/CD, observability, security, scaling. L5 — AI-Assisted: AI suggests configs, auto-remediates, predicts failures.

Startup (15 devs): Start at L2 — a single GitHub template with Dockerfile + K8s manifests + CI pipeline. Don't jump to L5. Evolve organically as the team grows.

Explanation

The maturity model is a CNPA exam favorite. Key point: you don't start at L5. You start at L2 (templates), then add self-service (L3), then automate (L4), then add AI (L5). Trying to build L5 on day one = platform failure. The exam asks: "What maturity level does a GitHub template represent?" → L2 (Templates).

Q4
What are the three layers of IDP architecture? Describe each using anihpj as an example.
Show Answer
Answer

Layer 1 — Developer Experience: Backstage portal + anictl CLI. Developers interact with the platform through a unified interface. They see service catalog, create new services, view CI status, access documentation.

Layer 2 — Platform Engineering: Templates, CI/CD pipelines, GitOps (ArgoCD), API gateway. The platform logic — every developer action in Layer 1 triggers workflows in Layer 2. Create service → template renders → repo created → CI runs → ArgoCD deploys.

Layer 3 — Infrastructure: Kubernetes clusters, databases (RDS), cloud (AWS/GCP). The actual compute, storage, and networking. Provisioned via Terraform (initial) and Crossplane (ongoing self-service).

Explanation

The 3-layer architecture is a CNPA Domain 1 concept. The layers are connected by APIs: Layer 1 → Layer 2 via Backstage plugins, Layer 2 → Layer 3 via K8s API and Crossplane CRDs. The exam may ask: "Which layer handles developer-facing interfaces?" → Layer 1. "Which layer manages infrastructure?" → Layer 3.

Q5
What is Backstage? Describe its three core components and how they solve the tool sprawl problem.
Show Answer
Answer

Backstage is an open-source CNCF Incubating framework for building developer portals. Software Catalog: Central registry of all services, APIs, websites, and resources. Auto-discovered from Git repos via catalog-info.yaml. Answers: "Who owns this? Where's the code? Is it healthy?" Software Templates (Scaffolder): Self-service service creation. Developer fills a form → Backstage creates a new repo with pre-configured CI/CD, Dockerfile, K8s manifests. TechDocs: Documentation-as-code — Markdown in each repo auto-rendered as documentation in Backstage.

Explanation

Backstage is the reference IDP implementation. The CNPA exam tests: "What are Backstage's core components?" → Catalog + Templates + TechDocs. "How does Backstage discover services?" → catalog-info.yaml files in Git repos. "What is the Scaffolder?" → Self-service template engine for creating new services.

Q6
What is a catalog-info.yaml file? Write one that registers the anihpj/jobpost service in Backstage.
Show Answer
Answer
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Component
metadata:
  name: jobpost
  description: Job posting service for anihpj platform
  annotations:
    github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost
    backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.
  tags: [django, python, api]
spec:
  type: service
  lifecycle: production
  owner: platform-team
  system: anihpj
Explanation

Every service in Backstage needs a catalog-info.yaml. This YAML tells Backstage: the service name, what type it is (service/library/website), who owns it, where the code lives, and what lifecycle stage it's in (experimental/production/deprecated). Backstage auto-discovers these files via GitHub integration or manual registration.

Q7
How does a Golden Path differ from a Paved Road? Which company pioneered each concept?
Show Answer
Answer

Both terms are often used interchangeably, but have subtle differences: Golden Path: (Spotify) The opinionated, fully-supported workflow. Internal — the platform team defines and maintains it. "Use this template, this CI, this deploy method — and we support you fully." Paved Road: (Netflix) The easy, supported path with explicit escape hatches. "Here's the paved road — it's fast, well-maintained, and we support it. You CAN go off-road, but you accept the cost of maintaining your own path." Key difference: Golden Path implies ONE right way. Paved Road implies one EASY way + escape hatches.

Explanation

The CNPA exam may ask about both concepts. Golden Path = Spotify, opinionated. Paved Road = Netflix, with escape hatches. Both achieve the same goal: making the right way the easy way. For anihpj: start with Golden Paths, allow teams to deviate if they accept the maintenance cost.

Q8
What are Backstage plugins? Name 5 essential plugins for a platform engineering portal.
Show Answer
Answer

Plugins extend Backstage with integrations to other tools. Five essential plugins: (1) Kubernetes plugin — see pods, deployments, services directly in Backstage. (2) ArgoCD plugin — see deployment status, sync health. (3) GitHub Actions plugin — CI status per service. (4) Grafana plugin — embedded dashboards. (5) PagerDuty plugin — on-call rotation, who to contact. Others: TechDocs, Catalog (built-in), Lighthouse (scorecards), Sentry (errors), Datadog.

Explanation

Plugins are Backstage's extensibility model. The CNPA exam values the concept: "How does Backstage integrate with other tools?" → Plugins. Each plugin adds a new tab or widget to service pages. The platform team chooses which plugins to install — this IS your IDP's feature set.

Q9
How does developer cognitive load relate to platform engineering? What's the platform's role in reducing it?
Show Answer
Answer

Cognitive load is the mental effort required to understand and work with infrastructure. High cognitive load = developers must understand K8s, Terraform, Helm, CI/CD, networking, security, monitoring — before shipping a single feature. The platform reduces cognitive load by abstracting infrastructure complexity behind self-service interfaces. Team Topologies identifies three types: intrinsic (core domain, good), extraneous (infra complexity, bad — platform targets this), germane (learning, good). The platform eliminates extraneous load so developers focus on intrinsic (business logic).

Explanation

Cognitive load theory (from Team Topologies) is a CNPA concept. The exam asks: "What type of cognitive load does an IDP reduce?" → Extraneous load. "Why reduce cognitive load?" → So developers focus on business value, not infrastructure. The platform's job is to make infrastructure invisible.

Q10
Design the complete IDP architecture for anihpj covering all three layers, tool selection, and how components interact.
Show Answer
Answer

Layer 1 (DevEx): Backstage (Catalog + Templates + TechDocs) + anictl CLI. Developers interact via web portal or terminal. Layer 2 (Platform): GitHub Actions (CI), ArgoCD (GitOps), Argo Rollouts (canary), cert-manager (TLS), Kyverno (policies). When a developer creates a service in Backstage → Scaffolder generates repo with CI/CD → ArgoCD deploys → cert-manager provisions TLS → Kyverno enforces policies. Layer 3 (Infra): EKS cluster, RDS PostgreSQL (via Crossplane), VPC. Terraform provisions initial infra, Crossplane handles ongoing self-service. Glue: APIs connect all layers — Backstage REST API, K8s API, Crossplane CRDs, ArgoCD API.

Explanation

This holistic architecture question tests your complete IDP understanding. The CNPA values this end-to-end thinking: every component has a purpose, and they form a coherent platform. No single tool is the IDP — the IDP IS the composition of all these tools working together.

Chapter 12: Service Catalogs and Developer Self-Service

A service catalog is the single source of truth for every service in your organization. Combined with self-service provisioning, it transforms how developers interact with infrastructure — from ticket-based requests to instant, automated fulfillment.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1 hour 📋 Catalog 🎯 Domain 5: 8%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The service catalog as the central nervous system of your platform
  • ✅ Self-service provisioning — "click to deploy" workflows
  • ✅ Scorecards for measuring service maturity
  • ✅ Building a Platform CLI (anictl) for terminal-native developers

12.1 The Service Catalog — Single Source of Truth

A service catalog is a central registry of every service, API, website, and resource in your organization. It answers the questions every developer asks: "Who owns this service? What does it depend on? Is it in production? Where's the runbook?"

🧠 The Catalog as the Central Nervous System

Service Catalog — Everything Connects Through It
                          ┌──────────────────────┐
                          │   📋 SERVICE CATALOG  │
                          │   (Single Source of   │
                          │    Truth)             │
                          └──────────┬───────────┘
                                     │
        ┌────────────┬───────────────┼───────────────┬────────────┐
        │            │               │               │            │
        ▼            ▼               ▼               ▼            ▼
  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │  Owner   │ │   Repo   │ │   CI/CD  │ │  Monitor │ │   Docs   │
  │platform- │ │ github/  │ │  Status  │ │ Grafana  │ │ TechDocs │
  │  team    │ │  anihpj  │ │  ✅ Pass │ │Dashboard │ │ Runbook  │
  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘

  💡 "Who owns the jobpost API?" → Catalog. "Is it healthy?" → Catalog.
     "Where's the code?" → Catalog. "Who's on call?" → Catalog.

📋 Essential Catalog Metadata

✅ Every Service in Your Catalog Should Have:
  1. Owner — Team or person responsible (not "everyone")
  2. Repository URL — Direct link to source code
  3. Deployment Status — Is it healthy? Last deploy?
  4. Runbook URL — What to do when it breaks at 3 AM
  5. SLOs/SLIs — Availability target, latency budget
  6. Dependencies — What services does it call? What calls it?
  7. Lifecycle — Experimental → Production → Deprecated

12.2 Self-Service Provisioning — "Click to Deploy"

Self-service is the defining capability of a mature IDP. Instead of filing a ticket ("Please create a new service for me"), developers use the platform to provision everything they need — instantly.

🏗️ Scaffolder Workflow — Form → Template → Git → CI → K8s

Self-Service Provisioning Flow for anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SELF-SERVICE PROVISIONING — End-to-End Flow                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① Developer fills form in Backstage:                                │
  │     Service Name: [jobpost-api          ]                             │
  │     Language:     [Python/Django     ▼  ]                             │
  │     Database:     [PostgreSQL        ▼  ]                             │
  │     Team:         [platform-team     ▼  ]                             │
  │                      [ CREATE SERVICE ]                               │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ② Scaffolder processes template:                                    │
  │     ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐                         │
  │     │ Template Variables → Rendered Output  │                         │
  │     │ {{name}}          → jobpost-api       │                         │
  │     │ {{db}}            → postgresql        │                         │
  │     │ {{port}}          → 8000              │                         │
  │     └──────────────────────────────────────┘                         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ③ Automated actions kick off:                                       │
  │     ✅ Create GitHub repo: anihpj/jobpost-api                        │
  │     ✅ Push template code (Django + Dockerfile + K8s YAML + CI)      │
  │     ✅ Create K8s namespace: jobpost-api-prod                        │
  │     ✅ CI pipeline starts automatically (GitHub Actions)             │
  │     ✅ ArgoCD deploys to staging environment                         │
  │     ✅ Register service in Backstage Catalog                         │
  │     ✅ Send Slack notification: "@platform-team jobpost-api is LIVE!" │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ⏱️ TOTAL TIME: ~5 minutes (vs. 3-5 days via ticket queue)          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
📖 What Should Be Self-Service?

New services, databases, K8s namespaces, CI/CD pipelines, secrets (with approval), monitoring dashboards, documentation sites, ephemeral preview environments for PRs. Basically everything a developer needs to ship software.

12.3 Scorecards and Service Maturity

Scorecards grade your services against best practices — giving teams visibility into what's healthy and what needs improvement. They turn platform standards from "someone said we should" into measurable, trackable goals.

📊 Example Scorecard for anihpj Services

Criteria🟢 Pass🔴 Fail
Has Owner?Owner team is defined in catalogOwner = "unknown" or empty
CI Configured?CI pipeline runs on every PRNo CI pipeline or disabled
Has Monitoring?Grafana dashboard + alerts existNo monitoring or alerting
Has Runbook?Runbook exists in TechDocsNo documentation
SLOs Defined?Availability + latency SLOs setNo SLOs
Secrets Managed?Uses Vault/External SecretsHardcoded secrets or env vars
Lifecycle Valid?Not deprecatedDeprecated but still running

Scorecards create healthy peer pressure. When every team sees their score, they naturally want to improve it. It's gamification for platform engineering excellence.

📝 Scorecard Definition YAML (Backstage TechDocs)

scorecard.yaml — Service Maturity Scorecard
apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1
kind: Scorecard
metadata:
  name: service-maturity
spec:
  checks:
    - id: has-owner
      name: "Has Owner"
      description: "Service has a defined owner team"
      weight: 10
      check:
        type: jsonata
        expression: "$exists(spec.owner) and spec.owner != 'unknown'"

    - id: has-ci
      name: "CI Configured"
      description: "CI pipeline runs on every PR"
      weight: 10
      check:
        type: github-actions
        workflow: ci.yml

    - id: has-monitoring
      name: "Has Monitoring"
      description: "Grafana dashboard and alerts configured"
      weight: 10
      check:
        type: grafana
        dashboardId: "anihpj-{{ metadata.name }}"

    - id: has-runbook
      name: "Has Runbook"
      description: "Runbook exists in TechDocs"
      weight: 5
      check:
        type: techdocs
        path: "/docs/runbook.md"

    - id: slos-defined
      name: "SLOs Defined"
      description: "Availability and latency SLOs are set"
      weight: 10
      check:
        type: jsonata
        expression: "$exists(spec.slos)"

    - id: secrets-managed
      name: "Secrets Managed"
      description: "Uses Vault or External Secrets (not hardcoded)"
      weight: 15
      check:
        type: vault
        path: "kv/anihpj/{{ metadata.name }}"

  scoring:
    passThreshold: 70
    levels:
      - name: 🥇 Gold
        minScore: 90
      - name: 🥈 Silver
        minScore: 70
      - name: 🥉 Bronze
        minScore: 50
      - name: 🔴 Needs Improvement
        minScore: 0

12.4 Platform CLI — Bringing the Platform to the Terminal

Developers live in the terminal. A Platform CLI (like anictl for anihpj) brings self-service capabilities directly to their fingertips — no UI required. It's the CLI equivalent of Backstage's Scaffolder.

🐍
Click (Python)

Best for Python-native platforms. Decorator-based, composable commands. Recommended for anihpj since it's a Django shop.

🐹
Cobra (Go)

Best for Kubernetes-native CLIs. Powers kubectl, Helm, and ArgoCD CLI. Single binary, no runtime dependency.

🟢
Oclif (Node.js)

Best for TypeScript/Node.js platforms. Plugin-based architecture. Used by Heroku CLI and Salesforce CLI.

🖥️ anictl — Platform CLI for anihpj (Python Click)

anictl — Platform CLI Example
# anictl/cli.py
import click

@click.group()
def cli():
    """anictl — anihpj Platform CLI"""

@cli.command()
@click.option('--name', prompt='Service name')
@click.option('--language', type=click.Choice(['python', 'node', 'go']))
@click.option('--database', type=click.Choice(['postgres', 'mysql', 'none']))
def create(name, language, database):
    """Create a new service from a Golden Path template"""
    click.echo(f"🚀 Creating service '{name}'...")
    # 1. Call Platform API to scaffold from template
    # 2. Git repo created + code pushed
    # 3. K8s namespace provisioned
    # 4. CI pipeline triggered
    # 5. ArgoCD deploys to staging
    click.echo(f"✅ {name} is live! Repo: github.com/anihpj/{name}")

@cli.command()
@click.option('--service', prompt='Service name')
@click.option('--env', type=click.Choice(['staging', 'production']))
@click.option('--version', prompt='Image version tag')
def deploy(service, env, version):
    """Deploy a service to an environment"""
    click.echo(f"📦 Deploying {service}:{version} to {env}...")
    # Update Git repo with new image tag → ArgoCD auto-syncs

@cli.command()
@click.option('--service', prompt='Service name')
def logs(service):
    """Stream logs from a service (connects to Loki)"""
    click.echo(f"📜 Streaming logs for {service}... (Ctrl+C to stop)")

@cli.command()
def env_list():
    """List available environments"""
    click.echo("Environments:")
    click.echo("  🧪 staging    — anihpj-staging namespace")
    click.echo("  🚀 production — anihpj-prod namespace")

if __name__ == '__main__':
    cli()

📟 CLI in Action

Terminal
$ anictl create --name jobpost-v2 --language python --database postgres
🚀 Creating service 'jobpost-v2'...
✅ jobpost-v2 is live! Repo: github.com/anihpj/jobpost-v2

$ anictl deploy --service jobpost-v2 --env staging --version v1.0.0-abc1234
📦 Deploying jobpost-v2:v1.0.0-abc1234 to staging...
✅ Deployed! https://staging.jobpost-v2.anihpj.com

$ anictl logs --service jobpost-v2
📜 Streaming logs for jobpost-v2...
[INFO] 2026-06-05T10:30:01Z Request: GET /api/jobs — 200 OK (12ms)

You've built a functioning IDP prototype for anihpj, from the architecture blueprint to the self-service CLI (anictl). You understand the IDP maturity model (L1: Documentation → L2: Templates → L3: Self-Service → L4: Fully Automated → L5: AI-Assisted), the critical components of a developer portal (service catalog, software templates, TechDocs, scorecards), and how Backstage and Port differ. Most importantly, you now see that an IDP is not a product you buy — it's a platform you build by composing open-source tools (Backstage, ArgoCD, Crossplane, Prometheus) into a unified developer experience.

🏗️ Key Takeaway 1

IDP = Internal Developer Platform = a self-service layer that lets developers deploy apps without knowing K8s, Terraform, or cloud details.

🏗️ Key Takeaway 2

Golden Paths = opinionated, supported workflows. Developers stay on the paved road and get support. Deviate at your own risk (but you CAN if needed).

🏗️ Key Takeaway 3

Start at L2 (templates), not L5. A simple GitHub template + CI pipeline reduces deploy time by 80%. Evolve to Backstage when you have 10+ services.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — IDPs & Service Catalogs (Domain 5, 8%)

The CNPA exam tests your understanding of Internal Developer Platforms conceptually. Key facts: (1) An IDP is a composition of tools (Backstage + ArgoCD + Crossplane + Prometheus), not a single product you buy. (2) The Service Catalog is the central nervous system — it answers: who owns this, where's the code, is it healthy? (3) Self-service is the defining feature of a mature IDP — replacing ticket queues with automated provisioning. (4) Scorecards measure service maturity and create healthy organizational pressure. (5) The CNPA exam may ask: "What is the purpose of a service catalog?" → Single source of truth for all services. "How does self-service improve DevEx?" → Eliminates manual ticket queues, empowers developers to provision their own infrastructure.

Chapter 13: Platform Engineering Metrics and Measuring Success

You can't improve what you don't measure. This chapter covers DORA metrics, platform-specific KPIs, Developer Experience (DevEx) measurement, and building a platform dashboard that gives everyone visibility into how the platform is performing.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1 hour 📊 Metrics 🎯 Domain 6: 8%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ DORA's four key metrics and elite performance benchmarks
  • ✅ Platform-specific metrics beyond DORA
  • ✅ How to measure and improve Developer Experience (DevEx)
  • ✅ Building a platform dashboard for visibility

13.1 DORA Metrics — The Four Key Metrics

DORA (DevOps Research and Assessment, now part of Google Cloud) identified four metrics that predict software delivery performance. These are the industry standard for measuring delivery effectiveness.

📊 The Four DORA Metrics

MetricDefinitionEliteHighMediumLow
1. Deployment FrequencyHow often code is deployed to productionOn-demand (multiple/day)Once/day-1 weekOnce/week-1 monthOnce/1-6 months
2. Lead Time for ChangesTime from code committed to code in production< 1 hour1 day-1 week1 week-1 month1-6 months
3. Change Failure Rate% of deployments causing failure in production0-15%0-15%0-15%46-60%
4. Time to Restore ServiceHow long to recover from a failure< 1 hour< 1 day< 1 day1 week-1 month
🔄
Deploy Freq

Multiple deploys/day = Elite. More deploys = smaller changes = safer.

⏱️
Lead Time

< 1 hour is Elite. Short lead time = fast feedback = faster learning.

❌
Change Fail Rate

0-15% across ALL tiers. Elite orgs deploy MORE but fail LESS.

🔧
Restore Time

< 1 hour is Elite. Fast recovery = high resilience. Blameless postmortems help.

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know all four DORA metrics by heart. The exam typically asks: (1) What are the four DORA metrics? (2) What defines elite performance? (3) How does a platform improve DORA metrics? Answer: by reducing friction — templates speed up lead time, CI/CD increases deploy frequency, GitOps reduces change failure rate.

13.2 Platform-Specific Metrics (Beyond DORA)

DORA measures delivery — but platform engineering needs additional metrics to measure platform adoption, efficiency, and developer satisfaction.

MetricDefinitionHow to MeasureTarget
Time to First DeployFrom idea → code running in productionTimestamp from service creation to first successful deploy< 1 day
Developer Onboarding TimeFrom laptop setup → first commit mergedTrack from account creation to first merged PR< 2 hours
Platform Adoption Rate% of teams actively using the platformTeams on platform / total teams> 80%
Developer NPS"How likely to recommend our platform?" (0-10)Quarterly survey> 50 (Excellent)
Cognitive Load ScoreHow much do developers need to know to deploy?Survey: "How many tools do you touch to deploy?"< 3 tools
Ticket Avoidance Rate% of requests handled via self-service (not tickets)Self-service actions / (self-service + tickets)> 60%
📖 Why These Matter

DORA tells you if you're delivering fast. Platform metrics tell you if your platform is helping — or getting in the way. If DORA metrics improve but Developer NPS drops, you've built a fast platform that developers hate. Both must trend upward.

13.3 Measuring and Improving Developer Experience (DevEx)

Developer Experience (DevEx) is how developers feel about their work environment, tools, and workflows. The SPACE framework provides a structured way to measure it across five dimensions.

🧩 The SPACE Framework

DimensionWhat It MeasuresHow to Measure
SatisfactionHow happy are developers with their tools?NPS surveys, micro-surveys after key workflows
PerformanceHow fast do they deliver?DORA metrics, lead time, deploy frequency
ActivityWhat are they doing?Commit frequency, PR review time, build counts
CommunicationHow well do teams collaborate?PR comments, documentation quality, knowledge sharing
EfficiencyHow much time is wasted?"Toil Tax" — time on repetitive, non-value-add work

💰 The "Toil Tax"

Where Developer Time Goes
  WITHOUT IDP:                          WITH IDP:
  ┌──────────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 🚀 Value Work:      ████ 20%    │   │ 🚀 Value Work:      ████████ 70% │
  │ 🔧 Toil/Toolchain:  ██████ 50%  │   │ 🔧 Toil:            ██ 15%       │
  │ 📋 Waiting/Tickets: ████ 30%    │   │ 📋 Waiting:         ██ 15%       │
  └──────────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────────┘

  💡 The platform's job: Shrink the red/orange and grow the green.

📋 How to Measure DevEx in Practice

MethodFrequencyWhat You Learn
Quarterly NPS SurveyEvery 3 monthsBroad satisfaction trend; "How likely to recommend our platform?"
Micro-SurveysAfter key workflows (deploy, onboard)Immediate feedback on specific experiences; "How was your deploy today?"
ShadowingQuarterly (1-2 devs)Watch a developer work for 2 hours; see friction they don't report
Contextual InterviewsBi-annuallyDeep understanding of pain points; "Walk me through your last deploy"
Platform AnalyticsContinuousTime-to-deploy, error rates, CLI command frequency, drop-off points
💡 DevEx Pro Tip

The most valuable DevEx data often comes from watching someone struggle. A developer might say "it's fine" in a survey but you'll see them copy-pasting secrets from Slack, switching between 7 tools, and waiting 20 minutes for a build. That's your backlog of platform improvements.

13.4 Platform Dashboard — Visibility for All

A platform dashboard gives everyone — developers, platform engineers, and leadership — visibility into how the platform is performing. It should be the first thing you see every morning.

📊 What to Show on Your Platform Dashboard

✅ Dashboard Panels
  1. Deploy Frequency Sparkline — Deployments per day over the last 30 days (trending up = good)
  2. Lead Time Histogram — Distribution of time from commit to production
  3. Change Failure Rate Trend — % of deployments causing incidents over time (trending down = good)
  4. Active Services Count — Total services, broken down by lifecycle (production, experimental, deprecated)
  5. Platform Adoption % — Teams on platform vs. total teams
  6. Developer NPS Gauge — Current NPS score with trend arrow
  7. Top 5 Services by Deploy Frequency — Leaderboard (gamification!)
  8. Open Incidents — Current incidents with status and on-call engineer

📈 PromQL Queries for Platform Metrics

Prometheus Queries for Platform Dashboard
# Deploy Frequency (deployments per day, 7-day avg)
rate(deployment_total[7d]) * 86400

# Lead Time (p99 commit-to-deploy time in minutes)
histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(lead_time_seconds_bucket[30d])) / 60

# Change Failure Rate (failed deploys / total deploys)
sum(rate(deployment_failed_total[30d])) / sum(rate(deployment_total[30d])) * 100

# Active Services Count
count(service_info{lifecycle="production"})

# Platform Adoption Rate
count(team_using_platform) / count(team_total) * 100
