🟡 Platform Engineering Context

Your platform dashboard is how you prove the platform's value to the organization. When leadership asks "What are we getting for this investment?", you pull up the dashboard and show: adoption up, lead time down, deploy frequency up, NPS trending positive. Data beats opinions every time.

📋 Chapters 12 & 13 Summary — Catalogs & Platform Metrics

📋
Service Catalog

Single source of truth for all services. Answers: who owns this? Is it healthy? Where's the code? Self-service provisioning and scorecards drive platform maturity.

📊
DORA Metrics

Deploy Frequency, Lead Time, Change Failure Rate, Time to Restore. Elite performers: multiple deploys/day, <1h lead time, 0-15% CFR, <1h MTTR.

📈
Platform KPIs

Time to First Deploy, Developer NPS, Platform Adoption, Ticket Avoidance, Cognitive Load. SPACE framework for DevEx. Platform dashboard for visibility.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Platform Metrics & DORA (Domain 6, 8%)

The CNPA exam tests your ability to measure platform success. Must-know: (1) Four DORA metrics — Deploy Frequency, Lead Time for Changes, Change Failure Rate, Time to Restore Service. Elite performers: multiple deploys/day, <1h lead time, 0-15% CFR, <1h MTTR. (2) Platform-specific KPIs: Time to First Deploy (target: <15 min), Developer NPS, Platform Adoption %, Ticket Avoidance Rate. (3) SLI vs SLO vs SLA: SLI = what you measure (99.95% success rate), SLO = target (99.9%), SLA = contract with consequences (refund if <99.5%). (4) Error Budget: 1 − SLO = allowed unreliability. Burn rate = how fast you consume it. (5) SPACE framework: Satisfaction, Performance, Activity, Communication, Efficiency — measures DevEx holistically.

📝 Chapters 12 & 13 — CNPA Practice Questions (Service Catalogs & Metrics)

Q1
What is a Service Catalog and what problems does it solve? What's the minimum metadata every service needs?
Show Answer
Answer

A Service Catalog is the single source of truth for every service in the organization. Problems solved: "Who owns this?", "Where's the code?", "Is it healthy?", "Who's on call?", "What does it depend on?" Minimum metadata: Owner (team/person), Repository URL, Deployment Status, Runbook URL, Lifecycle (experimental/production/deprecated). Without a catalog, developers waste hours searching wikis, Slack, and asking around.

Explanation

The Service Catalog is the central nervous system of the IDP. CNPA exam: "What is the purpose of a service catalog?" → Single source of truth for all services. "How do services get registered?" → catalog-info.yaml in each repo, auto-discovered by Backstage. The catalog answers the 5 Ws: Who, What, Where, When (last deploy), Why (runbook/SLOs).

Q2
What is self-service provisioning? Walk through the flow from a developer clicking "Create Service" to the service running in production.
Show Answer
Answer

Self-service replaces ticket queues with automated workflows. Flow: (1) Developer fills form (service name, language, DB type). (2) Scaffolder processes template, generates code + Dockerfile + K8s YAML + CI config. (3) New GitHub repo created, code pushed. (4) CI pipeline auto-starts (build → test → scan → push image). (5) ArgoCD detects new app in config repo → deploys to staging. (6) cert-manager provisions TLS, Kyverno validates. (7) Service registered in Catalog. (8) Slack notification: "Your service is live!" Total: ~5 min vs ~3-5 days via ticket queue.

Explanation

Self-service is the defining feature of a mature IDP. CNPA exam: "What enables self-service provisioning?" → Service Templates + Scaffolder + automated CI/CD. "What is the benefit?" → Time-to-deploy drops from weeks to minutes. This is how you go from L2 (templates) to L3 (self-service) on the maturity model.

Q3
What are Scorecards in the context of a developer platform? List 5 criteria you'd measure for anihpj services.
Show Answer
Answer

Scorecards grade services against best practices. Five criteria for anihpj: (1) Has Owner — defined team, not "unknown." (2) CI Configured — pipeline runs on every PR. (3) Has Monitoring — Grafana dashboard + alerts exist. (4) Has Runbook — documentation for 3 AM incidents. (5) SLOs Defined — availability and latency targets. Scoring: Gold (90+), Silver (70+), Bronze (50+), Needs Improvement (<50). Scorecards create healthy peer pressure — teams naturally want to improve their score.

Explanation

Scorecards are a platform governance tool. CNPA exam: "How do you enforce platform standards without being a bottleneck?" → Scorecards — they measure, not block. Teams see their scores and self-improve. This is "platform as a product" thinking: provide visibility, let teams act on it. Backstage's TechDocs plugin can render scorecards.

Q4
Name the four DORA metrics and their Elite performer benchmarks. How would you measure each for anihpj?
Show Answer
Answer

① Deploy Frequency: How often code is deployed to production. Elite: multiple times/day. Measure: count production ArgoCD syncs per day. ② Lead Time for Changes: Time from commit to production. Elite: <1 hour. Measure: git commit timestamp → ArgoCD deployment timestamp. ③ Change Failure Rate: % of deploys causing failure. Elite: 0-15%. Measure: (deploys triggering rollback or incident) / total deploys. ④ Time to Restore Service: Time to recover from failure. Elite: <1 hour. Measure: incident start → service restored timestamp.

Explanation

DORA metrics are the CNPA Domain 6 gold standard. Memorize all four + Elite benchmarks. The exam asks: "Which metric measures deployment speed?" → Deploy Frequency. "Which measures recovery?" → Time to Restore. "Which measures stability?" → Change Failure Rate. Elite = multiple deploys/day, <1h lead time, <15% CFR, <1h MTTR.

Q5
Define SLI, SLO, and SLA. What is an Error Budget and how is it calculated? Give an anihpj example.
Show Answer
Answer

SLI (Service Level Indicator): What you MEASURE. Example: "99.95% of jobpost API requests succeed over 30 days." SLO (Service Level Objective): Your TARGET. Example: "jobpost API availability must be ≥ 99.9%." SLA (Service Level Agreement): CONTRACT with consequences. Example: "If availability drops below 99.5%, customers receive a 25% refund." Error Budget: 1 − SLO = allowed unreliability. If SLO = 99.9%, error budget = 0.1% (~43 min/month downtime). Burn rate = how fast you consume it. When budget is exhausted → freeze features, fix reliability.

Explanation

SLI/SLO/SLA is a core CNPA concept (Domain 2). The exam tests distinctions: "Which is the measurement?" → SLI. "Which is the target?" → SLO. "Which has financial consequences?" → SLA. Error Budget is the SRE concept that balances reliability vs feature velocity. When error budget runs out, you stop shipping features and invest in reliability.

Q6
What is the SPACE framework for measuring Developer Experience? List all 5 dimensions.
Show Answer
Answer

SPACE measures DevEx holistically: S — Satisfaction: Developer surveys, NPS. "Are you happy with the platform?" P — Performance: DORA metrics, deploy frequency, lead time. "How fast can you ship?" A — Activity: PR count, deploy count, commit count. "How much are you shipping?" C — Communication: Docs quality, knowledge sharing, onboarding time. "How easy is it to learn?" E — Efficiency: Time-to-first-deploy, cognitive load, tool switching. "How much friction is there?"

Explanation

SPACE (from GitHub/Microsoft research) is the comprehensive DevEx framework. The CNPA exam tests: "What framework measures developer experience?" → SPACE. Key insight: don't just measure DORA (Performance) — measure ALL five dimensions. A team shipping fast (Performance) but miserable (Satisfaction) is not a success.

Q7
What is Platform Adoption and how do you measure it? What's a good adoption target?
Show Answer
Answer

Platform Adoption = % of teams/services using the platform vs going their own way. Measure: (1) % teams onboarded to platform. (2) % new services created via Golden Path (not manual). (3) % deploys going through platform CI/CD. (4) Time-to-10th-deploy (a better metric than time-to-first — it measures consistency). Target: 80%+ adoption. Key insight: adoption isn't mandated — it's EARNED. If adoption is low, the platform has a problem, not the developers. Fix the platform, don't force adoption.

Explanation

Platform adoption is a Domain 6 metric. The CNPA exam values: "How do you know if your platform is successful?" → Adoption rate + Developer NPS. Low adoption = platform isn't solving real problems. Never mandate adoption — if developers voluntarily use the platform, you've built something good. If not, go back and build what they actually need.

Q8
What is a Platform Dashboard? Design the key panels showing platform health for anihpj stakeholders.
Show Answer
Answer

A Platform Dashboard provides visibility into platform performance for different audiences. Key panels: Executive View: Platform adoption %, Developer NPS trend, Time-to-deploy trend, Cost per service. Platform Team View: CI pipeline success rate, ArgoCD sync health, Crossplane resource health, SLO attainment. Developer View: My services' CI status, My services' deploy history, My services' SLO status, Incident count.

Explanation

The Platform Dashboard proves value. CNPA exam: "How do you demonstrate platform ROI?" → Dashboard with adoption, NPS, time-to-deploy metrics. Different audiences need different views. The dashboard turns platform engineering from "cost center" to "value driver" in the eyes of leadership.

Q9
How does developer NPS (Net Promoter Score) work for platforms? What score indicates a healthy platform?
Show Answer
Answer

Developer NPS asks: "How likely are you to recommend our platform to a colleague?" (0-10). Promoters (9-10): Actively recommend. Passives (7-8): Satisfied but not enthusiastic. Detractors (0-6): Unhappy. NPS = % Promoters − % Detractors. Healthy platform: 50+ NPS. Excellent: 70+. Measure quarterly, track trends, follow up with detractors to understand WHY. NPS tells you WHAT (satisfaction level), follow-up interviews tell you WHY.

Explanation

Developer NPS is a Platform as Product metric. CNPA exam: "How do you measure developer satisfaction?" → Developer NPS surveys. Important: NPS alone isn't enough — you need qualitative follow-up. A score of 30 with detailed feedback is better than 70 with no feedback. Always close the loop: "You said X was broken → we fixed it → try again?"

Q10
A team's time-to-deploy went from 2 hours to 15 minutes after adopting the IDP. The VP asks "What's the business value?" How do you quantify it?
Show Answer
Answer

Quantify in dollars: Before: 2 hours/deploy × 20 deploys/week × 50 developers = 2,000 developer-hours/year lost to deployment. At $150/hour loaded cost = $300,000/year in deploy overhead. After: 15 min/deploy × 20 deploys/week × 50 = 250 dev-hours/year = $37,500/year. Savings: $262,500/year in reclaimed developer time. Plus: faster time-to-market (competitive advantage), fewer deployment errors (reliability), happier developers (retention). The IDP's ROI is 5-10x its cost within the first year.

Explanation

CNPA Domain 6 tests your ability to measure and communicate platform value. Always translate metrics into dollars and developer-hours — that's what leadership understands. "15-minute deploys" is a technical metric. "$262K saved" is a business metric. The best platform engineers speak both languages.

🟡 Part 5: Observability — Logs, Metrics, and Traces Chapters 14-16

Chapter 14: Observability Fundamentals — The Three Pillars

Observability is the ability to understand what's happening inside your system from its external outputs. For platform engineers, it's not just about dashboards — it's about giving developers the tools to debug and understand their applications without SSH access.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔍 Observability 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The three pillars: Logs, Metrics, and Traces — and how they combine
  • ✅ OpenTelemetry — the universal observability standard for all services
  • ✅ Structured logging for machine-parseable, searchable logs
  • ✅ Distributed tracing — following a request across microservices

14.1 What is Observability and Why Platform Engineers Need It?

Monitoring tells you known failures: "CPU is at 95%." Observability tells you unknown failures: "Why are users getting 500 errors when CPU is fine?" Monitoring answers known-unknowns; observability answers unknown-unknowns.

📖 Platform Relevance

As a platform engineer, you don't build the applications — but you MUST provide the observability infrastructure so developers can debug their own apps. Build observability INTO the platform (standardized across all services), not bolted on after.

🔍 The Three Pillars of Observability

Logs + Metrics + Traces = Complete Observability
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐                 │
  │  │ 📝 LOGS     │  │ 📊 METRICS  │  │ 🔗 TRACES   │                 │
  │  │             │  │             │  │             │                 │
  │  │ What        │  │ How much?   │  │ Where did   │                 │
  │  │ happened?   │  │ How fast?   │  │ time go?    │                 │
  │  │             │  │             │  │             │                 │
  │  │ "POST /jobs │  │ "p99 latency│  │ "Request     │                 │
  │  │ 500 error   │  │ is 200ms"   │  │ spent 50ms   │                 │
  │  │ at 14:23:05"│  │             │  │ in DB query" │                 │
  │  │             │  │             │  │             │                 │
  │  │ Tool:       │  │ Tool:       │  │ Tool:       │                 │
  │  │ Loki/EFK    │  │ Prometheus  │  │ Jaeger/Tempo│                 │
  │  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘  └──────┬──────┘                 │
  │         │                │                │                          │
  │         └────────────────┼────────────────┘                          │
  │                          │                                           │
  │                          ▼                                           │
  │                   ┌─────────────┐                                    │
  │                   │ 🔮 COMPLETE │                                    │
  │                   │  Picture of │  ← All three together give you     │
  │                   │  System     │    the full story of what's        │
  │                   │  Health     │    happening in your platform      │
  │                   └─────────────┘                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📈 Observability Maturity Model

LevelNameCapabilities
🔴 L1: ReactiveBasic MonitoringCPU/memory dashboards, manual log checks via SSH
🟠 L2: ProactiveStructured MonitoringAlerts on thresholds, centralized logging, basic dashboards
🟡 L3: ObservableFull ObservabilityDistributed tracing, structured logs, RED metrics, SLO-based alerts
🟢 L4: PredictiveAI-AssistedAnomaly detection, automated root cause analysis, capacity forecasting
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

The exam expects you to understand: (1) The difference between monitoring and observability — monitoring tells you WHAT broke, observability tells you WHY, (2) The three pillars — Logs=what happened, Metrics=how much, Traces=where time went, (3) Why platform engineers must build observability INTO the platform. Expect: "A developer reports slow responses but CPU is normal. Which tool helps?" Answer: distributed tracing.

14.2 OpenTelemetry — The Universal Observability Standard

OpenTelemetry (OTel) is a CNCF project that provides a single, vendor-neutral standard for collecting logs, metrics, and traces. Instead of instrumenting your code 3 times (once per backend), you instrument once with OTel and export to any backend.

🏗️ OTel Architecture

OpenTelemetry — App → SDK → Collector → Backends
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐                                                 │
  │  │  anihpj Django   │                                                 │
  │  │  (Python App)    │                                                 │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐ │     ┌──────────────────┐                      │
  │  │  │ OTel SDK    │─┼────▶│ OTel Collector   │                      │
  │  │  │ (Auto-instr)│ │     │ (Gateway Mode)   │                      │
  │  │  └─────────────┘ │     │                  │                      │
  │  └─────────────────┘     │ • Receives data  │                      │
  │                          │ • Processes      │                      │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐     │ • Batches        │                      │
  │  │  jobpost Service │     │ • Exports        │                      │
  │  │  ┌─────────────┐ │     └────┬───┬───┬────┘                      │
  │  │  │ OTel SDK    │─┼──────────┘   │   │                            │
  │  │  └─────────────┘ │              │   │                            │
  │  └─────────────────┘              │   │                            │
  │                                   │   │                            │
  │                    ┌──────────────┘   └──────────────┐             │
  │                    ▼                     ▼            ▼             │
  │             ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐              │
  │             │ 📊 Metrics│  │ 📝 Logs  │  │ 🔗 Traces│              │
  │             │ Prometheus│  │   Loki   │  │  Tempo   │              │
  │             └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🐍 Python OTel Auto-Instrumentation for anihpj

Django OTel Setup
# requirements.txt additions
opentelemetry-api
opentelemetry-sdk
opentelemetry-instrumentation-django
opentelemetry-instrumentation-psycopg2
opentelemetry-exporter-otlp

# manage.py or wsgi.py
from opentelemetry.instrumentation.django import DjangoInstrumentor
DjangoInstrumentor().instrument()

# settings.py
OTEL_SERVICE_NAME = "anihpj-jobpost"
OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT = "http://otel-collector:4317"

# Run with auto-instrumentation (zero code changes!)
opentelemetry-instrument gunicorn anihpj.wsgi:application --bind 0.0.0.0:8000
💡 Auto vs Manual Instrumentation

Auto-instrumentation (shown above) adds traces and metrics with zero code changes — just attach the OTel agent. Manual instrumentation gives you fine-grained control: create custom spans for business logic, add attributes (user ID, tenant, feature flag), and instrument non-HTTP workloads like Celery tasks or Kafka consumers. Start with auto; add manual for critical paths.

14.3 Structured Logging for Platform Teams

Structured logging means writing logs as JSON objects instead of plain text. This makes logs machine-parseable, searchable, and consistent across all services — critical when you have dozens of services writing logs.

🐍 Django Structured JSON Logging

settings.py — LOGGING Configuration
LOGGING = {
    'version': 1,
    'disable_existing_loggers': False,
    'formatters': {
        'json': {
            '()': 'pythonjsonlogger.jsonlogger.JsonFormatter',
            'format': '%(timestamp)s %(level)s %(name)s %(message)s',
        },
    },
    'handlers': {
        'console': {
            'class': 'logging.StreamHandler',
            'formatter': 'json',
        },
    },
    'root': {
        'handlers': ['console'],
        'level': 'INFO',
    },
}

# Output (stdout → collected by Fluentd/Bit → Loki):
# {"timestamp": "2026-06-05T10:30:01Z", "level": "INFO",
#  "service": "jobpost", "request_id": "abc-123",
#  "method": "GET", "path": "/jobs/", "status": 200,
#  "duration_ms": 45, "message": "Request completed"}

📋 What to Log (and What NOT to Log)

✅ Always Log❌ Never Log
Request ID (trace correlation)Passwords, API keys, tokens
Service name and versionCredit card numbers, SSNs
HTTP method, path, status codeFull request bodies with PII
Request durationSession tokens (log hashed ID only)
Error stack traces (redact secrets)Personal emails, phone numbers

🔄 Log Aggregation Pipeline

Fluentd/Bit → Loki → Grafana
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐                        │
  │  │ anihpj   │   │ jobpost  │   │ skills-  │   ← Apps write JSON     │
  │  │ Pod      │   │ Pod      │   │ service  │     logs to stdout      │
  │  └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┘                        │
  │       └───────────────┼──────────────┘                               │
  │                       │                                              │
  │                       ▼  DaemonSet collects logs from all pods       │
  │                 ┌──────────┐                                         │
  │                 │ Fluentd  │  (or Fluent Bit — lighter weight)       │
  │                 │ DaemonSet│  Parses JSON + adds K8s metadata        │
  │                 └────┬─────┘  Routes to multiple destinations        │
  │                      │                                               │
  │           ┌──────────┼──────────┐                                    │
  │           ▼          ▼          ▼                                    │
  │     ┌─────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐                             │
  │     │  Loki   │ │ Kafka  │ │ S3/GCS   │  ← Storage backends          │
  │     │(Grafana)│ │(Stream)│ │(Archive) │                             │
  │     └────┬────┘ └────────┘ └──────────┘                             │
  │          │                                                            │
  │          ▼  Grafana queries Loki with LogQL                           │
  │     ┌─────────┐                                                     │
  │     │ Grafana │  "Show ERROR logs for jobpost in last 1h"           │
  │     │ Explore │  {app="jobpost",level="ERROR"} |= "timeout"          │
  │     └─────────┘                                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

14.4 Distributed Tracing — Following Requests Across Services

A trace is the complete journey of a request through all services. A span is a single unit of work within a trace (e.g., "DB query took 30ms"). Traces let you see exactly where time is spent — critical for debugging slow requests in microservice architectures.

🔗 Trace Waterfall for anihpj — GET /jobs/

Distributed Trace — Request Journey
  TRACE ID: 1a2b3c4d5e6f — GET /jobs/ (Total: 150ms)
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  │
  ├─ SPAN: Django View "job_list"          [0ms → 150ms]  150ms
  │   ├─ SPAN: Auth Check                  [0ms →   5ms]    5ms
  │   ├─ SPAN: DB Query "SELECT * FROM     [5ms → 105ms]  100ms ⚠️
  │   │   jobpost_jobpost WHERE status=1"
  │   │   └─ SPAN: PostgreSQL Execute      [8ms → 103ms]   95ms
  │   └─ SPAN: Template Render             [105ms→ 135ms]  30ms
  │       └─ SPAN: Cache Check (Redis)     [106ms→ 107ms]   1ms ✅
  │
  💡 FINDING: DB query takes 100ms (67% of total). That's your bottleneck.
     Query optimization or caching could reduce total time to ~55ms.

🔑 Context Propagation — How Traces Cross Services

Traces work across services via HTTP headers automatically injected by OTel:

HeaderPurposeExample
traceparentTrace ID + Span ID + flags00-1a2b3c...-4d5e6f...-01
tracestateVendor-specific trace contextvendor=opaque-value

When anihpj calls another service, OTel automatically adds these headers. The downstream service picks them up and creates child spans — building a complete trace across your entire platform.

✅ Chapter 14 Summary — Observability Fundamentals

You now understand the three pillars of observability: Logs (discrete events — "what happened?"), Metrics (numerical data over time — "how much?"), and Traces (request journeys across services — "where did the time go?"). You've learned how OpenTelemetry standardizes all three, how context propagation (traceparent headers) enables distributed tracing, and the critical distinction between monitoring (known unknowns) and observability (unknown unknowns). For the CNPA exam, remember: OTel is the observability standard for CNCF, the three signal types map to different debugging questions, and traces are the most powerful for microservice debugging.

🔍 Key Takeaway 1

Logs = discrete events. Metrics = time-series data. Traces = request journeys. All three together = observability. OTel standardizes all of them.

🔍 Key Takeaway 2

Monitoring = known failures (dashboards, alerts). Observability = ability to ask NEW questions about your system without deploying new code.

🔍 Key Takeaway 3

Context propagation (traceparent header) is how traces cross service boundaries. OTel SDK automatically injects these — you get distributed tracing for free.

📝 Chapter 14 — CNPA Practice Questions (Observability)

Q1
What are the three pillars of observability? What question does each answer?
Show Answer
Answer

Logs: Discrete, timestamped events. Answers: "What happened at this exact moment?" Example: "User 123 failed login at 10:30:01." Metrics: Numerical data over time. Answers: "How much/how fast?" Example: "Request rate: 200 req/s, P99 latency: 450ms." Traces: Request journey across services. Answers: "Where did the time go?" Example: "Request spent 100ms in DB query, 30ms in template render." All three together = full observability.

Explanation

CNPA Domain 2 tests the three pillars. Key exam fact: Logs = events, Metrics = aggregates, Traces = journeys. Monitoring = known unknowns (dashboards). Observability = unknown unknowns (ability to ask new questions). The three pillars complement each other — you need ALL three for true observability.

Q2
What is OpenTelemetry (OTel) and what problem does it solve? Which CNCF maturity level is it?
Show Answer
Answer

OTel is a CNCF Incubating project that provides a single, vendor-neutral standard for collecting logs, metrics, and traces. Without OTel: you instrument your code 3 times (once per backend — Prometheus, Jaeger, Loki). With OTel: instrument ONCE, export to ANY backend. OTel provides SDKs (auto-instrumentation for Python, Java, Go, etc.) and a Collector (processes, batches, and routes telemetry data).

Explanation

OTel is the CNCF observability standard. CNPA exam: "Which project standardizes observability data collection?" → OpenTelemetry. "What CNCF level is OTel?" → Incubating (not yet Graduated, but widespread adoption). Key concept: vendor-neutral — you can switch from Jaeger to Tempo without changing instrumentation code.

Q3
What is the RED method? How would you apply it to the anihpj jobpost API?
Show Answer
Answer

RED = Rate, Errors, Duration — the three golden signals for every service. Rate: Requests per second — rate(http_requests_total{app="jobpost"}[5m]). Errors: Failed request rate — rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]). Duration: Request latency — histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])). If you monitor nothing else, monitor RED. It applies universally — Django, Go, Node.js, any HTTP service.

Explanation

RED is the simplest effective monitoring pattern. CNPA exam: "What method monitors Rate, Errors, Duration?" → RED method. It's from Tom Wilkie (Grafana Labs). USE method (Utilization, Saturation, Errors) is for resources (CPU, memory). RED is for services (requests, failures, latency). Know the difference.

Q4
What is structured logging and why is JSON format preferred for cloud native platforms? Show an example for anihpj.
Show Answer
Answer

Structured logging outputs logs in a machine-parseable format (JSON) instead of free-form text. Why JSON: (1) Queryable — Loki/Grafana can filter {level="ERROR"} instantly. (2) Aggregatable — count errors by service, endpoint. (3) Correlatable — join logs with traces via trace_id. Example: {"timestamp":"2026-06-05T10:30:01Z","level":"INFO","service":"jobpost","method":"GET","path":"/jobs/","status":200,"duration_ms":45}. Compare to unstructured: "2026-06-05 10:30:01 INFO jobpost: GET /jobs/ 200 45ms" — harder to query, harder to parse.

Explanation

Structured logging is a platform best practice. CNPA exam: "Why JSON logging?" → Machine-parseable, queryable, correlates with traces. Key: include trace_id and span_id in every log line to correlate logs ↔ traces. Loki automatically indexes JSON fields for fast LogQL queries.

Q5
Explain distributed tracing and context propagation. How does a trace cross from anihpj Django to the auth service?
Show Answer
Answer

A trace is a collection of spans representing a request's journey. Context propagation passes trace context (trace ID + span ID) across service boundaries via HTTP headers. When anihpj Django calls the auth service: OTel SDK automatically injects traceparent: 00-{trace_id}-{span_id}-01 into the outgoing HTTP request. The auth service's OTel SDK reads this header, creates a child span with the same trace_id but a new span_id. Result: one unified trace showing time spent in Django + auth + DB — even though they're different services.

Explanation

Context propagation is what makes distributed tracing actually WORK across services. CNPA exam: "How do traces cross service boundaries?" → Context propagation via HTTP headers (traceparent). W3C Trace Context standard defines the header format. OTel SDKs handle this automatically — you get distributed tracing with minimal code changes.

Q6
What's the difference between Monitoring and Observability? Give an example of an observability question you CAN'T answer with monitoring alone.
Show Answer
Answer

Monitoring: Known unknowns — you set up dashboards and alerts for things you EXPECT might fail. "Is CPU above 80%?" "Is error rate above 1%?" You know what you're looking for. Observability: Unknown unknowns — you can ask NEW questions about your system without deploying new code or adding new metrics. Observability question: "A user reported slow response at 10:32 AM on Tuesday. What was the exact request path through all our services? Which service spent the most time? Was it the same for other users?" Monitoring can't answer this — traces can.

Explanation

Monitoring vs Observability is a CNPA Domain 2 distinction. Monitoring = predefined checks. Observability = exploratory debugging. The key: with observability (traces + structured logs + high-cardinality metrics), you can debug issues you never anticipated. OTel enables observability; Prometheus dashboards are monitoring.

Q7
What are the four golden signals from Google SRE? How do they map to the RED method?
Show Answer
Answer

Google SRE defines four golden signals: Latency: Time to serve a request. Traffic: Demand on your system (requests/sec). Errors: Rate of failed requests. Saturation: How "full" your system is (CPU, memory, queue depth). RED maps cleanly: Rate = Traffic, Errors = Errors, Duration = Latency. Saturation is the additional dimension RED misses — it's about resource utilization. RED + Saturation = complete picture.

Explanation

Google SRE golden signals are a CNPA concept. The exam may ask: "What are the four golden signals?" → Latency, Traffic, Errors, Saturation. "How do they relate to RED?" → RED covers Latency/Traffic/Errors; Saturation is the fourth dimension. Together they tell you: is the service fast, busy, broken, or overloaded?

Q8
What is an OTel Collector? Compare Gateway mode vs Agent mode deployment.
Show Answer
Answer

The OTel Collector is a vendor-neutral service that receives, processes, and exports telemetry data. Agent mode: Collector runs as a DaemonSet on each K8s node. Each pod sends data to its local collector, which forwards to backends. Lower latency, higher resource usage (one collector per node). Gateway mode: Centralized collectors as a Deployment. All telemetry flows through a central gateway. Easier to manage, single point for batching/retrying. Hybrid: Agent → Gateway → Backend. Agents handle local collection, Gateway handles central processing, batching, and routing.

Explanation

OTel Collector deployment is a platform engineering decision. CNPA exam: "Which OTel Collector mode runs on every node?" → Agent mode (DaemonSet). "Which centralizes processing?" → Gateway mode. Best practice: hybrid — agents for proximity, gateway for central policy + batching + routing to multiple backends.

Q9
How does Loki differ from Elasticsearch/ELK for log aggregation? When would you choose Loki?
Show Answer
Answer

Loki: Indexes only metadata (labels: app, namespace, level) — NOT full text. Log content is stored as compressed chunks. Much cheaper storage (10-100x less than ELK). Queries via LogQL. Natively integrated with Grafana. ELK (Elasticsearch): Indexes FULL text of every log line. Powerful full-text search. Much more expensive (indexes everything). Better for: complex full-text search, compliance/audit use cases. Choose Loki when: you're already using Grafana, you query by labels (service/level), cost is a concern.

Explanation

Loki vs ELK is a platform engineering tool choice. CNPA exam: "Which log aggregator indexes only metadata?" → Loki. "Which does full-text indexing?" → ELK. Loki follows the Prometheus philosophy — label-based indexing. ELK follows the search engine philosophy. Modern platforms prefer Loki for cost + Grafana integration.

Q10
Design the complete observability stack for anihpj covering: collection, storage, visualization, alerting, and tracing.
Show Answer
Answer

Collection: OTel SDK auto-instruments Django → OTel Collector (Agent DaemonSet) receives all telemetry. Metrics storage: Prometheus (TSDB, scrapes /metrics every 15s via ServiceMonitor). Log storage: Loki (label-indexed, compressed, integrated with Grafana). Fluent Bit DaemonSet collects stdout logs → Loki. Trace storage: Tempo (or Jaeger). OTel Collector exports traces → Tempo. Visualization: Grafana — unified dashboards for metrics, logs, and traces. LogQL → Loki, PromQL → Prometheus, TraceQL → Tempo. Alerting: Prometheus AlertManager → PagerDuty/Slack. Grafana alerts for SLO burn rate.

Explanation

This is the full CNCF LGTM stack + OTel. CNPA exam values this end-to-end thinking: OTel collects → Prometheus/Tempo/Loki store → Grafana visualizes → AlertManager alerts. Every component has a specific role. This is the production observability architecture for cloud native platforms.

Chapter 15: Prometheus and Grafana — Metrics and Visualization

Prometheus is the de facto standard for metrics in Kubernetes. Grafana turns those metrics into beautiful, actionable dashboards. Together, they form the monitoring backbone of every cloud native platform.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📊 Monitoring 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

15.1 Prometheus — The CNCF Metrics Standard

Prometheus is a pull-based monitoring system: it scrapes metrics from HTTP endpoints on your services (default: /metrics on port 9090 targets). It stores data in a custom time-series database and queries it with PromQL.

🏗️ Prometheus Architecture

Prometheus — Scrape → TSDB → PromQL → AlertManager
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐               │
  │  │ K8s Node 1   │  │ K8s Node 2   │  │ K8s Node 3   │               │
  │  │ ┌──┐ ┌──┐   │  │ ┌──┐ ┌──┐   │  │ ┌──┐ ┌──┐   │               │
  │  │ │P1│ │P2│   │  │ │P3│ │P4│   │  │ │P5│ │P6│   │               │
  │  │ └──┘ └──┘   │  │ └──┘ └──┘   │  │ └──┘ └──┘   │               │
  │  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘  └──────┬───────┘               │
  │         │                 │                 │                        │
  │         └─────────────────┼─────────────────┘                        │
  │                           │  ① Scrape every 15s (pull)               │
  │                           ▼                                          │
  │                    ┌──────────────┐                                  │
  │                    │  PROMETHEUS  │                                  │
  │                    │  ┌────────┐  │                                  │
  │                    │  │  TSDB  │  │  Time-Series Database           │
  │                    │  └────────┘  │                                  │
  │                    │  ┌────────┐  │                                  │
  │                    │  │ PromQL │  │  ② Query Engine                 │
  │                    │  └────────┘  │                                  │
  │                    └──────┬───────┘                                  │
  │                           │                                          │
  │              ┌────────────┼────────────┐                            │
  │              │            │            │                             │
  │              ▼            ▼            ▼                             │
  │       ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐                       │
  │       │ Grafana  │ │AlertMgr  │ │  API     │  ③ Visualization,     │
  │       │Dashboard │ │→ PagerD  │ │ Clients  │    Alerting, API      │
  │       └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📊 Prometheus Metric Types

TypeBehaviorExampleUse Case
CounterOnly increases (or resets to 0)http_requests_totalRequest count, errors
GaugeGoes up and downcpu_usage_percentCPU, memory, queue depth
HistogramObservations in bucketsrequest_duration_secondsP50, P95, P99 latency
SummaryPre-calculated quantilesrequest_duration_summaryClient-side quantiles

🔍 Prometheus Service Discovery in Kubernetes

prometheus.yml — K8s Service Discovery
scrape_configs:
  - job_name: 'kubernetes-pods'
    kubernetes_sd_configs:
      - role: pod
    relabel_configs:
      # Only scrape pods with annotation: prometheus.io/scrape=true
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_scrape]
        action: keep
        regex: 'true'
      - source_labels: [__meta_kubernetes_pod_annotation_prometheus_io_path]
        action: replace
        target_label: __metrics_path__
        regex: '(.+)'
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know Prometheus fundamentals: (1) It's pull-based (scrapes targets, unlike push-based systems), (2) Four metric types — Counter (only up), Gauge (up/down), Histogram (buckets for P50/P95/P99), Summary (client-side quantiles), (3) PromQL basics like rate(), histogram_quantile(). The exam may ask: "Which metric type for request latency?" Answer: Histogram (server-side percentiles).

15.3 Grafana — Beautiful Dashboards and Alerting

Grafana is an open-source visualization platform that connects to Prometheus (and 100+ other data sources) to create dashboards, set alerts, and share insights. It's where your metrics become actionable.

📊
Dashboard Panels

Time series, stat (big number), gauge, bar gauge, table, heatmap, geomap, pie chart, histogram, logs panel, node graph — the right visualization for every metric and audience.

🚨
Alerting

Define alert rules in Grafana (or Prometheus AlertManager). Route to PagerDuty, Slack, email, webhook. Multi-channel, severity-based routing.

📝 Dashboard as Code — Provisioning

Grafana Dashboard Provisioning
# grafana-dashboards.yaml (ConfigMap, auto-imported by Grafana)
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: grafana-dashboards
  labels:
    grafana_dashboard: "1"      # Auto-discovered by Grafana sidecar
data:
  platform-overview.json: |
    {
      "title": "anihpj Platform Overview",
      "panels": [
        {
          "title": "Request Rate",
          "targets": [{
            "expr": "rate(http_requests_total{app=\"jobpost\"}[5m])"
          }]
        }
      ]
    }

15.4 RED Method — The Golden Signals for Every Service

The RED Method (Rate, Errors, Duration) gives you three essential metrics for every service. If you monitor nothing else, monitor these three. They apply universally — whether it's a Django monolith or 50 microservices.

🔴🟡🟢 RED Applied to anihpj

SignalDefinitionanihpj ExamplePromQL
RateRequests per secondGET /jobs/ requests per secondrate(http_requests_total{app="jobpost"}[5m])
ErrorsFailed requests5xx errors on /jobs/ endpointrate(http_requests_total{app="jobpost",status=~"5.."}[5m])
DurationHow long requests takeP95 latency of /jobs/ listinghistogram_quantile(0.95,rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{app="jobpost"}[5m]))
💡 Platform Engineer's Rule

Every service deployed on your platform should expose RED metrics in Prometheus format. If a service doesn't have them, the platform should flag it via a scorecard (Ch 12). RED is non-negotiable for production services.

15.5 Hands-On: Set Up Monitoring for anihpj

1
Install Prometheus

helm install prometheus prometheus-community/kube-prometheus-stack -n monitoring

2
Install Grafana

Included in kube-prometheus-stack. Access via: kubectl port-forward svc/grafana 3000:80 -n monitoring

3
Configure Scraping

Add annotation to anihpj pods: prometheus.io/scrape: "true"

4
Import K8s Dashboard

Import Grafana dashboard ID 315 (Kubernetes cluster monitoring)

5
Create RED Dashboard

Create panels for Rate, Errors, Duration for jobpost service

6
Set Up Alerts

Pod restarts > 3 in 1h → Slack. P95 latency > 500ms for 5min → PagerDuty

📝 Helm Values for anihpj Monitoring Stack

values.yaml — kube-prometheus-stack
prometheus:
  prometheusSpec:
    podMonitorSelectorNilUsesHelmValues: false
    retention: 15d
    resources:
      requests: { cpu: 500m, memory: 2Gi }
      limits: { cpu: 2, memory: 4Gi }

grafana:
  adminPassword: "admin123"  # Change in production!
  dashboards:
    default:
      kubernetes-cluster:
        gnetId: 315          # Pre-built K8s cluster dashboard
        datasource: Prometheus

alertmanager:
  config:
    route:
      group_by: ['alertname']
      receiver: 'slack'
      routes:
        - match: { severity: critical }
          receiver: 'pagerduty'
    receivers:
      - name: 'slack'
        slack_configs:
          - channel: '#alerts-anihpj'
      - name: 'pagerduty'
        pagerduty_configs:
          - routing_key: 'your-pd-integration-key'

Chapter 16: Alerting, Incident Management, and SLOs

Metrics are useless if nobody looks at them. This chapter covers how to turn your observability data into actionable alerts, manage incidents when they happen, and define SLOs that set clear expectations for platform reliability.

🟡 Intermediate ⏱️ ~1 hour 🚨 Alerting 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ SLIs, SLOs, SLAs, and Error Budgets — the reliability vocabulary
  • ✅ Alerting best practices: signal vs noise, severity levels, routing
  • ✅ Incident management lifecycle and blameless postmortems
  • ✅ Hands-on: Define SLOs and burn-rate alerts for anihpj

16.1 Service Level Objectives (SLOs) — The Platform Promise

SLOs are quantifiable reliability targets you set for your services. They turn vague expectations ("the platform should be reliable") into measurable goals ("99.9% of requests succeed within 500ms").

📐 SLI → SLO → SLA → Error Budget

The Reliability Hierarchy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  SLI (Service Level Indicator) — What you MEASURE                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ "P99 request latency for /jobs/ endpoint over last 30 days"   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼                                       │
  │  SLO (Service Level Objective) — Your TARGET                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ "99.9% of requests complete within 500ms over 30 days"        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                              │                                       │
  │                              ▼                                       │
  │  SLA (Service Level Agreement) — Your CONTRACT (with penalties)     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ "99.5% uptime or customer gets 10% refund" ← Usually external │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ERROR BUDGET = 100% - SLO                                          │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ 99.9% SLO = 0.1% error budget ≈ 43 min downtime/month        │   │
  │  │ BURN RATE: How fast you're consuming the budget                │   │
  │  │ → Fast burn (2% of budget in 1h) = PAGE the on-call!          │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📊 SLO Examples for Different Service Types

Service TypeAvailability SLOLatency SLO (P95)Error Budget/Month
Critical API (jobpost)99.95%< 500ms~22 min downtime
Standard Web App99.9%< 1s~43 min downtime
Background Worker99%< 10s~7.2 hours downtime
Internal Dashboard99.5%< 2s~3.6 hours downtime
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

You MUST know the difference between SLI, SLO, and SLA. The exam will test: (1) What is an error budget? (2) How do you calculate burn rate? (3) When should you halt deployments? Answer: when you're burning error budget too fast — use the remaining budget for reliability work, not feature work.

16.2 Alerting Best Practices

Bad alerting is worse than no alerting — it causes alert fatigue, where people start ignoring alerts. Good alerting follows clear principles.

🌳 Should I Create an Alert for This?

Alert Decision Tree
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Does this require IMMEDIATE human action?│
  └──────────┬──────────────────┬───────────┘
             │ YES              │ NO
             ▼                  ▼
  ┌──────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ Is it a SYMPTOM      │  │ Don't alert.          │
  │ (not a cause)?       │  │ Use a dashboard,      │
  └───┬─────────────┬────┘  │ ticket, or automated   │
      │ YES         │ NO    │ remediation instead.   │
      ▼             ▼       └──────────────────────┘
  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────────────────┐
  │ CREATE   │ │ Alert on the SYMPTOM │
  │ ALERT    │ │ not the cause.       │
  │          │ │ "Service returning   │
  │          │ │ errors" NOT "CPU     │
  │          │ │ high on node-3"      │
  └──────────┘ └──────────────────────┘

  💡 GOLDEN RULE: Every alert must be:
     • Actionable (someone must DO something)
     • Linked to a runbook (how to respond)
     • Properly prioritized (Critical → Wake up, Warning → Slack, Info → Email)

🚨 Alert Severity Levels

SeverityActionChannelResponse Time
🔴 CriticalWake someone up NOWPagerDuty (phone call)< 5 minutes
🟠 WarningLook soon, might escalateSlack #oncall channel< 30 minutes
🟡 InfoFYI, no action needed nowEmail / Dashboard annotationNext business day

16.3 Incident Management for Platform Teams

When an alert fires, you need a clear process — not panic. The incident management lifecycle ensures structured response, resolution, and learning.

🔄 Incident Lifecycle

Detect → Respond → Mitigate → Resolve → Learn
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① DETECT           ② RESPOND          ③ MITIGATE                   │
  │  ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────┐                  │
  │  │ Alert    │ ───▶ │ Incident │ ───▶  │ Stop the │                  │
  │  │ fires    │      │ declared │       │ bleeding │                  │
  │  │ (PagerD) │      │ IC named │       │ (rollback│                  │
  │  └──────────┘      └──────────┘       │ or scale)│                  │
  │                                        └────┬─────┘                  │
  │                                             │                        │
  │  ⑤ LEARN              ④ RESOLVE            │                        │
  │  ┌──────────┐        ┌──────────┐           │                        │
  │  │Blameless │ ◀───── │ Service  │ ◀────────┘                        │
  │  │Postmortem│        │ Restored │                                    │
  │  │+ Action  │        │ + Verified│                                   │
  │  │ Items    │        └──────────┘                                    │
  │  └──────────┘                                                        │
  │                                                                      │
  │  👤 ROLES:                                                            │
  │  • Incident Commander (IC) — Coordinates, delegates, communicates    │
  │  • Operations Lead — Technical troubleshooting and fixes             │
  │  • Communications Lead — Updates status page, notifies stakeholders  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📝 Blameless Postmortem Template

✅ Postmortem Sections
  1. Summary — What happened, impact, duration (2-3 sentences)
  2. Timeline — Minute-by-minute from detection to resolution
  3. Root Cause — What caused it (not WHO caused it)
  4. What Went Well — What worked in our response?
  5. What Went Poorly — What could have been faster/better?
  6. Action Items — Concrete, assigned, with due dates

Golden rule of postmortems: Focus on systems and processes, not people. Every incident is an opportunity to make the platform more resilient. If your postmortem blames a person, you've failed.

16.4 Hands-On: Define SLOs and Alerts for anihpj

1
Identify Critical User Journeys

For anihpj: View jobs (GET /jobs/), Post job (POST /jobs/), Search jobs (GET /jobs/?search=)

2
Define SLIs

Availability (% of 2xx responses), Latency (P95 in ms), Error Rate (% of 5xx)

3
Set SLOs

99.9% availability, P95 < 500ms, error rate < 1% over 30-day rolling window

4
Calculate Error Budgets

0.1% budget = ~43 min downtime/month. Track burn rate continuously.

5
Create Recording Rules

Prometheus rules to pre-compute SLI metrics for faster querying

6
Create Burn-Rate Alerts

Fast burn: 2% budget consumed in 1 hour → Page on-call

7
Create SLO Dashboard

Grafana dashboard showing SLO compliance, error budget remaining, burn rate

📝 Prometheus SLO Recording Rules

slo-rules.yaml
groups:
  - name: anihpj-slos
    interval: 30s
    rules:
      # SLI: Availability (ratio of successful requests)
      - record: job:http_requests:availability_ratio
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="jobpost",status!~"5.."}[30d]))
          /
          sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="jobpost"}[30d]))

      # SLI: P95 Latency
      - record: job:http_request_duration:p95
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.95,
            sum(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket{app="jobpost"}[30d])) by (le))

      # Error Budget Remaining (as percentage)
      - record: job:slo:error_budget_remaining
        expr: |
          (1 - (1 - job:http_requests:availability_ratio) / (1 - 0.999)) * 100

🚨 Burn-Rate Alert Rules

alert-rules.yaml
groups:
  - name: anihpj-alerts
    rules:
      # Fast burn: 2% of error budget consumed in 1 hour
      - alert: SLOFastBurn
        expr: |
          job:slo:error_budget_remaining < 98
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj jobpost burning error budget too fast"
          description: "Error budget {{ $value | humanize }}% remaining. Check Grafana."
          runbook_url: "https://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/slo-fast-burn"

      # P95 latency exceeding SLO for 5 minutes
      - alert: HighLatency
        expr: job:http_request_duration:p95 > 0.5
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "anihpj jobpost P95 latency > 500ms"
          runbook_url: "https://wiki.anihpj.com/runbooks/high-latency"
📖 Platform Engineer's Role

As a platform engineer, you define the SLO framework (recording rules, dashboards, alert templates) — but service teams set their own SLO targets. The platform provides the tools; teams own their reliability. This is the essence of "you build it, you run it" with platform support.

📋 Chapter 16 Summary — Alerting, Incidents & SLOs

📐
SLO Framework

SLI = what you measure. SLO = your target. SLA = your contract. Error Budget = 100% - SLO. Burn rate = how fast you consume it.

🚨
Alerting Rules

Alert on symptoms, not causes. Every alert: actionable + runbook + proper severity (Critical/Warning/Info). Avoid alert fatigue.

🔄
Incident Mgmt

Detect→Respond→Mitigate→Resolve→Learn. Blameless postmortems with action items. Platform provides tools; teams own reliability.

🔴 Part 6: Security, Compliance & Policy Management Chapters 17-19

Chapter 17: Platform Security Fundamentals — Defense in Depth

Security is not a feature — it's a property of the platform. As a platform engineer, you build the secure defaults that every developer and every service inherits automatically. This chapter covers the foundational security controls every platform must have.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🛡️ Security 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Defense in Depth — the 7-layer security model for platforms
  • ✅ Kubernetes RBAC — controlling who can do what
  • ✅ Pod Security Standards — Privileged, Baseline, Restricted
  • ✅ Network Policies — micro-segmentation for workloads
  • ✅ Secrets Management — Vault, External Secrets, Sealed Secrets

17.1 The Platform Security Mindset

Platform security is fundamentally different from application security. You're not securing one app — you're building secure defaults that protect every service on the platform. Key principles:

⬅️
Shift Left

Security checks early in development — not at deploy time. Lint secrets, scan dependencies, scan images in CI — before they reach the cluster.

🧅
Defense in Depth

Multiple security layers — if one fails, the next catches it. No single point of security failure. Code→Build→Registry→Deploy→Runtime→Network→Data.

🛡️ The 7 Layers of Platform Security

Defense in Depth — Security Layers for anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  Layer 1: CODE           │ SAST (Bandit), dependency scan, secret    │
  │                          │ detection, code review, signed commits    │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 2: BUILD          │ Reproducible builds, SBOM generation,     │
  │                          │ signed provenance (SLSA L2+)              │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 3: REGISTRY       │ Image scanning (Trivy), signed images     │
  │                          │ (Cosign), immutable tags, RBAC            │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 4: DEPLOY         │ Admission control (Kyverno/OPA), policy   │
  │                          │ as code, GitOps approvals, image verify   │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 5: RUNTIME        │ Pod Security (non-root, read-only FS),    │
  │                          │ seccomp, AppArmor/SELinux, Falco          │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 6: NETWORK        │ Network Policies (micro-segmentation),    │
  │                          │ mTLS (service mesh), API gateway, DNS     │
  │  ────────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────│
  │  Layer 7: DATA           │ Encryption at rest/in transit, secret     │
  │                          │ rotation, access audit, data residency    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Platform Security (Domain 2, 20%)

Security is 20% of the exam — tied for second-highest weight. Must-know facts: (1) 4 Cs of Cloud Native Security: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code — security at every layer. (2) OPA (Rego) vs Kyverno (YAML) — this distinction is tested. OPA uses Rego (general purpose), Kyverno uses YAML (K8s-native). (3) Admission Controllers — Mutating (modify resources before creation) vs Validating (allow/deny). Kyverno can do both. (4) Falco = runtime threat detection (eBPF-based), Trivy = image vulnerability scanner, Cosign = image signing/verification. (5) Pod Security — non-root user, read-only filesystem, seccomp profiles, AppArmor/SELinux. (6) Vault = dynamic secrets (temporary, auto-rotated) vs K8s Secrets = static (base64-encoded, not encrypted by default).

17.2 Kubernetes RBAC — Who Can Do What

RBAC (Role-Based Access Control) controls who (Subject) can do what (Verb) on which resources in Kubernetes. It's your first and most important security control.

📐 RBAC Hierarchy

Subject → RoleBinding → Role → Permissions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  👤 SUBJECT                    🔗 BINDING              🎭 ROLE       │
  │  (Who?)                       (Connects)             (What?)         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐              ┌──────────────┐       ┌──────────────┐  │
  │  │ User:    │              │RoleBinding:  │       │ Role:        │  │
  │  │ alice    │─────────────▶│anihpj-dev    │──────▶│anihpj-dev    │  │
  │  └──────────┘              │              │       │              │  │
  │  ┌──────────┐              │Subjects:     │       │Verbs:        │  │
  │  │ Group:   │              │- alice       │       │get, list,    │  │
  │  │ dev-team │              │- dev-team    │       │create, update│  │
  │  └──────────┘              │              │       │              │  │
  │  ┌──────────┐              │RoleRef:      │       │Resources:    │  │
  │  │ Service  │              │anihpj-dev    │       │pods,deploy,  │  │
  │  │ Account  │              └──────────────┘       │svc,configmaps│  │
  │  └──────────┘                                     └──────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

📝 RBAC for anihpj — Three Roles

anihpj-rbac.yaml
# Developer: edit pods, deployments, services in dev namespace
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata: { name: anihpj-developer, namespace: anihpj-dev }
rules:
  - apiGroups: ["", "apps"]
    resources: ["pods", "deployments", "services", "configmaps"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "create", "update", "patch", "delete"]
---
# Viewer: read-only across all anihpj namespaces
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRole
metadata: { name: anihpj-viewer }
rules:
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["pods", "services", "nodes"]
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
---
# Admin: full control, cannot delete cluster
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: ClusterRole
metadata: { name: anihpj-admin }
rules:
  - apiGroups: ["*"]
    resources: ["*"]
    verbs: ["*"]
  - apiGroups: [""]
    resources: ["nodes"]
    verbs: ["get", "list"]  # No delete!
💡 Aggregated ClusterRoles

Kubernetes includes built-in aggregated roles: admin (full namespace control), edit (modify most resources), and view (read-only). The edit ClusterRole aggregates permissions for pods, deployments, services, configmaps, secrets, and more — perfect for anihpj-developer. Create custom aggregations with aggregationRule to build roles from reusable building blocks instead of from scratch.

17.3 Pod Security Standards (PSS) and Pod Security Admission (PSA)

Pod Security Admission (PSA) is the built-in Kubernetes controller that enforces Pod Security Standards at the namespace level. It replaced PodSecurityPolicy (PSP) in K8s 1.25.

LevelDescriptionAllowsBlocks
🔴 PrivilegedUnrestricted — for system podsEverything (hostPath, hostNetwork, privileged)Nothing
🟡 BaselineMinimal restrictions — for most appsStandard podshostPath, hostNetwork, privileged containers
🟢 RestrictedHardened — for productionNon-root, read-only FS, no privilege escalationRoot containers, writable root FS, host mounts
Namespace labels for PSS
# Enforce Restricted on production namespaces
kubectl label ns anihpj-prod \
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted \
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit=restricted \
  pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted

17.4 Network Policies — Micro-Segmentation

By default, all pods in Kubernetes can communicate with each other. Network Policies implement micro-segmentation — you define exactly which traffic is allowed, and everything else is denied.

NetworkPolicy — anihpj 3-tier
# Default deny all ingress in production
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: deny-all, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  podSelector: {}
  policyTypes: [Ingress]
---
# Allow frontend → anihpj API
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: allow-frontend, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: anihpj } }
  policyTypes: [Ingress]
  ingress:
    - from:
        - podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: frontend } }
      ports: [{ port: 8000 }]
---
# Allow anihpj → PostgreSQL
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: allow-db, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: anihpj } }
  policyTypes: [Egress]
  egress:
    - to:
        - podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: postgres } }
      ports: [{ port: 5432 }]

17.5 Secrets Management for Platform Teams

Kubernetes Secrets are base64-encoded, NOT encrypted by default. Platform teams must implement proper secret management. Here are your options:

ApproachSecurity LevelComplexityBest For
K8s Secrets (default)🔴 Low (base64 only)🟢 LowDev environments only
etcd Encryption at Rest🟡 Medium🟡 MediumBasic production security
Sealed Secrets🟡 Medium🟢 LowGitOps-safe secrets in Git
External Secrets Operator🟢 High🟡 MediumSync from cloud providers
HashiCorp Vault🟢 Very High🔴 HighDynamic secrets, rotation, audit

📝 ExternalSecret for anihpj Database Password

ExternalSecret YAML
apiVersion: external-secrets.io/v1beta1
kind: ExternalSecret
metadata:
  name: anihpj-db-password
  namespace: anihpj-prod
spec:
  refreshInterval: 1h
  secretStoreRef:
    name: aws-secretsmanager
    kind: SecretStore
  target:
    name: anihpj-db-credentials
  data:
    - secretKey: password
      remoteRef:
        key: prod/anihpj/database
        property: password

🏦 HashiCorp Vault — Dynamic Secrets

Vault Architecture for anihpj
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ① Auth via K8s SA     ┌──────────────┐             │
  │  │ anihpj   │─────────────────────────▶│   🔐 VAULT   │             │
  │  │ Pod      │                          │              │             │
  │  │          │◀─────────────────────────│ • Auth: K8s  │             │
  │  │ Vault    │  ② Issue short-lived     │ • Secrets:   │             │
  │  │ Agent    │     DB credentials        │   DB engine  │             │
  │  │ (sidecar)│     (TTL: 1 hour)         │ • Policies   │             │
  │  └──────────┘                          │ • Audit Log  │             │
  │                                        └──────┬───────┘             │
  │                                               │                     │
  │                                      ③ Create ephemeral DB user      │
  │                                               │                     │
  │                                               ▼                     │
  │                                      ┌──────────────┐              │
  │                                      │  PostgreSQL  │              │
  │                                      │  (RDS)       │              │
  │                                      └──────────────┘              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Vault creates TEMPORARY credentials. After 1 hour, they EXPIRE.  │
  │     No long-lived passwords to steal, rotate, or manage.              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

✅ Chapter 17 Summary — Platform Security Fundamentals

You've built the security foundation every platform needs: Defense in Depth (multiple security layers, no single point of failure), the 4 Cs of cloud native security (Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code), RBAC for least-privilege access, policy engines (OPA/Kyverno) for automated enforcement, secrets management with Vault, and runtime security with Falco. You now understand that security is not added after the fact — it's baked into the platform as default. For the CNPA exam, Domain 2 (20%) tests exactly these concepts: RBAC structure, policy enforcement (OPA vs Kyverno), secret rotation, and the shared responsibility model.

🔒 Key Takeaway 1

4 Cs: Cloud → Cluster → Container → Code. Security must be addressed at EVERY layer. A secure container on an insecure cluster is still insecure.

🔒 Key Takeaway 2

OPA (Rego) vs Kyverno (YAML): both enforce policies. OPA is general-purpose, Kyverno is K8s-native. Both appear on the CNPA exam.

🔒 Key Takeaway 3

Dynamic secrets (Vault) > static secrets (K8s Secrets). Temporary, auto-rotated credentials eliminate the #1 security risk: leaked long-lived passwords.

Chapter 18: Supply Chain Security — Securing the Pipeline

Your CI/CD pipeline is a high-value attack target. If an attacker compromises your pipeline, they can inject malicious code into every application on your platform. Supply chain security is about securing every step from code to production.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 🔗 Supply Chain 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ The growing threat of software supply chain attacks
  • ✅ SLSA framework — Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts
  • ✅ SBOMs — Software Bill of Materials and why they matter
  • ✅ Policy as Code with OPA/Gatekeeper and Kyverno
  • ✅ Hands-on: Secure the anihpj supply chain end-to-end

18.1 Software Supply Chain Attacks — The Growing Threat

Supply chain attacks target the tools and processes used to build software — not the software itself. If an attacker compromises your CI pipeline, every image it produces is compromised.

Major Supply Chain Attacks — Timeline
  2020 ─── SolarWinds ────── Backdoor in build system → 18,000 orgs affected
  2021 ─── Codecov ───────── Compromised bash uploader → stole CI secrets
  2021 ─── Log4Shell ─────── Vulnerability in ubiquitous logging library
  2024 ─── xz utils ──────── Backdoor planted over 2 years by trusted maintainer

  💡 LESSON: Your platform amplifies both security AND insecurity.
     If your CI pipeline is compromised, every anihpj service is at risk.
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know the key supply chain threats: (1) Compromised dependencies (Log4Shell), (2) CI/CD pipeline injection (SolarWinds-style), (3) Poisoned images in registries, (4) Insider threats from compromised maintainers (xz utils). The exam tests your ability to identify mitigation strategies at each stage.

18.2 SLSA Framework — Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts

SLSA (Supply-chain Levels for Software Artifacts, pronounced "salsa") is a framework from Google/OpenSSF that defines increasing levels of build integrity.

LevelNameRequirementsGuarantees
🔴 L1ProvenanceBuild is scripted; provenance shows how artifact was builtEasy error identification
🟡 L2Hosted BuildBuild runs on hosted platform (GitHub Actions); signed provenanceTamper resistance
🟢 L3Hardened BuildIsolated, ephemeral, hermetic builds; non-falsifiable provenanceHigh confidence in source↔artifact mapping
🔵 L4MaximumTwo-person review of all changes + hermetic, reproducible buildsHighest integrity guarantee

For anihpj: SLSA L2 is immediately achievable with GitHub Actions + signed provenance. L3 requires BuildKit with hermetic builds.

18.3 SBOM — Software Bill of Materials

An SBOM is a machine-readable inventory of every component in your software — like a nutrition label. When a vulnerability hits, you can query your SBOMs and know in minutes which services are affected. US Executive Order 14028 now mandates SBOMs for all software sold to the federal government — making SBOMs a compliance requirement, not just best practice.

Generate SBOM for anihpj image
# Generate SBOM with Syft (formats: SPDX, CycloneDX)
syft ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:latest -o cyclonedx-json > anihpj-sbom.json

# Attach SBOM to image in registry
cosign attach sbom --sbom anihpj-sbom.json ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:latest

# Query SBOM for vulnerable component
grype sbom:anihpj-sbom.json | grep -i "log4j"
# → Nothing found? Your anihpj is safe. If found → patch immediately.

18.4 Policy as Code — OPA and Kyverno

Policy as Code means defining security rules as version-controlled code — not wiki pages or email threads. Two leading tools: OPA/Gatekeeper (Rego language) and Kyverno (YAML-based, Kubernetes-native).

FeatureKyvernoOPA/Gatekeeper
Policy LanguageYAML (K8s-native)Rego (purpose-built)
Learning Curve🟢 Easy🔴 Steep (Rego is unique)
Mutation✅ Built-in❌ Via separate component
Generation✅ Auto-generate resources❌ Not supported
Best ForK8s-only shops, quick setupMulti-system policy, complex logic
Kyverno — Block :latest tag + Require liveness probe
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata: { name: block-latest-tag }
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: validate-image-tag
      match:
        resources: { kinds: [Pod] }
      validate:
        message: "Using :latest tag is prohibited"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - image: "!*:latest"   # Reject :latest
---
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata: { name: require-probes }
spec:
  rules:
    - name: require-liveness-readiness
      match:
        resources: { kinds: [Deployment] }
      validate:
        message: "Liveness and readiness probes are required"
        pattern:
          spec:
            template:
              spec:
                containers:
                  - livenessProbe: "?*"     # Must exist
                    readinessProbe: "?*"

18.5 Hands-On: Secure the anihpj Supply Chain

1
Scan in CI

Add Trivy scan to CI pipeline: trivy image ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:$GITHUB_SHA

2
Generate SBOM

syft ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:latest -o spdx-json — attach with Cosign

3
Sign Image

cosign sign --yes ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj@$DIGEST (keyless via OIDC)

4
Verify at Deploy

Kyverno policy: only signed images can be deployed

5
Block :latest

Kyverno policy: reject any pod using :latest tag

6
Test Unsigned

Deploy unsigned image → Kyverno blocks it! ✅

7
Test Signed

Deploy signed image → Kyverno allows it! ✅
