Chapter 19: Compliance, Audit, and Governance for Platforms

Compliance isn't just about checking boxes for auditors — it's about proving your platform is secure by design. This chapter covers the compliance frameworks, audit logging, and automation that make governance a continuous process, not a last-minute scramble.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1 hour 📋 Compliance 🎯 Domain 2: 20%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Key compliance frameworks and their platform implications
  • ✅ Kubernetes audit logging — who did what and when
  • ✅ Compliance automation — policy as code for governance
  • ✅ Hands-on: Implement compliance controls for anihpj

19.1 Compliance Frameworks Every Platform Engineer Should Know

FrameworkDomainKey Platform Requirements
SOC 2General SaaSAudit trails, access controls, encryption, change management
PCI DSSPayment cardsNetwork segmentation, encryption, vulnerability scanning, access logging
HIPAAHealthcareData encryption, access controls, audit logs, BAAs with vendors
GDPREU data protectionData residency, right to deletion, breach notification (72h)
FedRAMPUS GovernmentContinuous monitoring, FIPS 140-2 encryption, strict access controls
📖 Platform Implication

You don't implement compliance per-app — you build compliance controls INTO the platform so every service inherits them. Audit logging, encryption, access controls, and policy enforcement are platform-level concerns. If done right, individual service teams don't need to think about SOC 2 — the platform handles it.

19.2 Kubernetes Audit Logging

Kubernetes audit logging records every API request made to the API server — answering "Who deleted that production deployment at 2 AM?" with forensic precision.

📝 Audit Policy Configuration

audit-policy.yaml
apiVersion: audit.k8s.io/v1
kind: Policy
rules:
  # Log ALL request metadata for Secrets (never log body!)
  - level: Metadata
    resources: [{ group: "", resources: ["secrets"] }]

  # Log request + response body for critical changes
  - level: RequestResponse
    resources:
      - { group: "", resources: ["pods"] }
      - { group: "apps", resources: ["deployments"] }
    verbs: ["create", "update", "patch", "delete"]

  # Log metadata only for reads (reduces log volume)
  - level: Metadata
    verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]

  # Don't log health checks (noisy!)
  - level: None
    userGroups: ["system:serviceaccounts:kube-system"]
Audit Log Flow
  kubectl delete pod anihpj-prod-abc --namespace anihpj-prod
       │
       ▼
  ┌─────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
  │ API Server  │───▶│ Audit Backend│───▶│    SIEM      │
  │             │    │ (Log File /  │    │ (Splunk/ELK/ │
  │ Stage:      │    │  Webhook)   │    │  Datadog)    │
  │ ResponseComp│    └──────────────┘    └──────────────┘
  └─────────────┘
  Audit record:
  { "user": "alice", "verb": "delete", "resource": "pods",
    "name": "anihpj-prod-abc", "namespace": "anihpj-prod",
    "timestamp": "2026-06-05T02:15:33Z", "sourceIP": "10.0.1.5" }

19.3 Compliance Automation

Manual compliance checks are slow, error-prone, and done once a year. Compliance as Code means defining rules in Git, enforcing via policy engines, and checking continuously — not just before audits.

🔄 Continuous Compliance Pipeline

Compliance as Code Workflow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① DEFINE              ② ENFORCE             ③ VERIFY               │
  │  ┌──────────┐         ┌──────────┐          ┌──────────┐            │
  │  │Compliance│  ───▶   │ Policy   │  ───▶   │Continuous│            │
  │  │Rules in  │         │ Engine   │          │Audit     │            │
  │  │Git (OPA/ │         │ enforces │          │Reports   │            │
  │  │Kyverno)  │         │ in cluster│         │generated │            │
  │  └──────────┘         └──────────┘          └──────────┘            │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Every change to compliance rules is peer-reviewed via PR.        │
  │     Every deployment is checked against current rules.               │
  │     Audit reports are generated automatically, not manually.         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Kyverno — Compliance: Only approved registries
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata: { name: approved-registries }
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: check-registry
      match:
        resources: { kinds: [Pod] }
      validate:
        message: "Images must come from approved registries: ghcr.io, ecr.aws"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - image: "ghcr.io/* | *.ecr.aws/* | *.dkr.ecr.*.amazonaws.com/*"

19.4 Hands-On: Implement Compliance Controls for anihpj

1
Enable Audit Logging

Configure audit policy with different levels per resource type

2
Deploy Falco

Runtime threat detection: alerts on unexpected shell spawns, file changes

3
Create Compliance Policies

Kyverno policies for PCI-DSS subset: approved registries, encrypted storage, RBAC

4
Generate Compliance Report

kyverno test — automated compliance checking against policies

5
Simulate Audit

Check audit logs: "Who deleted pod anihpj-prod-abc?" → alice at 2:15 AM

6
Compliance Dashboard

Grafana dashboard showing compliance status, policy violations, audit events

📋 Chapter 19 Summary — Compliance & Governance

📋
Frameworks

SOC 2, PCI DSS, HIPAA, GDPR, FedRAMP. Platform handles compliance so services don't have to. Audit trails, encryption, access controls built in.

📝
Audit Logging

Records every K8s API request. Audit policy controls what's logged. Stages: RequestReceived→ResponseStarted→ResponseComplete. Exported to SIEM.

🔄
Automation

Compliance as Code: rules in Git, enforced by Kyverno/OPA/Falco. Continuous compliance checks, automated reports. No last-minute audit panic.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Compliance & Governance (Domain 2, 20%)

The CNPA exam tests compliance understanding: (1) Compliance as Code — policy rules defined in Git, enforced automatically by Kyverno/OPA. (2) Audit logging — K8s audit policy records every API request. Know the three stages: RequestReceived → ResponseStarted → ResponseComplete. (3) Key frameworks: SOC 2 (service org controls), PCI DSS (payment card data), HIPAA (healthcare data), GDPR (EU data privacy), FedRAMP (US government cloud). (4) SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) is becoming mandatory for compliance — it lists every component in your software. (5) Separation of duties: Developers deploy code, platform team manages infrastructure, security team audits — all enforced by RBAC and admission control.

🔴 Part 7: Multi-Cloud, Hybrid & Edge Platform Strategies Chapters 20-22

Chapter 20: Managed Kubernetes Services — EKS, GKE, AKS

Most enterprise platforms run on managed Kubernetes. This chapter compares the big three — EKS, GKE, and AKS — and helps you build a multi-cloud strategy that leverages each provider's strengths without lock-in.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours ☁️ Cloud 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Managed vs self-managed K8s — tradeoffs and when to choose each
  • ✅ EKS, GKE, and AKS — architectures, key integrations, CLI tools
  • ✅ Serverless K8s: GKE Autopilot, AKS Automatic, EKS Fargate
  • ✅ Building a multi-cloud platform strategy

20.1 Why Managed Kubernetes?

🔧
Self-Managed

Full control over control plane, etcd, upgrades, HA. Maximum flexibility but YOU handle: API server scaling, etcd backups, control plane upgrades, security patches. Best for: specialized hardware, air-gapped environments.

☁️
Managed

Cloud provider runs the control plane (often free). You manage worker nodes and workloads. Automatic upgrades, integrated with cloud IAM, logging, monitoring. Best for: 95% of enterprise platforms, including anihpj.

DimensionSelf-ManagedEKSGKEAKS
Control Plane Cost$ (your infra)$72/moFreeFree
UpgradesManual (kubeadm)Managed (1-click)Auto (release channels)Auto (maintenance windows)
Serverless OptionN/AFargateAutopilotAutomatic
IAM IntegrationManualIAM Roles for SAWorkload IdentityAzure AD + Pod Identity
Fleet ManagementManualEKS AnywhereFleet (multi-cluster)Azure Arc

20.2 Amazon EKS (Elastic Kubernetes Service)

AWS's managed K8s: control plane runs in AWS-managed VPC, worker nodes on EC2 or Fargate. eksctl is the CLI for creating and managing EKS clusters (think kubeadm for EKS).

eksctl — Create anihpj cluster
# eksctl cluster config for anihpj
apiVersion: eksctl.io/v1alpha5
kind: ClusterConfig
metadata:
  name: anihpj-prod
  region: us-east-1
managedNodeGroups:
  - name: platform
    instanceType: t3.large
    desiredCapacity: 3
    iam:
      withAddonPolicies:
        ebs: true          # EBS CSI driver for persistent volumes
        albIngress: true   # ALB Ingress Controller

20.3 Google GKE (Google Kubernetes Engine)

The original managed K8s (since 2015). GKE Autopilot is the game-changer: Google manages everything — control plane AND nodes. You only pay for pod resources (vCPU, memory, storage). No node management at all.

GKE Autopilot — Serverless Kubernetes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GKE AUTOPILOT — You deploy pods, Google manages everything else     │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                          │
  │  │ anihpj   │  │ jobpost  │  │ skills-  │  ← You: define pods       │
  │  │ Pod      │  │ Pod      │  │ svc Pod  │     with resource requests │
  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  Google: automatically provisions nodes, scales, patches,     │   │
  │  │  upgrades control plane AND nodes, manages security           │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 PRICING: Pay per pod (vCPU/memory/GB-hour), NOT per node.       │
  │     No more right-sizing node pools!                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
GKE Autopilot creation
gcloud container clusters create-auto anihpj-prod \
  --region us-central1 \
  --workload-pool=anihpj.svc.id.goog  # Workload Identity

20.4 Azure AKS (Azure Kubernetes Service)

Microsoft's managed K8s with free control plane. AKS Automatic is Azure's answer to GKE Autopilot — managed nodes, auto-scaling, auto-upgrades. Key integration: Azure AD for RBAC, Key Vault CSI for secrets, Azure Policy for governance.

AKS creation
az aks create --resource-group anihpj-rg \
  --name anihpj-prod \
  --enable-managed-identity \
  --enable-azure-keyvault-secrets-provider \
  --node-count 3

20.5 Multi-Cloud Platform Strategy

Multi-cloud isn't about running everything everywhere — it's about leveraging each provider's strengths while maintaining a consistent platform experience for developers.

Unified Platform Across Clouds
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🏗️ UNIFIED PLATFORM CONTROL PLANE                 │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐               │
  │  │Backstage │ │  ArgoCD  │ │Crossplane│ │ClusterAPI│               │
  │  │(Catalog) │ │ (GitOps) │ │  (IaC)   │ │(Clusters) │               │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘               │
  └──────────┬───────────────┬───────────────┬──────────────────────────┘
             │               │               │
             ▼               ▼               ▼
  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐
  │   AWS EKS    │ │  Google GKE  │ │  Azure AKS   │
  │  us-east-1   │ │  us-central1 │ │  eastus2     │
  │  RDS + S3    │ │  Cloud SQL   │ │  CosmosDB    │
  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘

  💡 Developers see: "Deploy to production" (platform chooses best cloud)
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know the managed K8s differentiators: GKE Autopilot (serverless, pay-per-pod), EKS Fargate (serverless pods, no nodes), AKS Automatic (managed nodes). The exam asks: "Which managed K8s offers free control plane and serverless pods?" Answer: GKE Autopilot (free control plane + serverless). "Which is best for Azure AD integration?" Answer: AKS.

✅ Chapter 20 Summary — Managed Kubernetes Services

You've compared the big three managed K8s services: GKE (pioneer, Autopilot for serverless), EKS (AWS-native, Fargate for serverless pods), and AKS (Azure-native, best AD integration). You understand the shared responsibility model (cloud provider manages control plane, YOU manage worker nodes and workloads), and the multi-cloud strategy of using a unified control plane (Backstage + ArgoCD + Crossplane + Cluster API) to give developers a single interface across clouds. For CNPA Domain 4, know the key differentiators: free control plane (GKE), best IAM integration (EKS + IRSA), best AD integration (AKS).

☁️ Key Takeaway 1

GKE Autopilot = serverless K8s (pay per pod, no node management). EKS Fargate = serverless pods on AWS. AKS Automatic = managed nodes on Azure.

☁️ Key Takeaway 2

Shared responsibility: cloud provider = control plane (API server, etcd, scheduler). You = worker nodes, workloads, networking, security policies.

☁️ Key Takeaway 3

Multi-cloud = NOT running everything everywhere. It's leveraging each cloud's strengths while maintaining a consistent developer experience via your platform.

Chapter 21: Crossplane — Kubernetes-Native Infrastructure Management

Crossplane turns Kubernetes into a universal control plane for infrastructure. Instead of Terraform scripts, you define cloud resources as Kubernetes YAML — and manage them with kubectl, GitOps, and all your existing K8s tooling.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours ✈️ Crossplane 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ Crossplane architecture: XRDs, Compositions, Managed Resources
  • ✅ Crossplane vs Terraform — when to use each
  • ✅ Building a self-service Platform API for anihpj with Crossplane

21.1 What is Crossplane? — The Cloud Control Plane

Crossplane extends Kubernetes with Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs) that represent cloud resources. You define your own platform APIs (e.g., "PostgreSQL database") and Crossplane provisions the actual cloud resources (RDS, Cloud SQL, etc.) behind the scenes.

Crossplane — Developer Request → Cloud Resource
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ① Developer: "I need a PostgreSQL database"                         │
  │     kubectl apply -f my-database.yaml                                │
  │     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐              │
  │     │ apiVersion: platform.anihpj.io/v1alpha1          │              │
  │     │ kind: PostgreSQL                                 │              │
  │     │ spec: { size: small, version: "14" }            │              │
  │     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘              │
  │                             │                                        │
  │                             ▼                                        │
  │  ② Crossplane XRD + Composition (Platform-defined template)          │
  │     ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐              │
  │     │ Composition maps PostgreSQL →                   │              │
  │     │   • AWS RDS Instance (db.t3.small)              │              │
  │     │   • Security Group (allow 5432)                 │              │
  │     │   • K8s Secret (connection string)              │              │
  │     └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘              │
  │                             │                                        │
  │                             ▼                                        │
  │  ③ Cloud resources provisioned automatically                         │
  │     ✅ RDS instance created + Security Group + K8s Secret injected    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

21.2 Crossplane vs Terraform — When to Use Which

DimensionTerraformCrossplane
ApproachDeclarative HCL, state fileK8s-native, continuous reconciliation
Control LoopPlan → Apply (discrete)Continuous (K8s reconciliation loop)
Self-ServiceLimited (via Terraform Cloud)Built-in (kubectl, GitOps, Backstage)
Drift DetectionTerraform plan (on demand)Continuous (every reconciler loop)
Best ForInitial provisioning, Day 0Ongoing management, Day 1+, self-service
ComplementTerraform provisions the K8s clusterCrossplane manages everything inside + cloud resources

21.3 Building a Platform API with Crossplane for anihpj

1
Install Crossplane

helm install crossplane crossplane-stable/crossplane -n crossplane-system

2
Install AWS Provider

Apply Provider + ProviderConfig with cloud credentials

3
Define XRD

Create CompositeResourceDefinition for "Database" type

4
Define Composition

Map "Database" → RDS Instance + SecurityGroup + K8s Secret

5
Developer Self-Service

kubectl apply -f my-db.yaml → Crossplane provisions everything!

📝 Crossplane Composition YAML

Database Composition for anihpj
apiVersion: apiextensions.crossplane.io/v1
kind: Composition
metadata: { name: postgresql.anihpj.io }
spec:
  compositeTypeRef:
    apiVersion: platform.anihpj.io/v1alpha1
    kind: XPostgreSQL
  resources:
    - name: rds-instance
      base:
        apiVersion: rds.aws.upbound.io/v1beta1
        kind: Instance
        spec:
          forProvider:
            instanceClass: db.t3.small
            engine: postgres
            engineVersion: "14"
            region: us-east-1
    - name: connection-secret
      base:
        apiVersion: kubernetes.crossplane.io/v1alpha1
        kind: Object
        spec:
          forProvider:
            manifest:
              apiVersion: v1
              kind: Secret
              metadata: { namespace: anihpj-prod }
              data:
                endpoint: "rds-instance.endpoint"

✅ Chapter 21 Summary — Crossplane — K8s-Native Infrastructure

You've mastered Crossplane, the Kubernetes-native infrastructure management tool. Unlike Terraform (which runs outside K8s in CI/CD), Crossplane runs inside your cluster and uses CRDs to represent cloud resources. Key concepts: XRD (Composite Resource Definition — your custom API), Composition (the blueprint that maps your API to cloud resources), Managed Resources (the actual cloud resources Crossplane creates). For the anihpj platform, a developer creates an XPostgreSQL YAML, Crossplane provisions an RDS instance + Kubernetes Secret — no Terraform knowledge needed. This is the essence of Domain 4: self-service infrastructure APIs.

✈️ Key Takeaway 1

Crossplane = K8s-native IaC. Terraform = external IaC. Crossplane runs reconciliation loops inside K8s, uses CRDs, and fits GitOps natively.

✈️ Key Takeaway 2

XRD = define your API (e.g., XPostgreSQL). Composition = define how to provision it. Managed Resource = the actual cloud resource created.

✈️ Key Takeaway 3

Crossplane enables true developer self-service: dev writes YAML, platform defines what that YAML provisions. No tickets, no wait time, no infra expertise needed.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Crossplane & Platform APIs (Domain 4, 12%)

Key CNPA facts: (1) Reconciliation loop: Observe → Diff → Act → Repeat — Crossplane's controllers continuously reconcile desired state (CRD) with actual state (cloud). (2) XRD vs Composition vs Managed Resource: XRD = your custom API (what devs see), Composition = blueprint (how to provision), Managed Resource = actual cloud resource. (3) Crossplane vs Terraform: Crossplane runs INSIDE K8s (CRDs, GitOps-native). Terraform runs OUTSIDE K8s (HCL, CI/CD). (4) ProviderConfig: Connects Crossplane to a cloud account (AWS IAM, GCP SA, Azure SP). (5) Self-service pattern: Developer creates claim → Crossplane provisions → connection Secret auto-created → app uses Secret.

Chapter 22: Hybrid and Edge Platform Engineering

Not everything runs in a public cloud. This chapter covers hybrid cloud patterns, GitOps for edge computing, Cluster API for declarative cluster management, and platform federation — managing many clusters as one.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1 hour 🌐 Hybrid 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

22.1 Hybrid Cloud Patterns

Hybrid cloud combines on-premises infrastructure with public cloud — driven by data sovereignty, latency requirements, existing data center investments, or burst-to-cloud capacity needs.

Hybrid Platform Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🏗️ UNIFIED PLATFORM CONTROL PLANE (Cloud-Hosted)        │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐               │
  │  │ Git Repos│ │Backstage │ │  ArgoCD  │ │ Grafana  │               │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘               │
  └────────┬──────────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┘
           │                              │
           ▼                              ▼
  ┌──────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────┐
  │  🏢 ON-PREM DC   │        │  ☁️ AWS CLOUD     │
  │  ┌────────────┐  │        │  ┌────────────┐  │
  │  │ K8s Cluster│  │  VPN/  │  │ EKS Cluster│  │
  │  │ (Bare Metal)│◀─┼─DC─────┼─▶│ (us-east-1)│  │
  │  │            │  │        │  │            │  │
  │  │ • Sensitive│  │        │  │ • Web tier │  │
  │  │   data     │  │        │  │ • Burst    │  │
  │  │ • Low      │  │        │  │   capacity │  │
  │  │   latency  │  │        │  │ • DR site  │  │
  │  └────────────┘  │        │  └────────────┘  │
  └──────────────────┘        └──────────────────┘

22.2 GitOps for Edge Computing

Edge computing presents unique challenges: intermittent connectivity, resource constraints, and massive scale (hundreds or thousands of locations). GitOps is the ideal management pattern for edge because each cluster reconciles independently from Git — no constant connection to a central control plane needed.

Edge GitOps — Central Git → 1000 Edge Clusters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │                      📁 CENTRAL GIT REPO                             │
  │                   (Single source of truth)                           │
  │                            │                                         │
  │          ┌─────────────────┼─────────────────┐                      │
  │          │                 │                 │                      │
  │          ▼                 ▼                 ▼                      │
  │   ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐  ┌─────────────┐                │
  │   │ 🏪 Store #1  │  │ 🏪 Store #2  │  │ 🏪 Store #N  │   (1000 stores)│
  │   │ ┌─────────┐ │  │ ┌─────────┐ │  │ ┌─────────┐ │                │
  │   │ │Flux     │ │  │ │Flux     │ │  │ │Flux     │ │                │
  │   │ │reconcil │ │  │ │reconcil │ │  │ │reconcil │ │                │
  │   │ │periodic │ │  │ │periodic │ │  │ │periodic │ │                │
  │   │ └─────────┘ │  │ └─────────┘ │  │ └─────────┘ │                │
  │   └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘  └─────────────┘                │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 Edge connects when it can, reconciles locally, works offline.    │
  │     No central server dependency. Flux (lighter) > ArgoCD for edge.  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

22.3 Cluster API — Declarative Cluster Lifecycle Management

Cluster API (CAPI) manages the lifecycle of Kubernetes clusters — using Kubernetes itself. Think of it as "Deployments for clusters": define a desired cluster spec, and CAPI creates, scales, upgrades, and deletes clusters across any infrastructure provider.

🏗️ Cluster API Architecture

Management Cluster → Workload Clusters
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🎮 MANAGEMENT CLUSTER (runs Cluster API controllers)         │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐              │   │
  │  │  │ Cluster    │  │ AWS        │  │ Docker     │              │   │
  │  │  │ API Core   │  │ Provider   │  │ Provider   │   ← CAPI     │   │
  │  │  │ Controller │  │ (CAPA)     │  │ (CAPD)     │    Providers │   │
  │  │  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────┘              │   │
  │  └──────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                 │ provision & manage                                  │
  │                 ▼                                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  ☸️ WORKLOAD CLUSTERS (where your apps run)                   │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐  ┌──────────────┐        │   │
  │  │  │ EKS Cluster  │  │ vSphere      │  │ Kind/Docker  │        │   │
  │  │  │ (AWS)        │  │ (On-Prem)    │  │ (Local Dev)  │        │   │
  │  │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐ │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐ │  │ ┌──┐┌──┐┌──┐ │        │   │
  │  │  │ │P1││P2││P3│ │  │ │P1││P2││P3│ │  │ │P1││P2││P3│ │        │   │
  │  │  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘  └──────────────┘        │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 One management cluster provisions clusters on ANY infrastructure.│
  │     ClusterClass = template for cluster (like Deployment for pods).  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Cluster API — EKS cluster provisioning
apiVersion: cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta1
kind: Cluster
metadata: { name: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  clusterNetwork:
    pods: { cidrBlocks: ["192.168.0.0/16"] }
  infrastructureRef:
    apiVersion: infrastructure.cluster.x-k8s.io/v1beta2
    kind: AWSManagedCluster
    name: anihpj-prod
  controlPlaneRef:
    kind: AWSManagedControlPlane
    name: anihpj-prod-control-plane

22.4 Platform Federation — Managing Many Clusters as One

When you have dozens or hundreds of clusters, you need a strategy for managing them as a unified platform — not as individual snowflakes.

ApproachHow It WorksBest For
ArgoCD Multi-ClusterOne ArgoCD instance deploys to many clustersGitOps across 10-100 clusters
CrossplaneProvision cloud resources across providersInfrastructure across clouds
Cluster APIDeclarative cluster lifecycle managementCluster provisioning at scale
Service Mesh FederationConnect services across clusters (Istio multicluster)Cross-cluster service communication

📋 Part 7 Summary — Multi-Cloud, Hybrid & Edge

☁️
Managed K8s

EKS (AWS), GKE (Google — Autopilot), AKS (Azure). Managed control planes, integrated IAM, serverless options. GKE Autopilot = pay per pod.

✈️
Crossplane

K8s-native IaC. XRDs define custom APIs, Compositions map to cloud resources. Build self-service platform APIs with kubectl.

🌐
Hybrid & Edge

GitOps ideal for edge (Flux for lightweight reconciliation). Cluster API for cluster lifecycle. Federation via ArgoCD/Crossplane/Service Mesh.

🔴 Part 8: Service Mesh & API Gateways Chapters 23-24

Chapter 23: Service Mesh Concepts and Architecture

A service mesh is an infrastructure layer for service-to-service communication. It moves reliability, security, and observability from application code into the platform — giving you mTLS, retries, circuit breaking, and traffic splitting without developers writing a single line of code.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~2 hours 🕸️ Service Mesh 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ What a service mesh is and why platforms need one
  • ✅ Istio (Envoy-based) vs Linkerd (Rust micro-proxy) — when to use each
  • ✅ Traffic management: canary, A/B testing, circuit breaking, retries, timeouts, fault injection
  • ✅ Zero-code observability with service mesh

23.1 What is a Service Mesh and Why Do Platforms Need One?

A service mesh is an infrastructure layer that handles service-to-service communication. It consists of a data plane (sidecar proxies next to each pod) and a control plane (manages configuration and policy). The key insight: reliability moves from app code → platform layer.

Service Mesh — Before & After
  ❌ WITHOUT SERVICE MESH:
  ┌─────────┐    direct HTTP     ┌─────────┐
  │ Service A│──────────────────▶│ Service B│
  │ (anihpj)│    no encryption   │ (auth)   │
  └─────────┘                    └─────────┘
  • No mTLS between services • Retries in app code • No circuit breaking

  ✅ WITH SERVICE MESH (Istio/Linkerd):
  ┌─────────┐      mTLS        ┌─────────┐
  │ anihpj  │                  │  auth   │
  │ ┌─────┐ │  ┌──────────┐   │ ┌─────┐ │
  │ │ app │─┼─▶│ sidecar  │───┼▶│ app │ │
  │ └─────┘ │  │ (Envoy/  │   │ └─────┘ │
  └─────────┘  │ linkerd) │   └─────────┘
               └────┬─────┘
                    │
               ┌────┴────┐
               │ Control │  ← Policy, mTLS certs, telemetry config
               │ Plane   │
               └─────────┘
  ✅ Auto mTLS ✅ Built-in retries/circuit breaking ✅ Traffic splitting

23.2 Istio — The Most Popular Service Mesh

Istio uses Envoy as its sidecar proxy and Istiod as its control plane. Key CRDs: VirtualService (routing rules), DestinationRule (load balancing, connection pools, mTLS), PeerAuthentication (enforce mTLS STRICT or PERMISSIVE).

Istio Architecture — Control Plane + Data Plane
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        ☸️ KUBERNETES CLUSTER                         │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🧠 ISTIOD (Control Plane)                                     │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                    │   │
  │  │  │ Pilot    │  │ Citadel  │  │ Galley   │                    │   │
  │  │  │(routing) │  │(certs)   │  │(config)  │                    │   │
  │  │  └──────────┘  └──────────┘  └──────────┘                    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                             │ xDS API (dynamic config)               │
  │                             ▼                                        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🚀 DATA PLANE (Envoy Sidecars)                               │   │
  │  │                                                               │   │
  │  │  ┌─────────┐  mTLS + metrics  ┌─────────┐                    │   │
  │  │  │ anihpj  │                  │  auth   │                    │   │
  │  │  │ ┌─────┐ │  ┌──────────┐   │ ┌─────┐ │                    │   │
  │  │  │ │ app │─┼─▶│  Envoy   │───┼▶│ app │ │                    │   │
  │  │  │ └─────┘ │  │ (sidecar)│   │ └─────┘ │                    │   │
  │  │  └─────────┘  └────┬─────┘   └─────────┘                    │   │
  │  │                    │  Traces → Jaeger                         │   │
  │  │                    │  Metrics → Prometheus                    │   │
  │  │                    │  Logs → stdout (collected by Fluentd)    │   │
  │  └────────────────────┼──────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                       │                                              │
  │  ┌────────────────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │  🌐 INGRESS GATEWAY (Istio Gateway)                            │   │
  │  │  External traffic → Gateway → VirtualService → anihpj         │   │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Istio VirtualService — Canary: 90% v1, 10% v2
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1beta1
kind: VirtualService
metadata: { name: anihpj }
spec:
  hosts: [anihpj]
  http:
    - route:
        - destination:
            host: anihpj
            subset: v1
          weight: 90     # 90% traffic to v1
        - destination:
            host: anihpj
            subset: v2
          weight: 10     # 10% traffic to v2 (canary)

23.3 Linkerd — The Lightweight Alternative

Linkerd is written in Rust, uses a micro-proxy (~10MB memory per pod, vs Envoy's ~50MB+), and is dramatically simpler than Istio (fewer CRDs, easier to operate).

DimensionIstioLinkerd
ProxyEnvoy (C++)linkerd-proxy (Rust)
Memory/Pod~50-100MB~10MB
CRDs50+~5
Traffic Splitting✅ VirtualService✅ SMI TrafficSplit
mTLS✅ Per-mesh or per-service✅ Automatic, always-on
Best ForLarge orgs, complex routingSimplicity-first, low resource

23.4 Traffic Management with Service Mesh

a) Canary Deployments

Route a percentage of traffic to new version, monitor, gradually increase. Istio VirtualService weight field controls the split. Start 5% → 25% → 50% → 100% based on metrics.

b) A/B Testing (Header-Based Routing)

Route users to different versions based on HTTP headers. Example: match: headers: { user-agent: { regex: ".*Mobile.*" } } → send mobile users to v2.

c) Circuit Breaking

Stop sending requests to a failing service. Istio DestinationRule sets connectionPool and outlierDetection (eject failing pods for 30s after 5 consecutive 5xx errors).

d) Retries

Automatically retry failed requests. VirtualService retries: { attempts: 3, perTryTimeout: 2s }. The mesh retries — not your application code.

e) Timeouts

Prevent waiting forever. VirtualService timeout: 5s — if the backend doesn't respond in 5 seconds, the mesh returns a 504.

f) Fault Injection

Test resilience by deliberately injecting failures. VirtualService fault: { delay: { percentage: 10, fixedDelay: 5s } } — 10% of requests get 5s delay. Test your circuit breakers!

💡 Platform Engineer's Insight

All six traffic management patterns are configured via YAML — no application code changes. This is the power of a service mesh: the platform team defines reliability patterns, and every service inherits them automatically.

23.5 Observability with Service Mesh

The mesh provides zero-code observability. Since every request flows through the sidecar proxy, you get metrics, traces, and service graphs without instrumenting your application.

ToolWhat It ProvidesIntegration
KialiService graph visualization — see all services and their traffic flowIstio-native
JaegerDistributed tracing — follow requests across servicesBuilt into Istio
GrafanaPre-built Istio dashboards (traffic, latency, errors)Via Prometheus
PrometheusRequest count, latency (P50/P95/P99), error rate — auto-collectedIstio metrics endpoint

✅ Chapter 23 Summary — Service Mesh Deep Dive

You've mastered the service mesh — the infrastructure layer that handles service-to-service communication without application code changes. You compared Istio (feature-rich, Envoy-based) to Linkerd (lightweight, Rust-based) to Cilium (eBPF-based, no sidecars), and learned the six traffic management patterns every platform engineer should know: canary deployments, A/B testing, circuit breaking, retries, timeouts, and fault injection. The key insight: a service mesh moves reliability logic from application code to platform infrastructure — developers get it for free, and platform engineers control it centrally.

🕸️ Key Takeaway 1

Service mesh = east-west traffic (service-to-service). API Gateway = north-south traffic (external → cluster). Together they cover all traffic.

🕸️ Key Takeaway 2

Sidecar (Istio/Linkerd) vs sidecarless (Cilium/eBPF). Sidecars add latency but are proven. eBPF is newer, faster, but less mature.

🕸️ Key Takeaway 3

Six patterns: canary, A/B, circuit break, retry, timeout, fault injection — ALL configured via YAML, zero code changes. Platform team defines, dev team inherits.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — Service Mesh (Domain 4, 12%)

The CNPA exam tests service mesh fundamentals: (1) North-South vs East-West: Ingress/API Gateway = north-south (external → cluster). Service Mesh = east-west (service-to-service). (2) Data Plane vs Control Plane: Data plane = sidecar proxies (Envoy/Linkerd-proxy) handling actual traffic. Control plane = management layer (Istiod/Linkerd control plane) distributing config. (3) Sidecar vs Sidecarless: Istio/Linkerd use sidecars (one proxy per pod). Cilium uses eBPF (kernel-level, no sidecars). Sidecarless = lower latency, lower resource usage. (4) mTLS: Automatic mutual TLS between all services — encrypts ALL pod-to-pod traffic without application changes. (5) Six traffic patterns every platform engineer should configure centrally: canary, A/B, circuit breaking, retries, timeouts, fault injection.

Chapter 24: API Gateways and Ingress Controllers

API Gateways manage north-south traffic (external → cluster), while service meshes manage east-west traffic (service-to-service). Together, they form a complete traffic management strategy for your platform.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1 hour 🌐 API 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

24.2 Popular API Gateways for Kubernetes

a) Kong

Open-source, Lua-based, massive plugin ecosystem (200+). Runs on Kubernetes with Kong Ingress Controller. Best for: enterprises needing auth, rate limiting, transformations, and API analytics in one platform.

b) Ambassador (Emissary)

Kubernetes-native, Envoy-based, CRD configuration. Mappings define routes. Best for: K8s-native teams who want API gateway as K8s YAML.

c) Gloo Edge (Solo.io)

Envoy-based with powerful request/response transformation. Supports function-level routing (Lambda, Cloud Functions). Best for: hybrid/multi-cloud with serverless integration.

d) APISIX (Apache)

High-performance (NGINX-based), rich plugin system, dynamic configuration via etcd. Best for: performance-critical API gateways handling millions of requests.

e) Traefik

Built-in Let's Encrypt, auto-discovery of K8s services, simple configuration. Best for: simplicity-first, automatic HTTPS, small-to-medium deployments.

GatewayProxyBest ForComplexity
KongNGINX/LuaEnterprise API management🟡 Medium
AmbassadorEnvoyK8s-native teams🟡 Medium
Gloo EdgeEnvoyHybrid/multi-cloud🔴 High
APISIXNGINXHigh-performance APIs🟡 Medium
TraefikGoSimplicity, auto-HTTPS🟢 Easy

24.3 API Management for Platform Teams

As a platform engineer, you provide the API management infrastructure so service teams can publish, secure, and monitor their APIs consistently.

📋 Platform API Management Capabilities

CapabilityWhat It DoesTool
API VersioningURL path (/v1/), header, query paramGateway routing
Rate LimitingProtect backends: 100 req/s per consumerKong rate-limit plugin
AuthenticationAPI keys, OAuth2, JWT, OIDCGateway auth plugins
Developer PortalSelf-service API discovery, docs, keysBackstage + Kong/Ambassador
API AnalyticsWho calls what, how often, latency, errorsPrometheus + Grafana
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know the difference: Ingress = basic L7 routing (host/path → Service), API Gateway = full API management (auth, rate limiting, developer portal), Service Mesh = east-west service-to-service (mTLS, retries, circuit breaking). They complement each other — a mature platform uses all three.

📝 Rate Limiting for anihpj API (Kong Example)

Kong Ingress + Rate Limiting Plugin
apiVersion: configuration.konghq.com/v1
kind: KongPlugin
metadata:
  name: rate-limit-anihpj
plugin: rate-limiting
config:
  minute: 100        # 100 requests per minute per consumer
  policy: local
---
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: anihpj-api
  annotations:
    konghq.com/plugins: rate-limit-anihpj
spec:
  rules:
    - host: api.anihpj.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /jobs
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: anihpj
                port: { number: 8000 }

📋 Part 8 Summary — Service Mesh & API Gateways

🕸️
Service Mesh

Infrastructure layer for service-to-service comms. Data plane (sidecar proxy) + control plane. Istio (Envoy, feature-rich) vs Linkerd (Rust, lightweight).

🚦
Traffic Management

Canary, A/B testing, circuit breaking, retries, timeouts, fault injection — all via YAML. Zero code changes. Platform defines; services inherit.

API Gateways

Kong, Ambassador, Gloo, APISIX, Traefik. North-south traffic + API management. Complement service mesh (east-west) and ingress (basic routing).

🔴 Part 9: FinOps & Cost Optimization for Platforms Chapters 25-26

Chapter 25: FinOps — Cloud Financial Operations for Platform Engineers

FinOps is the discipline of managing cloud costs through collaboration between Engineering, Finance, and Product teams. For platform engineers, it means building cost visibility and optimization directly into the IDP.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 💰 FinOps 🎯 Domain 6: 8%

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ FinOps lifecycle: Inform → Optimize → Operate (continuous loop)
  • ✅ Kubernetes cost management: right-sizing, spot instances, Kubecost
  • ✅ Cost-aware platform design: resource quotas, limit ranges, instance strategies
  • ✅ Chargeback vs showback models
  • ✅ Hands-on: Implement cost visibility for anihpj

25.1 What is FinOps?

FinOps (Financial Operations) is the cloud financial management discipline that brings together Engineering, Finance, and Product teams to make data-driven spending decisions. It's not about cutting costs — it's about maximizing business value per cloud dollar spent.

FinOps Lifecycle — Inform → Optimize → Operate
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │              ┌──────────────────────────┐                            │
  │              │        🔍 INFORM          │                            │
  │              │    (Visibility & Data)    │                            │
  │              │  • Tag ALL resources      │                            │
  │              │  • Show team costs        │  ← Continuous Loop!       │
  │              │  • Benchmark vs industry  │                            │
  │              │  • Anomaly detection      │                            │
  │              └──────────┬───────────────┘                            │
  │                         │                                            │
  │        ┌────────────────┼────────────────┐                           │
  │        ▼                │                ▼                           │
  │  ┌──────────┐           │           ┌──────────┐                    │
  │  │⚡OPTIMIZE│◀──────────┼──────────▶│🔧 OPERATE│                    │
  │  │          │           │           │          │                    │
  │  │• Rightsiz│           │           │• Automate│                    │
  │  │• Spot/RIs│           │           │• Budgets │                    │
  │  │• Delete  │           │           │• Alerts  │                    │
  │  │  unused  │           │           │• Govern  │                    │
  │  └──────────┘           │           └──────────┘                    │
  │                         ▼                                            │
  │              ┌──────────────────────────┐                            │
  │              │ 📊 Back to INFORM        │                            │
  │              └──────────────────────────┘                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

25.2 Kubernetes Cost Management

Kubernetes resource requests and limits are the primary cost drivers. Over-provisioned pods waste 30-50% of cloud spend at scale.

Optimization LeverTypical SavingsDifficulty
Right-sizing requests/limits30-50%🟡 Medium
Spot/Preemptible nodes60-80%🟢 Easy
Reserved/Savings Plans30-50%🟢 Easy
Schedule dev shutdown (nights/weekends)50-65%🟢 Easy
Delete unused resources (volumes, IPs, LBs)10-20%🟢 Easy
ARM/Graviton migration20-40%🔴 Hard
Autoscaling (HPA + Karpenter)25-40%🟡 Medium

25.3 Cost-Aware Platform Design

Build cost controls into the platform so teams don't have to think about them. Resource Quotas and Limit Ranges prevent cost runaway; spot instances reduce spend on fault-tolerant workloads.

ResourceQuota + LimitRange for anihpj
# Limit total resources per namespace (cost guardrail)
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata: { name: anihpj-quota, namespace: anihpj-dev }
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "20"
    requests.memory: 40Gi
    limits.cpu: "40"
    limits.memory: 80Gi
    persistentvolumeclaims: "10"
---
# Set default requests/limits for all pods
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata: { name: anihpj-limits, namespace: anihpj-dev }
spec:
  limits:
    - default: { cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi }
      defaultRequest: { cpu: 200m, memory: 256Mi }
      type: Container
Instance Strategy — Spot vs On-Demand vs Reserved
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Is the workload STATELESS & fault-tolerant?                         │
  │  ┌──────┴──────┐                                                     │
  │  │ YES         │ NO                                                  │
  │  ▼             ▼                                                     │
  │  Can handle    Steady-state workload?                                │
  │  2-min interr?  ┌──────┴──────┐                                      │
  │  ┌──┴──┐       │ YES         │ NO                                    │
  │  │YES  │NO     ▼             ▼                                       │
  │  ▼     ▼   ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐                                 │
  │  SPOT  On- │RESERVED │  │ON-DEMAND│  ← Baseline (always-on)          │
  │  60-80%    │ 30-50%  │  │(Full $) │                                  │
  │  OFF!  Dem-│  OFF!   │  └─────────┘                                  │
  │        and │         │                                                │
  │                                                                      │
  │  💡 OPTIMAL MIX: 60% Reserved + 30% Spot + 10% On-Demand             │
  │     Total Savings: ~40-50% vs 100% On-Demand                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

25.4 Chargeback and Showback Models

📊
Showback

Show teams their costs without billing them. Increases awareness. "Your team spent $3,200 this month on compute." No payment required. Start here.

💳
Chargeback

Actually bill teams for their usage. Creates accountability. "Your department is charged $3,200 for this month's infrastructure." Requires organizational maturity.

Implementation: Label ALL resources with team, cost-center, environment. Use Kubecost to aggregate costs by label and present per-team dashboards.

25.5 Hands-On: Implement Cost Visibility for anihpj

1
Install Kubecost

helm install kubecost kubecost/cost-analyzer -n kubecost

2
Label Resources

Label all anihpj resources: team=platform-team, env=production

3
Set Resource Quotas

Namespace quotas prevent one team from consuming all resources

4
View Dashboard

Kubecost shows per-team, per-namespace, per-service cost breakdown

5
Identify Waste

Find underutilized pods, unclaimed volumes, idle load balancers

6
Right-Size

Adjust requests based on actual usage (not guesses)

7
Set Alerts

"anihpj spending > $500/month → Slack #finops"

✅ Chapter 25 Summary — FinOps & Cost Optimization

You've mastered FinOps for platforms: the three phases (Inform → Optimize → Operate), cost optimization techniques (right-sizing, spot instances, HPA downscaling, resource quotas, LimitRange), chargeback vs showback models, and hands-on cost visibility with Kubecost. You now understand that FinOps is not just about saving money — it's about making cost data actionable so teams make informed trade-offs between speed, reliability, and cost. For CNPA Domain 6 (8%), know: Spot Instances = 60-90% cheaper but can be reclaimed, ResourceQuota = namespace-level limits, LimitRange = default pod limits, Kubecost/OpenCost = the standard tools.

💰 Key Takeaway 1

Spot instances = 60-90% cheaper, but can be reclaimed with 2-min notice. Use for stateless workloads. On-demand for stateful/ critical services.

💰 Key Takeaway 2

Showback first (show costs, no billing), then Chargeback (actual billing). Label EVERYTHING: team, cost-center, environment. Kubecost aggregates by label.

💰 Key Takeaway 3

Right-sizing: set requests = actual usage (not guesses). Over-provisioning is the #1 cloud waste. ResourceQuota prevents one team from consuming all resources.

🎯 EXAM HOTSPOT — FinOps & Cost (Domain 6, 8%)

The CNPA exam tests cost management fundamentals: (1) FinOps phases: Inform (visibility) → Optimize (right-sizing, spot) → Operate (continuous improvement). (2) Spot Instances: 60-90% cheaper but can be terminated with 2-min notice. Use for stateless workloads with PodDisruptionBudget. (3) Kubecost/OpenCost: The standard tools for K8s cost visibility. Group costs by namespace, label, team. (4) Chargeback vs Showback: Showback = show costs without billing (start here). Chargeback = actually bill teams (requires organizational maturity). (5) Right-sizing: Kubecost shows recommended requests/limits based on actual usage — most teams over-provision by 50-90%. (6) Waste elimination: Unattached PVs, idle LoadBalancers ($18/month each!), old snapshots, unclaimed IPs.

Chapter 26: Platform Scalability and Performance Engineering

Scaling isn't just about adding more pods. It's a 4-level pyramid: Application (HPA/VPA) → Cluster (Karpenter) → Platform (multi-cluster) → Global (multi-region). This chapter covers all four levels.

🔴 Advanced ⏱️ ~1.5 hours 📈 Scaling 🎯 Domain 4: 12%

26.1 Scaling Your Platform — The 4-Level Pyramid

The Scaling Pyramid
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                                      │
  │                        ┌─────┐                                       │
  │                        │ 🌍  │  Level 4: GLOBAL                      │
  │                        │Multi│  DNS routing, multi-region            │
  │                        │Region│  active-active, geo-failover         │
  │                       ┌┴─────┴┐                                      │
  │                       │  🏗️   │  Level 3: PLATFORM                   │
  │                       │Multi- │  Federation, multi-cluster           │
  │                       │Cluster│  global service mesh                 │
  │                      ┌┴──────┴┐                                      │
  │                      │  ☸️    │  Level 2: CLUSTER                    │
  │                      │Karpent│  Just-in-time node provisioning      │
  │                      │er / CA │  Spot instance management            │
  │                     ┌┴───────┴┐                                      │
  │                     │  📦     │  Level 1: APPLICATION                │
  │                     │HPA/VPA │  Pod autoscaling (CPU, mem, custom)  │
  │                     │ KEDA   │  Event-driven scaling                 │
  │                     └────────┘                                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
HPA for anihpj — CPU + Requests/sec
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: anihpj-hpa }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef:
    apiVersion: apps/v1
    kind: Deployment
    name: anihpj
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 20
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource:
        name: cpu
        target: { type: Utilization, averageUtilization: 70 }
    - type: Pods
      pods:
        metric: { name: http_requests_per_second }
        target: { type: AverageValue, averageValue: "1000" }

26.2 Karpenter — Next-Gen Node Autoscaling

Karpenter is an open-source, high-performance Kubernetes node autoscaler from AWS. Unlike Cluster Autoscaler (which works at node-group level), Karpenter provisions nodes just-in-time — in seconds, not minutes.

Karpenter — Just-in-Time Node Provisioning
  ① Pod Pending ("No node fits!")     ② Karpenter evaluates constraints
  ┌────────┐                          ┌──────────────────────┐
  │  New   │─────────────────────────▶│ CPU: 2, RAM: 4Gi     │
  │  Pod   │                          │ Arch: amd64          │
  │Pending!│                          │ Zone: us-east-1a     │
  └────────┘                          └──────────┬───────────┘
                                                 │
  ③ Select cheapest instance that fits          │
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  │  "c5.xlarge: 4CPU, 8GiB, $0.17/hr"  ← Best price!
  │
  ▼
  ④ Launch node + Bind pod  →  ⏱️ ~30 seconds (vs ~5 min for CA)
  │ ✅ Pod → Running!

  💡 Karpenter also consolidates: replaces underutilized nodes
     with fewer, cheaper, better-packed nodes automatically.
Karpenter Provisioner for anihpj
apiVersion: karpenter.sh/v1beta1
kind: NodePool
metadata: { name: anihpj-default }
spec:
  template:
    spec:
      requirements:
        - key: kubernetes.io/arch
          operator: In
          values: [amd64, arm64]  # Mix x86 + Graviton
        - key: karpenter.sh/capacity-type
          operator: In
          values: [spot, on-demand]  # Prefer spot
        - key: node.kubernetes.io/instance-type
          operator: In
          values: [t3.large, t3.xlarge, c5.large]
      nodeClassRef:
        name: default
  limits: { cpu: 100 }
  disruption:
    consolidationPolicy: WhenUnderutilized
    expireAfter: 720h

26.3 Performance Optimization for Platform Services

BottleneckSolutionImpact
Slow container startupOptimized base image (Alpine/Distroless), lazy importsScaling+Rollout speed
CPU throttlingRemove CPU limits OR increase them; use Guava/CFS metricsP99 latency
OOMKillsIncrease memory limits; fix memory leaks; add swap (if appropriate)Pod stability
DB connection pool exhaustionPgBouncer (PostgreSQL), connection pooling in appRequest queuing
Cross-AZ latencyPod anti-affinity to keep in same AZ; topology-aware routing5-15ms per request saved

26.4 Global Load Balancing and Multi-Region Platforms

For a truly global platform, you need to route users to the nearest healthy region. This requires DNS-based global load balancing (Route53, Cloud DNS, Traffic Manager) with health checks that automatically failover when a region is unhealthy.

🔄
Active-Active

All regions serve traffic simultaneously. Higher cost, zero failover time. DNS routes to nearest healthy region. Best for: global SaaS, latency-sensitive apps.

🔄
Active-Passive

One region serves traffic; standby region activates on failure. Lower cost, 1-5 min failover. Best for: cost-conscious, DR compliance, non-latency-sensitive.

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip

Know the scaling pyramid: App (HPA/VPA/KEDA) → Cluster (Karpenter/CA) → Platform (multi-cluster/federation) → Global (DNS/LB). The exam asks: "A pod is pending due to no available nodes. Which component resolves this?" Answer: Karpenter or Cluster Autoscaler provisions a new node.

📋 Part 9 Summary — FinOps & Scalability

💰
FinOps

Inform→Optimize→Operate. Right-sizing saves 30-50%. Spot instances save 60-80%. Reserved plans save 30-50%. Kubecost for visibility.

📈
Scaling Pyramid

4 levels: HPA/VPA (pods) → Karpenter (nodes) → Federation (clusters) → Global DNS/LB (regions). Karpenter provisions nodes in ~30 seconds.

⚡
Performance

CPU throttling, OOMKills, DB pool exhaustion, cross-AZ latency. Optimize images, tune limits, use PgBouncer, topology-aware routing.

📊 Pass-the-Exam Score Math

Domain 1 (36%)Target: 28/36 correct (78%)= 28 points
Domain 2 (20%)Target: 15/20 correct (75%)= 15 points
Domain 3 (16%)Target: 12/16 correct (75%)= 12 points
Domain 4 (12%)Target: 8/12 correct (67%)= 8 points
Domain 5 (8%)Target: 5/8 correct (63%)= 5 points
Domain 6 (8%)Target: 5/8 correct (63%)= 5 points
TOTAL73/60 = 73% ✅

💡 Even if you score JUST 50% on Domains 5+6, you still pass if D1+D2 are strong. The math works in your favor when you prioritize.

27.2a Per-Domain Exam Strategy — How to Attack Each Domain

🟦
Domain 1: Core Fundamentals (36%)

Question types: "Which K8s component does X?", "What is the correct YAML structure for a Deployment?", "Docker vs containerd — which is the CNCF runtime?", "What does declarative mean?". Strategy: Master the K8s architecture diagram (control plane + worker nodes). Know every component's ONE job. Be able to spot YAML errors (wrong apiVersion, missing selector). Docker fundamentals (Dockerfile instructions, multi-stage builds).

🟩
Domain 2: Observability & Security (20%)

Question types: "Which tool for centralized logging?", "OPA vs Kyverno — which is YAML-based?", "What does Falco detect?", "Which metric type for request latency?". Strategy: Know the 3 pillars COLD: Logs (Loki/ELK), Metrics (Prometheus), Traces (Jaeger/Tempo). OTel standardizes all three. Security: OPA = Rego (general), Kyverno = YAML (K8s-native). Falco = runtime threat detection. Trivy = image scanning. Cosign = image signing.

🟨
Domain 3: CD & Platform Engineering (16%)

Question types: "GitOps: where is the source of truth?", "ArgoCD vs Flux — which uses Helm-like releases?", "What is progressive delivery?", "CI vs CD — what's the difference?". Strategy: GitOps = Git is the single source of truth. ArgoCD = GUI-rich, multi-cluster. Flux = Helm-controller native. Progressive delivery = canary/blue-green via Argo Rollouts. CI = build+test. CD = deploy automatically (continuous) or on approval (delivery).

🟧
Domain 4: Platform APIs (12%)

Question types: "What is the reconciliation loop?", "CRD vs Operator — what's the difference?", "Crossplane vs Terraform — which runs inside K8s?", "What does a ServiceCatalog provide?". Strategy: Reconciliation = observe → diff → act → repeat. CRD = custom resource type. Operator = CRD + controller (logic). Crossplane = K8s-native infra (inside cluster). Terraform = external infra (CI/CD). Cluster API = declarative cluster management.

🟪
Domain 5: IDPs & DevEx (8%)

Question types: "What does Backstage provide?", "What is a Golden Path?", "Service Catalog vs Software Catalog?", "How does AI help platforms?". Strategy: Backstage = Spotify's CNCF portal (Catalog + Templates + TechDocs). Golden Path = paved road. Service Catalog = what's running. Software Catalog = what exists (docs, ownership). DevEx = reducing cognitive load. AI = code generation, template scaffolding, incident auto-remediation.

🟥
Domain 6: Measuring (8%)

Question types: "Name the four DORA metrics", "What is an Error Budget?", "SLI vs SLO vs SLA", "How do you measure platform adoption?". Strategy: DORA = DF + LT + CFR + TTR (memorize!). SLI = what you measure (e.g., % of successful requests). SLO = target (e.g., 99.9%). SLA = contract with consequences. Error Budget = 1 − SLO. Burn Rate = how fast you consume budget. Adoption = # services onboarded, % teams using platform.

27.2b CNPA Question Patterns — The 6 Types You'll See

TypeExampleHow to Answer
1. Tool Selection"A team needs self-service databases on K8s. Which tool?"Match tool to use case. Crossplane = K8s-native infra provisioning. Eliminate obviously wrong tools first.
2. Definition"What does 'Declarative API' mean in cloud native?"Know exact definitions. "Declare desired state; system reconciles" — not "write scripts to configure servers."
3. Comparison"Terraform vs Crossplane: which runs inside K8s?"Know key differentiators. Crossplane = inside K8s (CRDs). Terraform = outside K8s (HCL, state file).
4. Architecture"Which Control Plane component schedules pods?"Know the K8s architecture. Scheduler = pod placement. Controller Manager = reconciliation. API Server = front door.
5. Scenario"Developers wait 3 weeks for databases. Platform solution?"Self-service API (Crossplane/Operator) + catalog. NOT: hire more ops. NOT: give everyone cloud access.
6. Best Practice"How should secrets be managed in CI/CD?"External secret manager (Vault), not hardcoded in pipelines, not in Git, not in environment variables.

27.2c Top 10 CNPA Exam Traps — Don't Fall for These

❌ TRAP
✅ RIGHT ANSWER / APPROACH
"Kubernetes provides built-in monitoring dashboards"
K8s has NO built-in dashboards. You need Prometheus + Grafana. K8s only exposes metrics endpoints.
"Terraform runs inside Kubernetes"
Terraform runs OUTSIDE K8s (in CI/CD). Crossplane runs INSIDE K8s. Don't confuse them.
"OPA uses YAML for policies"
OPA uses Rego (a custom language). Kyverno uses YAML. This distinction appears on the exam.
"ArgoCD and Jenkins serve the same purpose"
Jenkins = CI (build+test). ArgoCD = CD/GitOps (deploy from Git). Different stages of the pipeline.
"Service Mesh = API Gateway"
Service Mesh = east-west (service-to-service). API Gateway = north-south (external → cluster). Different directions.
"More replicas always fix performance"
Only if the bottleneck is CPU/memory per pod. DB bottlenecks, connection pool limits, or downstream slowness won't improve with more replicas.
"SLI, SLO, SLA are interchangeable"
SLI = Indicator (measurement). SLO = Objective (target). SLA = Agreement (contract). Different concepts tested separately.
"Observability = Monitoring"
Monitoring = known unknowns (dashboards). Observability = unknown unknowns (explore new questions). OTel enables observability.
"GitOps means deploying from Git"
GitOps = Git is THE source of truth + reconciliation loop. Push vs Pull: ArgoCD/Flux PULL from Git (not CI pushing to cluster).
"Platform Engineering replaces DevOps"
PE AUGMENTS DevOps. DevOps = culture + practices. PE = building IDPs to reduce cognitive load. They coexist.

27.4b Practice Questions — Domains 2-6

#DomainQuestionAnswer
11D2Which tool provides runtime threat detection via eBPF?Falco — monitors syscalls in real-time
12D2What is an Error Budget and how is it calculated?1 − SLO = allowed unreliability. If SLO = 99.9%, error budget = 0.1% downtime (~43 min/month)
13D2OPA policies are written in which language?Rego — a declarative policy language. Kyverno uses YAML instead.
14D3ArgoCD pulls config from Git. This is an example of?Pull-based GitOps — the agent inside K8s pulls desired state (vs CI pushing to cluster)
15D3What is Progressive Delivery?Gradually rolling out changes (canary/blue-green) with automated analysis and rollback if metrics degrade
16D4What is the Kubernetes Reconciliation Loop?Observe → Diff → Act → Repeat. Controllers continuously compare desired state (spec) with actual state (status) and reconcile differences.
17D4CRD vs Operator — what's the difference?CRD = custom resource TYPE (schema only). Operator = CRD + Controller (schema + logic to manage lifecycle)
18D5What are the 3 core Backstage components?Software Catalog (what exists), Software Templates (scaffold new services), TechDocs (documentation)
19D6SLI vs SLO vs SLA — define eachSLI = Indicator (measurement, e.g., 99.95% success rate). SLO = Objective (target, e.g., 99.9%). SLA = Agreement (contract with consequences)
20D6How do you measure platform adoption?# services onboarded, % teams using platform, Time-to-10th-deploy, Developer NPS. Not just raw usage — measure satisfaction and productivity gains.

Chapter 28: Hands-On CNPA Lab Scenarios

10 hands-on labs that simulate real CNPA exam scenarios — each with a clear objective, requirements, detailed steps, and success criteria. Click each lab to expand the full walkthrough.
