🔬 28.1 Lab 1: Build a Golden Path Template

🎯 Objective: Create a reusable Django scaffold that lets any developer create a new anihpj-style service in under 10 minutes.

📋 Requirements:

  • Cookiecutter template with: Django project structure, pre-configured Dockerfile (multi-stage), K8s manifests (Deployment, Service, Ingress), GitHub Actions CI pipeline, Makefile with common commands
  • Template prompts for: service name, description, team name, port number
  • Generated project must pass docker build and kubectl apply --dry-run=client immediately

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Create & Use Golden Path Template
# Step 1: Install Cookiecutter
pip install cookiecutter

# Step 2: Create the template structure
mkdir -p cookiecutter-anihpj-service/{{cookiecutter.service_name}}
cd cookiecutter-anihpj-service

# Step 3: Write cookiecutter.json (template prompts)
cat > cookiecutter.json << 'EOF'
{
  "service_name": "my-service",
  "description": "A new anihpj microservice",
  "team_name": "platform-team",
  "port": 8000,
  "python_version": "3.11",
  "database": ["postgresql", "none"]
}
EOF

# Step 4: Create templated Dockerfile
cat > {{cookiecutter.service_name}}/Dockerfile << 'EOF'
FROM python:{{cookiecutter.python_version}}-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

FROM python:{{cookiecutter.python_version}}-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
COPY . .
EXPOSE {{cookiecutter.port}}
CMD ["gunicorn", "{{cookiecutter.service_name}}.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:{{cookiecutter.port}}"]
EOF

# Step 5: Create templated K8s manifests (deployment.yaml, service.yaml, ingress.yaml)
# Step 6: Create .github/workflows/ci.yml (lint, test, build, push)
# Step 7: Test the template
cookiecutter ./cookiecutter-anihpj-service
# Enter service_name: jobpost-v2, team: backend-team, port: 8000
cd jobpost-v2
docker build -t jobpost-v2:latest .
kubectl apply --dry-run=client -f k8s/

✅ Success Criteria: A developer clones the template, answers 4 prompts, and has a Docker-built, K8s-ready service in <10 minutes.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 1 (PE Core Fundamentals), Domain 5 (IDPs & DevEx)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Cookiecutter "not found": Ensure pip install cookiecutter ran in the correct Python environment. Use which cookiecutter to verify.
  • Template variables not rendering: Check that {{cookiecutter.service_name}} uses double braces and matches the key name in cookiecutter.json exactly (case-sensitive).
  • docker build fails on pip install: The template's requirements.txt may reference packages not in PyPI. Always include django>=4.2,<5.0 and gunicorn>=21.2 as minimum deps.
  • kubectl dry-run fails: The K8s manifests may reference {{ .Values.x }} Helm syntax — replace with hardcoded values for the base template. Golden Path templates should produce raw, deployable YAML.

🌍 Real-World Context: Spotify's Backstage uses a similar pattern with their Software Templates — they define a template.yaml that Backstage renders into a new GitHub repo with CI/CD pre-configured. Airbnb uses Cookiecutter internally for their 500+ service templates. The pattern is universal: define once, use thousands of times. Your anihpj Golden Path should be versioned in Git and treated as a product — update it as best practices evolve.

🔄 28.2 Lab 2: Set Up GitOps with ArgoCD

🎯 Objective: Configure ArgoCD to automatically deploy anihpj whenever the Git repository changes.

📋 Requirements:

  • Install ArgoCD on your K8s cluster
  • Create an ArgoCD Application that watches your anihpj Git repo
  • Enable auto-sync (automatic deployment on Git change)
  • Enable auto-pruning (remove resources deleted from Git)

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — ArgoCD Setup & GitOps Flow
# Step 1: Install ArgoCD
kubectl create namespace argocd
kubectl apply -n argocd -f https://raw.githubusercontent.com/argoproj/argo-cd/stable/manifests/install.yaml

# Step 2: Access ArgoCD UI
kubectl port-forward svc/argocd-server -n argocd 8080:443
# Get admin password:
kubectl -n argocd get secret argocd-initial-admin-secret -o jsonpath="{.data.password}" | base64 -d

# Step 3: Create ArgoCD Application for anihpj
cat > anihpj-argocd-app.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Application
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: argocd
spec:
  project: default
  source:
    repoURL: https://github.com/anihpj/anihpj-config
    targetRevision: main
    path: k8s/overlays/production
  destination:
    server: https://kubernetes.default.svc
    namespace: anihpj-prod
  syncPolicy:
    automated:
      prune: true        # Delete resources removed from Git
      selfHeal: true     # Revert manual changes
    syncOptions:
      - CreateNamespace=true
EOF
kubectl apply -f anihpj-argocd-app.yaml

# Step 4: Test GitOps flow
# Make a change in Git (e.g., update image tag)
git commit -m "Update anihpj to v1.3.0"
git push
# ArgoCD detects change → auto-syncs → deploys new version
# Watch sync status: argocd app get anihpj --refresh

✅ Success Criteria: Push a change to Git → ArgoCD auto-detects and deploys within <5 minutes. Manual kubectl apply changes are auto-reverted (selfHeal).

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 3 (Continuous Delivery & GitOps)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • ArgoCD "OutOfSync": This is NORMAL for auto-sync with a 3-minute polling interval. Check argocd app get anihpj --refresh to force immediate refresh. If still OutOfSync, check the ArgoCD repo server can reach your Git URL (network, auth).
  • "Repository not found": For private repos, create a Secret with Git credentials. Use argocd repo add CLI or reference a RepoSecret CR in your Application YAML under source.repoURL.
  • Health check "Degraded": A K8s resource is unhealthy. Click the app in ArgoCD UI → find the red resource. Common causes: image pull error, CrashLoopBackOff, missing ConfigMap.
  • SelfHeal not reverting: Verify selfHeal: true in syncPolicy. It runs on a 5-min reconciliation loop — not instant. Use argocd app sync anihpj for immediate sync.

🌍 Real-World Context: Intuit runs ArgoCD across 200+ clusters managing 4,000+ applications. The key principle: Git is the single source of truth, and the reconciliation loop ensures the cluster ALWAYS matches Git. GitOps is CNCF-recommended — ArgoCD and Flux are both graduated projects.

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • ArgoCD "OutOfSync": This is NORMAL for auto-sync with a 3-minute polling interval. Check argocd app get anihpj --refresh to force immediate refresh. If still OutOfSync, check the ArgoCD repo server can reach your Git URL (network, auth).
  • "Repository not found" or auth error: For private repos, create a Secret with your Git credentials and reference it in the Application's source.repoURL config. Use argocd repo add CLI or a RepoSecret CR.
  • Health check shows "Degraded": One or more K8s resources in the app are unhealthy. Click the app in ArgoCD UI → see which resource is red. Common causes: image pull error, CrashLoopBackOff, or missing ConfigMap.
  • SelfHeal not reverting manual changes: Verify selfHeal: true is set in the syncPolicy. SelfHeal runs on a 5-minute reconciliation loop by default — it won't be instant.

🌍 Real-World Context: Intuit (TurboTax, Mint) runs ArgoCD across 200+ clusters managing 4,000+ applications. They use ApplicationSets (a superset of Applications) to deploy the same app to dev/staging/prod clusters with a single definition. GitOps is now the CNCF-recommended deployment pattern — ArgoCD and Flux are both graduated projects. The key principle: Git is the single source of truth, and the reconciliation loop ensures the cluster ALWAYS matches Git.

📊 28.3 Lab 3: Implement Observability Stack

🎯 Objective: Deploy Prometheus + Grafana + Loki for the anihpj platform and create a dashboard that surfaces a performance issue in under 2 minutes.

📋 Requirements:

  • Prometheus: scrape anihpj metrics (request rate, latency, error rate)
  • Grafana: provision a "Platform Overview" dashboard with RED metrics
  • Loki: collect and search anihpj application logs
  • Simulate a performance degradation and verify it appears on the dashboard

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Observability Stack Setup
# Step 1: Install kube-prometheus-stack (Prometheus + Grafana)
helm repo add prometheus-community https://prometheus-community.github.io/helm-charts
helm install monitoring prometheus-community/kube-prometheus-stack \
  --namespace monitoring --create-namespace \
  --set grafana.adminPassword=anihpj-admin

# Step 2: Add ServiceMonitor for anihpj
cat > anihpj-servicemonitor.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: monitoring.coreos.com/v1
kind: ServiceMonitor
metadata:
  name: anihpj
  namespace: monitoring
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  endpoints:
    - port: metrics
      interval: 15s
EOF
kubectl apply -f anihpj-servicemonitor.yaml

# Step 3: Install Loki for log aggregation
helm install loki grafana/loki-stack --namespace monitoring

# Step 4: Create Grafana dashboard (provision as ConfigMap)
# Import dashboard JSON with panels for:
# - Request Rate: rate(http_requests_total{app="anihpj"}[5m])
# - Error Rate: rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m])
# - P99 Latency: histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m]))

# Step 5: Simulate performance issue
# Run load test that saturates DB connections
# Watch dashboard — P99 latency spikes, error rate increases
# Identify issue in <2 minutes from the dashboard

✅ Success Criteria: Introduce a performance issue (e.g., slow DB query) → identify it on the Grafana dashboard in <2 minutes using RED metrics.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 2 (Observability, Security & Conformance)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Prometheus targets "DOWN": The ServiceMonitor must be in the same namespace as Prometheus (or use namespaceSelector). Verify the Service has matching labels. Check kubectl get servicemonitors -A.
  • Grafana "No Data": Check Data Sources → Prometheus URL should be http://monitoring-kube-prometheus-prometheus.monitoring:9090. Test connection. Verify your PromQL query is valid by running in Prometheus UI first.
  • Loki not receiving logs: Apps must log to stdout/stderr (not files). Promtail only collects container stdout by default. For structured logging, use JSON format with level, message, timestamp fields for best Loki experience.
  • High cardinality: Avoid labels with user IDs, request IDs, timestamps — each creates a new time series. Use exemplars for trace correlation instead of high-cardinality labels.

🌍 Real-World Context: The LGTM stack (Loki, Grafana, Tempo, Mimir) is the modern CNCF observability standard. Uber runs Jaeger at billions of spans/day. Pinterest runs Prometheus at petabyte scale with Thanos. Your anihpj platform should start with the basic stack and add Thanos for long-term storage as you scale beyond 30 days of retention.

🔒 28.4 Lab 4: Secure the Platform

🎯 Objective: Implement platform security controls: RBAC, NetworkPolicy, Kyverno policies, and image signing. Verify that a non-compliant pod is blocked.

📋 Requirements:

  • RBAC: Create Role + RoleBinding limiting dev-team to get/list/create in anihpj-dev
  • NetworkPolicy: Default-deny all ingress, allow only from frontend pods on port 8000
  • Kyverno: Block pods using :latest tag, require livenessProbe and readinessProbe
  • Cosign: Sign the anihpj image and verify signature at admission

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Security Implementation
# Step 1: Install Kyverno
helm install kyverno kyverno/kyverno --namespace kyverno --create-namespace

# Step 2: Create Kyverno policy (block :latest, require probes)
cat > require-probes.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata:
  name: require-probes
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: require-liveness-readiness
      match:
        resources:
          kinds: [Pod]
      validate:
        message: "livenessProbe and readinessProbe are required"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - livenessProbe: "?*"
                readinessProbe: "?*"
                image: "!*:latest"
EOF
kubectl apply -f require-probes.yaml

# Step 3: Test — deploy non-compliant pod
kubectl run bad-pod --image=nginx:latest --restart=Never
# ❌ BLOCKED by Kyverno: ":latest tag prohibited"

# Step 4: Test — deploy compliant pod
kubectl apply -f anihpj-deployment.yaml  # Has probes + non-latest tag
# ✅ ALLOWED

# Step 5: Sign image with Cosign
cosign sign --key cosign.key ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0
# Step 6: Create Kyverno policy to verify signatures at admission

✅ Success Criteria: Deploy a pod with :latest tag and no probes → BLOCKED by Kyverno. Deploy a compliant pod → ALLOWED.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 2 (Observability, Security & Conformance)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Kyverno not enforcing: Check validationFailureAction: Enforce (not Audit). Verify kubectl get clusterpolicy -o yaml | grep ready shows ready: true. The match.resources.kinds must include the resource type being tested.
  • RBAC "forbidden": Test with kubectl auth can-i create pods --as=dev-user -n anihpj-dev. Roles are namespace-scoped; ClusterRoles are cluster-wide. RoleBindings bind within a namespace.
  • NetworkPolicy not working: Your CNI MUST support NetworkPolicy (Calico, Cilium, Weave). Flannel does NOT. Check with kubectl get pods -n kube-system | grep -E 'calico|cilium|weave'. Multiple policies OR together for allowed traffic.
  • Cosign verify fails: Verify manually: cosign verify --key cosign.pub ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0. Ensure the public key in the Kyverno policy matches the private key used to sign.

🌍 Real-World Context: Google's BeyondCorp implements zero-trust — every request authenticated regardless of source. In CNCF: OPA/Gatekeeper (graduated) and Kyverno enforce policies. The 2024 CNCF Security Audit found 68% of organizations use policy engines in production. For anihpj: start with Kyverno (easiest K8s-native), add Falco for runtime detection, implement image signing as you mature.

✈️ 28.5 Lab 5: Build Platform API with Crossplane

🎯 Objective: Create a self-service "Database" API using Crossplane so developers can provision PostgreSQL instances by running kubectl apply -f db.yaml.

📋 Requirements:

  • Install Crossplane with AWS provider
  • Define an XRD (Composite Resource Definition) for PostgreSQL
  • Define a Composition that maps XRD → RDS instance + K8s Secret
  • Developer creates an XPostgreSQL claim → Crossplane provisions RDS

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Crossplane Setup & Self-Service DB
# Step 1: Install Crossplane
helm install crossplane crossplane-stable/crossplane --namespace crossplane-system --create-namespace

# Step 2: Install AWS Provider + configure credentials
cat > provider-aws.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: pkg.crossplane.io/v1
kind: Provider
metadata:
  name: provider-aws-rds
spec:
  package: xpkg.upbound.io/upbound/provider-aws-rds:v1.0.0
EOF
kubectl apply -f provider-aws.yaml

# Step 3: Define XRD (API for developers)
# xpostgresql.yaml — defines what fields developers can set (size, version, region)

# Step 4: Define Composition (blueprint mapping API → real resources)
# composition-postgresql.yaml — maps XPostgreSQL to RDS Instance + Secret

# Step 5: Developer provisions database!
cat > my-db.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: platform.anihpj.io/v1alpha1
kind: XPostgreSQL
metadata:
  name: jobpost-db
spec:
  size: small       # db.t3.small
  version: "16.1"
  region: us-east-1
EOF
kubectl apply -f my-db.yaml
# ⏱️ ~5 minutes → RDS instance provisioned + connection Secret created!

✅ Success Criteria: Run kubectl apply -f my-db.yaml → PostgreSQL RDS instance is provisioned, connection details stored in a K8s Secret. DB provisioned via self-service API.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 4 (Platform APIs & Provisioning Infrastructure)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Provider not HEALTHY: Check kubectl get providers.pkg.crossplane.io — if not Healthy, the package may fail to pull. Check kubectl describe provider for events. Verify ProviderConfig has valid cloud credentials.
  • Claim stuck SYNCING: Check kubectl describe <your-claim> for events. Common: Composition references a non-existent Managed Resource, patch errors, or cloud API quota exceeded.
  • Cloud API auth failure: ProviderConfig must reference a Secret with correct keys. For AWS: aws_access_key_id, aws_secret_access_key. Better: use IRSA (IAM Roles for Service Accounts) with OIDC for pod identity — no static credentials.
  • Connection Secret empty: The Composition must include writeConnectionSecretToRef on the XRD claim, OR an explicit K8s Secret resource in the Composition with connectionDetails patching from the managed resource.

🌍 Real-World Context: Crossplane is used by SAP, VSHN, and Mux to build internal cloud platforms. AWS ACK and GCP Config Connector follow the same CRD pattern — define cloud resources as K8s objects. The pattern is universal: CRD = API, Operator/Crossplane = reconciliation. For anihpj: start with PostgreSQL and S3 APIs, then add Redis, SQS, and EKS node groups as developers need them. Every new API is another "paved road."

🐤 28.6 Lab 6: Canary Deployments with Argo Rollouts

🎯 Objective: Set up progressive delivery for anihpj using Argo Rollouts. Deploy a bad version and verify it auto-rolls back.

📋 Requirements:

  • Install Argo Rollouts controller
  • Convert anihpj Deployment to Rollout resource
  • Define canary strategy: 20% → 50% → 100% with analysis
  • Configure AnalysisTemplate that checks error rate via Prometheus
  • Deploy a version that returns 50% errors → verify auto-rollback

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Canary Deployment with Auto-Rollback
# Step 1: Install Argo Rollouts
kubectl create namespace argo-rollouts
kubectl apply -n argo-rollouts -f https://github.com/argoproj/argo-rollouts/releases/latest/download/install.yaml

# Step 2: Create Rollout (replaces Deployment)
cat > anihpj-rollout.yaml << 'EOF'
apiVersion: argoproj.io/v1alpha1
kind: Rollout
metadata:
  name: anihpj
spec:
  replicas: 5
  strategy:
    canary:
      steps:
        - setWeight: 20     # 20% traffic to new version
        - pause: {duration: 60s}
        - setWeight: 50     # 50% traffic
        - pause: {duration: 60s}
        - setWeight: 100    # 100% traffic
      analysis:
        templates:
          - templateName: error-rate-check
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: anihpj
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: anihpj
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: anihpj
          image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.3.0
EOF

# Step 3: Deploy broken version
kubectl argo rollouts set image anihpj anihpj=ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.4.0-broken
# Argo Rollouts: 20% → 50% → Analysis detects high error rate → ❌ AUTO-ROLLBACK!

✅ Success Criteria: Deploy a broken version → Argo Rollouts detects errors via Prometheus analysis → auto-rolls back to the previous good version.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 3 (Continuous Delivery & Platform Engineering)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Rollout stuck at a step: Check kubectl argo rollouts get rollout anihpj. If paused at a canary step, the analysis template is running. Check kubectl get analysisruns for the analysis status.
  • AnalysisTemplate can't query Prometheus: Verify the Prometheus URL in the AnalysisTemplate. It should be the cluster-internal URL. Use http://monitoring-kube-prometheus-prometheus.monitoring:9090.
  • Blue-Green vs Canary confusion: Blue-Green = instant switch (100% traffic to new version at once). Canary = gradual shift (20%→50%→100%). Use canary for most services; blue-green when you need instant rollback capability.
  • Rollout not replacing Deployment: Delete the existing Deployment first (Rollout manages its own ReplicaSets). Or use kubectl apply with the same name — Rollout will take over (but test this in dev first).

🌍 Real-World Context: Argo Rollouts powers progressive delivery at Adobe, Intuit, and Tesla. The pattern: metrics-driven deployment → automated rollback — is now considered table stakes for production platforms. Google uses a similar approach internally (Borg canary with automated health checks). The key insight: don't deploy and pray. Deploy, verify, and automatically roll back on failure.

☁️ 28.7 Lab 7: Multi-Cloud Platform

🎯 Objective: Deploy anihpj across EKS (us-east-1) and GKE (us-central1). Configure global DNS to route users to the nearest healthy cluster.

📋 Requirements:

  • Provision EKS cluster (us-east-1) and GKE cluster (us-central1) via Terraform
  • Deploy identical anihpj manifests to both clusters via ArgoCD
  • Configure Route53 latency-based routing between clusters
  • Simulate us-east-1 failure → traffic automatically routes to GKE

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Multi-Cloud Setup
# Step 1: Terraform provisions EKS + GKE
# main.tf provisions both clusters

# Step 2: Register both clusters in ArgoCD
argocd cluster add eks-us-east-1 --name eks-east
argocd cluster add gke-us-central1 --name gke-central

# Step 3: Create ArgoCD ApplicationSet (deploy to all clusters)
# ApplicationSet with cluster generator deploys anihpj to both

# Step 4: Configure Route53 latency-based routing
# Create 2 A records (alias to each cluster's LoadBalancer)
# Routing policy: Latency
# Record 1: api.anihpj.com → EKS LB (us-east-1)
# Record 2: api.anihpj.com → GKE LB (us-central1)

# Step 5: Test failover
# Scale down anihpj in us-east-1
kubectl scale deployment anihpj --replicas=0 --context eks-east
# Route53 health check detects failure → routes all traffic to GKE

✅ Success Criteria: Users routed to nearest cluster by latency. If one cluster fails, traffic auto-routes to the healthy cluster with zero manual intervention.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 4 (Platform APIs & Provisioning Infrastructure)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • ArgoCD can't reach remote cluster: Verify the kubeconfig context works: kubectl --context eks-east get nodes. Use argocd cluster add with explicit context name. For private clusters, ensure network connectivity between ArgoCD and the remote API server.
  • Route53 health checks failing: Health check endpoint must return HTTP 200-399. Create a dedicated /healthz endpoint that checks DB connectivity. Ensure the health check source IPs can reach your LB (security group rules).
  • Latency routing not working as expected: Route53 uses IP-to-region mapping based on the DNS resolver's IP, not the user's IP. If using a VPN, all users may route to the same region. Test from multiple geographic locations.
  • Data consistency across regions: Each region has its own RDS instance. For read-heavy apps, use read replicas. For write-heavy apps, pick a primary region and replicate. Crossplane can manage multi-region DB topologies.

🌍 Real-World Context: Netflix runs a full active-active multi-region architecture — every region can serve all traffic. They use their own DNS service (Zuul) with health-based routing. The pattern is: deploy everywhere, route to the nearest healthy region, and failover automatically. For most platforms, start with active-passive (one primary + one DR region), evolve to active-active as you scale.

💰 28.8 Lab 8: Cost Optimization

🎯 Objective: Reduce the anihpj platform's monthly cloud bill from $5,000 to under $3,000 (40% reduction) using spot instances, right-sizing, and resource quotas.

📋 Requirements:

  • Install Kubecost for cost visibility
  • Convert non-critical workloads to spot instances
  • Right-size resource requests based on actual usage (not guesses)
  • Set ResourceQuota and LimitRange per namespace
  • Identify and remove unused resources (unattached volumes, idle LBs)

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — FinOps Implementation
# Step 1: Install Kubecost
helm install kubecost kubecost/cost-analyzer -n kubecost --create-namespace
# Access dashboard: kubectl port-forward svc/kubecost-cost-analyzer 9090 -n kubecost

# Step 2: Analyze current spending
# Kubecost shows: $5,000/month — $3,200 compute, $800 storage, $600 networking, $400 other

# Step 3: Switch to spot instances (60-80% cheaper)
# Add toleration for spot nodes in non-critical deployments
tolerations:
  - key: "kubernetes.azure.com/scalesetpriority"
    operator: "Equal"
    value: "spot"
    effect: "NoSchedule"

# Step 4: Right-size resources
# Kubecost shows: dev services request 500m CPU, actually use 50m (90% waste!)
# Reduce requests: cpu: 100m, memory: 128Mi

# Step 5: Set ResourceQuota
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: dev-quota
  namespace: anihpj-dev
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "10"
    requests.memory: 20Gi
    persistentvolumeclaims: "5"

# Step 6: Clean up waste
# Identify unattached PVs, idle LoadBalancers, old snapshots
# Delete unused resources → ~$400/month savings

✅ Success Criteria: Monthly bill drops from $5,000 to <$3,000 (40%+ reduction) through spot instances, right-sizing, and waste cleanup.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 6 (Measuring Your Platform)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Spot instances being reclaimed constantly: Use Karpenter or Cluster Autoscaler with spot fallback to on-demand. Configure PodDisruptionBudget (minAvailable: 2) so spot evictions don't cause downtime. Diversify across multiple instance types and availability zones.
  • Kubecost showing wrong costs: Ensure all nodes have proper labels and cost allocation tags. For AWS, enable Cost and Usage Reports (CUR) integration. For on-prem, configure custom pricing in Kubecost's helm values.
  • Right-sizing causing OOMKills: Don't reduce limits below the 95th percentile of actual usage. Kubecost shows \"Recommended Requests/Limits\" based on historical data — use that. Set a 20% buffer above recommendations.
  • Idle resources not detected: Kubecost's \"Savings\" tab shows orphaned resources (disks, IPs, LBs). Also check: unused LoadBalancer Services (each costs ~$18/month on AWS), unattached PersistentVolumes, old snapshots.

🌍 Real-World Context: The FinOps Foundation reports that organizations using FinOps practices save 20-30% on cloud costs within the first year. Spotify saved millions by migrating to spot instances with predictive autoscaling. The key principles: visibility first (you can't optimize what you don't measure), then optimize, then automate. For anihpj: run weekly cost reviews, set budget alerts at 80% of monthly target, and make cost a first-class dashboard metric alongside latency and error rate.

🚨 28.9 Lab 9: Incident Response Simulation

🎯 Objective: anihpj is DOWN! Find the root cause, fix it, restore service, and write a postmortem — all within 15 minutes.

📋 Scenario: Users report api.anihpj.com/jobs/ returning 500 errors. You have access to kubectl, Grafana, and Loki. Fix it.

🔧 Steps:

Terminal — Incident Response Playbook
# ⏱️ T+0min — ALERT: api.anihpj.com error rate > 5%

# Step 1: Check pod status
kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod
# NAME                    READY   STATUS             RESTARTS
# anihpj-abc123          0/1     CrashLoopBackOff   12
# anihpj-def456          0/1     CrashLoopBackOff   12
# All pods crashing!

# Step 2: Check logs
kubectl logs anihpj-abc123 -n anihpj-prod --tail=20
# django.db.utils.OperationalError: could not connect to server: Connection refused
#   Is the server running on host "postgres.anihpj-prod"?

# Step 3: Check database
kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod -l app=postgres
# NAME        READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
# postgres-0  0/1     OOMKilled 3          2m
# Database pod being OOMKilled repeatedly!

# Step 4: Check DB logs
kubectl logs postgres-0 -n anihpj-prod --previous
# FATAL: out of memory — increased query load from new reporting service

# Step 5: Fix — increase memory limit + scale
kubectl set resources statefulset/postgres -n anihpj-prod --limits=memory=2Gi
kubectl scale statefulset postgres --replicas=1 -n anihpj-prod
# Postgres restarts with more memory

# Step 6: Verify recovery
kubectl get pods -n anihpj-prod
# All pods Running! Error rate drops to 0%. Service restored.

📝 Postmortem Template: What happened (DB OOM), Timeline (T+0 alert → T+2 identified → T+4 fixed → T+6 restored), Root cause (new reporting service caused query spike), Action items (add DB monitoring alert at 80% memory, implement connection pooling via PgBouncer, capacity planning for new services).

✅ Success Criteria: Identify root cause + restore service + write postmortem in <15 minutes.

🎯 CNPA Domain: Domain 2 (Observability), Domain 3 (Incident Response)

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • Can't find the root cause in logs: Expand your search. Check: kubectl get events -n anihpj-prod --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp for cluster-level events. Check node status: kubectl describe node for disk pressure, memory pressure. Check if any dependent services (Redis, ingress controller) are also failing.
  • CrashLoopBackOff — can't see logs: Use kubectl logs <pod> --previous to see logs from the PREVIOUS crashed container instance. This is the #1 debugging trick for CrashLoopBackOff.
  • Fix works but service still down: Check readinessProbe — the pod may be running but not passing readiness checks. Check Service endpoints: kubectl get endpoints -n anihpj-prod — empty means no pods are ready to serve traffic.
  • Postmortem culture: The goal is BLAMELESS postmortems. Focus on: what happened (not who), how we detected it, how we fixed it, and what prevents recurrence. Action items must be specific and tracked (Jira tickets).

🌍 Real-World Context: Google's SRE book defines the incident response process: Detect → Respond → Mitigate → Postmortem. Etsy popularized blameless postmortems. PagerDuty's incident response framework adds: IC (Incident Commander), Ops Lead, Communications Lead. For anihpj: start with a simple runbook (this lab is your runbook), add automated runbooks (Argo Workflows for auto-remediation), and always write postmortems — they prevent the same incident from happening twice.

🏆 28.10 Lab 10: End-to-End Platform Build

🎯 Objective: Build a complete Internal Developer Platform for anihpj that enables a new service to go from code to production in under 15 minutes. Combine everything from Labs 1-9.

📋 Architecture:

Complete IDP Architecture — anihpj Platform
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    🏗️ anihpj INTERNAL DEVELOPER PLATFORM              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  👨‍💻 Developer                                                          │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ├─ Backstage Portal (Catalog + Templates + TechDocs)             │
  │     ├─ anictl CLI (create, deploy, logs, status)                     │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ▼                                                                │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                    PLATFORM SERVICES                          │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────────┐   │   │
  │  │  │  ArgoCD  │ │ Argo     │ │Crossplane│ │ cert-manager │   │   │
  │  │  │ (GitOps) │ │ Rollouts │ │ (DB API) │ │   (TLS)      │   │   │
  │  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────────┘   │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────────┐   │   │
  │  │  │Prometheus│ │ Grafana  │ │   Loki   │ │  Kyverno     │   │   │
  │  │  │(metrics) │ │(dashbrd) │ │  (logs)  │ │ (policies)   │   │   │
  │  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────────┘   │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ☸️ KUBERNETES (EKS/GKE/AKS)                                        │
  │  ☁️ CLOUD (AWS + GCP + Azure) — provisioned via Terraform + Crossplane│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔧 Components to Deploy:

  1. Infrastructure: EKS cluster (Terraform), RDS PostgreSQL (Crossplane), VPC + networking
  2. CI/CD: GitHub Actions (CI) → Docker build → push to GHCR → ArgoCD (CD/GitOps)
  3. Progressive Delivery: Argo Rollouts for canary deployments with auto-rollback
  4. Observability: Prometheus + Grafana (metrics), Loki (logs), OTel (traces)
  5. Security: Kyverno (admission policies), RBAC, NetworkPolicy, Cosign (image signing)
  6. Developer Portal: Backstage with Software Catalog + Templates + TechDocs
  7. Cost: Kubecost for visibility, spot instances for non-critical workloads

✅ Success Criteria: A developer creates a new service via Backstage template → code is pushed to Git → CI builds + pushes image → ArgoCD deploys to K8s → Grafana shows metrics → service live in <15 minutes. End-to-end platform operational.

🎯 CNPA Domain: ALL 6 Domains — this lab validates your complete CNPA readiness

🔍 Troubleshooting Common Issues:

  • \"Too many tools\" feeling: You don't need to deploy everything at once. Phase 1: Terraform + GitHub Actions + ArgoCD (infra + CI/CD). Phase 2: Prometheus + Grafana (observability). Phase 3: Kyverno + RBAC (security). Phase 4: Crossplane + Backstage (self-service). Each phase adds value independently.
  • Backstage not showing services: Register services in the Catalog via catalog-info.yaml files in each repo. Use the GitHub Discovery plugin to auto-discover repos with catalog files. Manual registration via Backstage UI is for testing only.
  • End-to-end flow breaking: Test each link independently: Git → CI (GitHub Actions logs), CI → Registry (docker pull), Registry → ArgoCD (manual sync), ArgoCD → K8s (kubectl get pods). Isolate where the chain breaks before debugging the whole pipeline.
  • Platform adoption low: Developers won't use your platform just because it exists. Measure: time-to-10th-deploy, Developer NPS, # services onboarded. If adoption is low, interview developers — find out what's blocking them and fix that first.

🌍 Real-World Context: This lab represents the minimum viable platform for a modern cloud native organization. The tools you deployed (ArgoCD, Prometheus, Grafana, Kyverno, Crossplane, Backstage) are all CNCF projects used by Fortune 500 companies. The pattern — GitOps deployment, automated observability, policy-as-code security, self-service APIs, developer portal — is the industry standard platform architecture. Companies like Spotify, Netflix, and Shopify built similar platforms internally. Your anihpj platform IS the CNPA exam in practice: every domain is represented in this architecture.

🎯 Exam Prep ⏱️ ~1 hour 📖 Case Studies

🎯 What You'll Learn

  • ✅ How Spotify's tool sprawl led to Backstage — the CNCF's most popular IDP portal
  • ✅ Netflix's "paved road" philosophy — opinionated defaults with escape hatches
  • ✅ Airbnb's service template strategy — standardize 80%, customize 20%
  • ✅ Shopify's DevEx investment — how developer experience compounds over time
  • ✅ How to apply ALL four patterns to anihpj today
🎵 29.1 Spotify Backstage — The IDP That Started It All

🏢 Company Profile: 1,600+ developers, 150+ autonomous squads, thousands of microservices. By 2019, Spotify had complete tool sprawl — every team used different CI tools, different monitoring, different deployment methods. New developers took months to become productive just learning the tooling landscape.

Spotify Before & After Backstage
  ❌ BEFORE (2019):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Developer: "How do I deploy my service?"                     │
  │ Team A: "Use Jenkins + Spinnaker"                            │
  │ Team B: "We use GitHub Actions + ArgoCD"                    │
  │ Team C: "We still use a bash script from 2018"              │
  │ Team D: "We have a custom CLI written in Go"                │
  │                                                              │
  │ New developer: 🤯 "Which one do I use? Who owns what?"       │
  │ Onboarding time: 2-4 MONTHS                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ AFTER Backstage (2020+):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                  BACKSTAGE PORTAL                      │   │
  │  │  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────┐  ┌────────────────┐  │   │
  │  │  │  Catalog   │  │ Templates  │  │   TechDocs     │  │   │
  │  │  │ "Find any  │  │ "Create a  │  │ "Documentation │  │   │
  │  │  │ service"   │  │  service"  │  │  for everyone" │  │   │
  │  │  └────────────┘  └────────────┘  └────────────────┘  │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                              │
  │ New developer: Opens Backstage → searches "jobpost-api"       │
  │ → sees owner, docs, CI status, deploy button                 │
  │ Onboarding time: 2-4 WEEKS (90% faster)                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔑 Key Innovations:

  • Software Catalog: Auto-discovered all services, APIs, websites, libraries from Git repos via YAML files. Every service has a catalog-info.yaml that Backstage ingests.
  • Software Templates: Cookiecutter-style scaffolding — developers click "Create Service" → answer 5 questions → GitHub repo created with Dockerfile, CI/CD, K8s manifests, and monitoring pre-configured.
  • TechDocs: Markdown files in each repo auto-rendered as documentation in Backstage. "Docs as Code" — developers write docs alongside code, not in a separate wiki.
  • Plugins: 100+ plugins — Kubernetes (see pods directly), Grafana (dashboards embedded), PagerDuty (on-call rotation), Jenkins/GitHub Actions (CI status).

📈 Results: 90% faster onboarding (4 months → 2-4 weeks), 2,000+ services cataloged, open-sourced to CNCF (now Incubating), adopted by Expedia, Zalando, Netflix, American Airlines.

💡 Steal This for anihpj

You don't need full Backstage on day one. Start with: (1) A central Git repo (anihpj-services) listing all services with owner, repo URL, and status. (2) A Cookiecutter template for new services (see Lab 28.1). (3) Markdown docs in each service repo (visible on GitHub). Evolve to Backstage when you have 15+ services.

🎬 29.2 Netflix — Platform at Massive Scale

🏢 Company Profile: 1,000+ microservices, 4,000+ daily production deploys, 200+ million subscribers globally. Netflix pioneered the "paved road" concept — opinionated, well-supported paths with escape hatches for edge cases.

Netflix Platform Architecture — The Paved Road
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    NETFLIX PLATFORM — PAVED ROAD                      │
  │                                                                      │
  │  👨‍💻 Developer                                                          │
  │     │                                                                │
  │     ├──────────────────────────────────────────────┐                 │
  │     │                                              │                 │
  │     ▼ PAVED ROAD (95% of teams)                   ▼ ESCAPE HATCH     │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐     │
  │  │  🚀 Spinnaker (CD)                  │    │ Custom deploy     │     │
  │  │  📦 Titus (Container Platform)       │    │ pipeline for      │     │
  │  │  📊 Atlas (Telemetry)                │    │ specialized needs │     │
  │  │  🔐 Repokid (IAM automation)        │    │ (GPU workloads,   │     │
  │  │  📈 A/B Testing Platform             │    │  real-time video  │     │
  │  │  ⚡ Chaos Monkey (resilience)        │    │  processing)      │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘     │
  │     │                                              │                 │
  │     │ "It just works"                               │ "You own it"    │
  │     │ Netflix supports this path                    │ Team maintains  │
  │     │ Zero infrastructure knowledge needed          │ their own path  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔑 Key Innovations:

  • Spinnaker (CD): Multi-cloud continuous delivery platform. Netflix open-sourced it; it's now used by Google, Microsoft, and Airbnb. Supports canary, blue-green, and rolling deployments across AWS, GCP, and K8s.
  • Titus (Containers): Netflix's container management platform — runs 3+ million containers/week. Schedules containers directly on AWS EC2 with deep AWS integration (IAM, VPC, security groups). Predates Kubernetes at Netflix.
  • Atlas (Telemetry): Unified observability platform. 1.5 BILLION metrics per minute. Combines logs, metrics, and traces into one query interface. Teams get dashboards auto-provisioned.
  • Chaos Engineering: Netflix INVENTED chaos engineering. Chaos Monkey randomly terminates production instances. Chaos Kong simulates entire region failures. Teams build resilience because they HAVE to.

📈 Results: 4,000+ deploys/day with zero downtime, 1,000+ microservices managed by ~100 platform engineers, 99.99% availability across 190 countries.

💡 Steal This for anihpj

Netflix's key lesson: make the right way the easy way. For anihpj: (1) Provide a "paved road" — pre-configured CI/CD + GitOps + observability (Chapters 8-9, 14-15). (2) Allow escape hatches — teams with special needs can use their own tools but lose platform support. (3) Chaos engineering: start with a simple script that kills a random anihpj pod weekly in staging. Your platform should survive it.

🏠 29.3 Airbnb — Service-Oriented Platform

🏢 Company Profile: Started as a Ruby on Rails monolith. By 2017, the monolith was slowing every team down — a change to the search algorithm required coordinating with 5 other teams who all worked in the same codebase. Airbnb adopted a service-oriented architecture with a standardized platform.

Airbnb — From Monolith to Service Platform
  ❌ MONOLITH (2016):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ruby on Rails Monolith                                      │
  │  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐   │
  │  │ Search   │ Booking  │ Payment  │ Messages │ Reviews  │   │
  │  │ (all in one codebase — any change affects everything) │   │
  │  └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘   │
  │  Deploy time: 2-4 hours (full monolith deploy)               │
  │  Team coordination: "I need 4 teams to approve my change"    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  ✅ SERVICE PLATFORM (2019+):
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │            STANDARDIZED SERVICE TEMPLATE               │   │
  │  │  Every service gets:                                   │   │
  │  │  • Git repo with pre-configured CI/CD                  │   │
  │  │  • Dockerfile (standardized base image)                │   │
  │  │  • K8s manifests (Deployment + Service + HPA)         │   │
  │  │  • Observability (auto-dashboards, auto-alerts)       │   │
  │  │  • Service mesh (Envoy sidecar, mTLS, retries)       │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐      │
  │  │ Search   │ │ Booking  │ │ Payment  │ │ Messages │      │
  │  │ Service  │ │ Service  │ │ Service  │ │ Service  │      │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘      │
  │  Each service: independent deploy, independent scaling      │
  │  Deploy time: 10-15 minutes per service                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔑 Key Innovations:

  • Service Template (Golden Path): Airbnb created a standardized template that produced a new service in minutes. Every service got the same CI/CD, the same Dockerfile structure, the same K8s manifests, and the same observability stack. "Standardize the 80%, customize the 20%."
  • SmartStack (Service Discovery): Airbnb's homegrown service discovery (predecessor to Envoy/service mesh). Combined client-side load balancing with centralized health checking.
  • Incremental Migration: They didn't rewrite the monolith. They extracted one service at a time, starting with the most painful one (Search). Over 3 years: 500+ services extracted.
  • Developer Empowerment: Teams owned their services end-to-end — they could deploy independently without coordinating with other teams. This was the biggest productivity unlock.

📈 Results: 80% faster deployment time, 500+ services from a single monolith, teams deploy independently (no coordination needed), zero downtime during migration.

💡 Steal This for anihpj

Airbnb's lesson: standardize everything you can. For anihpj: (1) Your Golden Path template (Lab 28.1) IS Airbnb's service template — every new service gets the same foundation. (2) Don't migrate everything at once — extract services incrementally, starting with the most painful one. (3) Measure deploy frequency before and after — the numbers will prove the platform's value.

🛒 29.4 Shopify — Internal PaaS on Kubernetes

🏢 Company Profile: 2,000+ developers, powering millions of online stores. Shopify built an internal Platform-as-a-Service on top of Kubernetes that abstracts away all infrastructure complexity. Their philosophy: "Developers should spend 100% of their time on business logic, 0% on infrastructure."

Shopify — Internal PaaS on Kubernetes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                    SHOPIFY INTERNAL PaaS                              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  👨‍💻 Developer Experience:                                              │
  │     "I need a new service" → writes business logic only              │
  │                                                                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                    PLATFORM ABSTRACTION                       │   │
  │  │                                                               │   │
  │  │  Developer declares:                                          │   │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │   │
  │  │  │ service: order-processor                             │     │   │
  │  │  │ language: ruby                                       │     │   │
  │  │  │ replicas: 5                                          │     │   │
  │  │  │ database: postgresql                                 │     │   │
  │  │  │ cache: redis                                         │     │   │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │   │
  │  │                                                               │   │
  │  │  Platform handles:                                            │   │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ K8s manifests (auto-generated)                     │     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ CI/CD pipeline (auto-configured)                   │     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ Database provisioning (RDS via Crossplane)         │     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ Monitoring (Prometheus + Grafana, auto-dashboard)  │     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ TLS certificates (cert-manager, auto-renewed)      │     │   │
  │  │  │ ✅ Network policies (auto-segmented)                  │     │   │
  │  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  │                                                                      │
  │  Result: 5 minutes from commit → production.                         │
  │  Developer never sees a K8s YAML file.                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🔑 Key Innovations:

  • Zero YAML Experience: Developers never write K8s YAML. They declare "I need a service with PostgreSQL and Redis" and the platform generates everything — Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, HPA, and NetworkPolicy.
  • DevEx as a Product: Shopify has a dedicated Developer Experience team that measures: time-to-first-deploy, deploy frequency, developer satisfaction (quarterly surveys), and cognitive load. They treat DevEx as a first-class product metric.
  • Platform Guild: A cross-functional group of senior engineers from every product team meets monthly to provide feedback to the platform team. This ensures the platform solves REAL problems, not imagined ones.
  • Invest Early, Compounding Returns: Shopify invested heavily in platform engineering early in their growth (2017-2019). The 5-minute deploy time enabled their Black Friday scale — they processed $3.4B in sales on Black Friday 2023 with zero platform incidents.

📈 Results: 5 minutes commit-to-production, $3.4B processed on Black Friday with zero platform issues, 2,000+ developers, DevEx as a competitive advantage.

💡 Steal This for anihpj

Shopify's lesson: DevEx compounds exponentially. For anihpj: (1) Your anictl CLI (Chapter 12) IS Shopify's platform abstraction — developers say what they need, platform provides it. (2) Measure everything — time-to-deploy, developer satisfaction, platform adoption. (3) Create a feedback loop — talk to developers monthly, prioritize based on their pain. (4) The 5-minute deploy goal is achievable — it's the end state of everything in this guide.

📊 29.5 Lessons for anihpj — Your Platform Blueprint
CompanyCore Patternanihpj ImplementationMaturity
SpotifyBackstage as IDP portalBackstage Catalog → Templates → TechDocs. Start with Git-based catalog, add Backstage at 15+ services.L2→L3
NetflixPaved road + escape hatchesGolden Path templates (Labs 28.1, 28.2) + DIY option for specialized services. Teams choose paved road = full support. Custom path = their responsibility.L3→L4
AirbnbStandardize 80%, custom 20%Cookiecutter template standardizes every service. 20% customization via values files, custom Dockerfiles, optional features.L2→L3
ShopifyDevEx as competitive advantageanictl CLI abstracts K8s complexity. Measure time-to-deploy, NPS, adoption. Quarterly developer surveys. Platform guild for feedback.L4→L5
🗺️
Your 12-Month Platform Roadmap

Month 1-3: Golden Path templates + GitOps (Spotify/Airbnb pattern). Month 4-6: Observability + CI/CD standardization (Netflix paved road). Month 7-9: Self-service APIs + Backstage portal (Spotify IDP). Month 10-12: DevEx measurement + continuous improvement (Shopify DevEx).

📈
Success Metrics to Track

Time-to-10th-deploy (target: <15 min), Developer NPS (target: 50+), % teams on platform (target: 80%+), Time from ticket to deployed DB (target: <5 min via Crossplane), Deploy frequency (target: 10x current rate).

🎯 The Common Thread: All four companies invested in making the right way the easy way. They didn't force developers to learn K8s — they abstracted it. They didn't dictate every choice — they provided paved roads with escape hatches. They didn't assume they knew what developers needed — they measured and iterated. Your anihpj platform should follow the same principles.

✅ Chapter 29 Summary — Real-World Case Studies

You've studied four industry-defining platform engineering implementations: Spotify's Backstage (unified developer portal, 90% faster onboarding), Netflix's paved road (4,000+ deploys/day, escape hatches for edge cases), Airbnb's standardized services (500+ services, 80% faster deploys), and Shopify's internal PaaS (5 min commit-to-prod, 2,000+ developers). The common thread: all four companies invested in making the right way the easy way — Golden Paths, self-service, and developer experience as a first-class concern. The anihpj implementation table shows exactly how to apply each lesson to your own platform.

📖 Key Takeaway 1

Start with Backstage Catalog (Spotify pattern), then add Templates, then TechDocs. Don't build everything at once — iterate based on developer feedback.

📖 Key Takeaway 2

Paved roads (Netflix) ≠ locked doors. Give developers escape hatches for edge cases. The platform should make the right way easy, not the only way possible.

📖 Key Takeaway 3

DevEx compounds (Shopify). Every minute saved per developer per day = hundreds of engineering-hours per year. Invest early, measure continuously.

A.2 Study Priority Matrix — What to Learn First

🔴
Tier 1: Must Master (⭐⭐⭐⭐⭐)

Docker + K8s + Helm (D1), Prometheus + Grafana (D2), ArgoCD + Flux (D3), OPA + Kyverno (D2). These are your exam-passing tools. Know them cold — they appear across multiple domains and questions.

🟡
Tier 2: Solid Understanding (⭐⭐⭐⭐)

Terraform/Crossplane (D4), Backstage (D5), EKS/GKE/AKS (D4). You need to understand these conceptually and be able to compare them. Expect 4-5 questions per tool.

🟢
Tier 3: Familiarity (⭐⭐⭐)

Istio/Linkerd (D4), Kubecost (D6), Consul. Know what they do and when to use them. 1-2 questions each. Don't spend more than 4-8 hours total on Tier 3.

📅
8-Week Tool Learning Plan

Weeks 1-2: Containers + K8s (D1). Weeks 3-4: Observability + Security (D2). Weeks 5-6: CI/CD + GitOps (D3). Week 7: IaC + APIs (D4). Week 8: IDP + Cost + Review (D5/D6).

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip — Tools Strategy

The CNPA exam tests your conceptual understanding of these tools, not hands-on usage. You won't be asked to write Terraform or PromQL — but you will be asked: "Which tool would you use for X?" and "How does tool A compare to tool B?" Focus on tool purpose, category, and trade-offs rather than memorizing every CLI flag.

✅ Appendix A Summary

You now have a complete catalog of the 50+ tools in the CNPA landscape across 10 categories, prioritized by exam relevance with estimated learning times. The 8-week plan above gives you a clear path: master Tier 1 first (those 5-star tools), then fill in the gaps. Remember: the exam tests which tool for which job, not CLI memorization.

  │  │    ▼                ▼                ▼                       │    │
  │  │ ┌──────┐       ┌──────┐        ┌──────┐                     │    │
  │  │ │ POD1 │       │ POD2 │        │ POD3 │  DEPLOYMENT (B.5)  │    │
  │  │ └──────┘       └──────┘        └──────┘  replicas: 3        │    │
  │  │    ▲                                                     │    │
  │  │    └─────── HPA (B.8) — min:3, max:20, CPU:70% ─────────┘    │
  │  │                                                               │    │
  │  │  NETWORKPOLICY (B.9)     PDB (B.11) — minAvailable: 2       │    │
  │  │  ┌─────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────┐                │    │
  │  │  │ allow-frontend  │    │ anihpj-pdb       │                │    │
  │  │  │ ingress: :8000  │    │ minAvailable: 2  │                │    │
  │  │  └─────────────────┘    └──────────────────┘                │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                                      │
  │  RBAC (B.10) — Role: anihpj-developer + RoleBinding                  │
  │  KYVERNO (B.12) — ClusterPolicy: block-latest-tag                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
💡 How to Use These Manifests

Copy the entire block below into a file named anihpj-complete.yaml. Apply with kubectl apply -f anihpj-complete.yaml. Each section is separated by --- (YAML document separator). The manifests are ordered: namespace first, then core resources, then policies.

B.1-B.9 Core Kubernetes Resources

YAML

These 9 manifests cover the essential runtime resources for the anihpj Django application.

B.1-B.9 — Core anihpj Manifests
# B.1 — Namespace
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata: { name: anihpj-prod }
---
# B.5 — Deployment (Django, 3 replicas)
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata: { name: anihpj, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  replicas: 3
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: anihpj } }
  template:
    metadata: { labels: { app: anihpj } }
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: anihpj
          image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8000 }]
          resources:
            requests: { cpu: 200m, memory: 256Mi }
            limits: { cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi }
          livenessProbe: { httpGet: { path: /health, port: 8000 } }
          readinessProbe: { httpGet: { path: /ready, port: 8000 } }
---
# B.6 — Service (ClusterIP)
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata: { name: anihpj, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  selector: { app: anihpj }
  ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8000 }]
---
# B.8 — HPA
apiVersion: autoscaling/v2
kind: HorizontalPodAutoscaler
metadata: { name: anihpj, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  scaleTargetRef: { apiVersion: apps/v1, kind: Deployment, name: anihpj }
  minReplicas: 3
  maxReplicas: 20
  metrics:
    - type: Resource
      resource: { name: cpu, target: { type: Utilization, averageUtilization: 70 } }
---
# B.9 — NetworkPolicy (default deny + allow specific)
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: NetworkPolicy
metadata: { name: allow-frontend, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: anihpj } }
  ingress:
    - from: [{ podSelector: { matchLabels: { app: frontend } } }]
      ports: [{ port: 8000 }]
---
# B.7 — Ingress (NGINX with TLS)
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata: { name: anihpj, namespace: anihpj-prod,
  annotations: { cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod } }
spec:
  tls: [{ hosts: [api.anihpj.com], secretName: anihpj-tls }]
  rules:
    - host: api.anihpj.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /
            pathType: Prefix
            backend: { service: { name: anihpj, port: { number: 80 } } }

B.10-B.12 Advanced Resources (RBAC, PDB, Kyverno)

YAML

Production platforms need access control, disruption budgets, and policy enforcement. These three manifests demonstrate CNPA Domain 2 (Security & Compliance) in practice.

B.10-B.12 — RBAC + PDB + Kyverno
# B.10 — RBAC (Role + RoleBinding)
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: Role
metadata: { name: anihpj-developer, namespace: anihpj-dev }
rules:
  - apiGroups: ["", "apps"]
    resources: [pods, deployments, services, configmaps]
    verbs: [get, list, create, update, patch, delete]
---
apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
kind: RoleBinding
metadata: { name: anihpj-dev-binding, namespace: anihpj-dev }
subjects:
  - { kind: Group, name: dev-team, apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io }
roleRef: { kind: Role, name: anihpj-developer, apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io }
---
# B.11 — PodDisruptionBudget
apiVersion: policy/v1
kind: PodDisruptionBudget
metadata: { name: anihpj-pdb, namespace: anihpj-prod }
spec:
  minAvailable: 2
  selector: { matchLabels: { app: anihpj } }
---
# B.12 — Kyverno Policy (block :latest + require probes)
apiVersion: kyverno.io/v1
kind: ClusterPolicy
metadata: { name: block-latest-tag }
spec:
  validationFailureAction: Enforce
  rules:
    - name: validate-image-tag
      match: { resources: { kinds: [Pod] } }
      validate:
        message: "Using :latest tag is prohibited"
        pattern:
          spec:
            containers:
              - image: "!*:latest"
🎯 CNPA Exam Tip — YAML Manifest Knowledge

The CNPA exam tests your ability to read and understand Kubernetes YAML, not necessarily write it from scratch. You should be able to: (1) Identify which apiVersion belongs to which resource, (2) Understand the difference between minAvailable and maxUnavailable in a PDB, (3) Recognize that validationFailureAction: Enforce means the policy blocks non-compliant resources (vs Audit which only reports).

✅ Appendix B Summary

You now have 12 production-ready Kubernetes manifests covering every layer of the anihpj platform: Namespace → Deployment → Service → Ingress (TLS) → HPA → NetworkPolicy → RBAC → PDB → Kyverno. These templates are your exam-day reference for Domain 1 (K8s architecture), Domain 2 (security policies), and Domain 4 (infrastructure provisioning).

C.1-C.4 Core Infrastructure (EKS + RDS)

TERRAFORM

The provider, EKS module, and RDS instance form the infrastructure backbone of the anihpj platform.

C.1-C.4 — EKS Cluster + RDS PostgreSQL + VPC Security
# C.1 — AWS Provider
provider "aws" { region = "us-east-1" }
---
# C.3 — EKS Cluster
module "eks" {
  source  = "terraform-aws-modules/eks/aws"
  version = "~> 19.0"
  cluster_name    = "anihpj-prod"
  cluster_version = "1.28"
  vpc_id     = module.vpc.vpc_id
  subnet_ids = module.vpc.private_subnets
  node_groups = {
    platform = {
      desired_capacity = 3
      instance_types   = ["t3.large"]
      capacity_type    = "SPOT"
    }
  }
}
---
# C.4 — RDS PostgreSQL + VPC
resource "aws_db_instance" "anihpj_db" {
  identifier     = "anihpj-prod"
  engine         = "postgres"
  engine_version = "16.1"
  instance_class = "db.t4g.medium"
  allocated_storage     = 100
  max_allocated_storage = 500
  username             = var.db_username
  password             = var.db_password
  backup_retention_period = 30
  performance_insights_enabled = true
  multi_az = true
  storage_encrypted = true
  vpc_security_group_ids = [aws_security_group.db.id]
  db_subnet_group_name   = aws_db_subnet_group.anihpj.name
  skip_final_snapshot = false
}
resource "aws_security_group" "db" {
  name   = "anihpj-db-sg"
  vpc_id = module.vpc.vpc_id
  ingress { from_port = 5432; to_port = 5432; protocol = "tcp"; cidr_blocks = ["10.0.0.0/8"] }
}

C.5 Crossplane IAM — Enabling Self-Service Infrastructure

TERRAFORM

Crossplane is the Kubernetes-native way to provision cloud infrastructure. Unlike Terraform (which runs in CI/CD), Crossplane runs inside your K8s cluster and uses CRDs to represent cloud resources. Developers create a RDSInstance YAML, Crossplane provisions it — no Terraform knowledge needed.

C.5 — Crossplane IAM (Pod Identity via OIDC)
# C.5 — Crossplane IAM (Pod Identity)
data "aws_iam_policy_document" "crossplane" {
  statement {
    actions   = ["ec2:*", "rds:*", "eks:*", "iam:*", "s3:*", "elasticloadbalancing:*"]
    resources = ["*"]
  }
}
resource "aws_iam_role" "crossplane_role" {
  name = "crossplane-controller"
  assume_role_policy = jsonencode({
    Version = "2012-10-17"
    Statement = [{ Effect = "Allow", Principal = { Federated = var.oidc_provider_arn }, Action = "sts:AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity" }]
  })
}
🔄
Terraform vs Crossplane — Which to Use?

Terraform: Best for initial cluster provisioning (EKS, VPC, IAM). Runs in CI/CD pipelines. Crossplane: Best for ongoing developer self-service (databases, buckets, queues). Runs inside K8s. Together they form the complete IaC story for CNPA Domain 4.

🔐
Pod Identity (OIDC) — Why It Matters

Crossplane uses IAM Roles for Service Accounts (IRSA) via OIDC federation — no static credentials, no secrets in K8s. The AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity action lets Crossplane pods assume an IAM role using their K8s service account token, following Zero Trust principles.

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip — Infrastructure Provisioning

Domain 4 (12% of exam) tests your understanding of how platforms provision infrastructure. Key concepts: (1) Reconciliation loop — Crossplane continuously reconciles desired state (CRD) with actual state (cloud), (2) Composition — Crossplane's way of bundling multiple resources into one API, (3) The difference between provisioning (Terraform) and ongoing management (Crossplane/Operators). Expect questions comparing these approaches.

✅ Appendix C Summary

You now have the complete Terraform configuration for the anihpj platform: AWS provider → EKS cluster (SPOT instances) → RDS PostgreSQL (Multi-AZ, encrypted) → VPC security group → Crossplane IAM (OIDC). This demonstrates the full Infrastructure as Code lifecycle for Domain 4, and shows how Terraform + Crossplane complement each other in a real platform.

⛵ Appendix D: Complete Helm Chart for anihpj

APPENDIX 📋 REFERENCE ⛵ HELM

A production-grade Helm chart for deploying the anihpj Django application on Kubernetes. Helm is the package manager for K8s — it templatizes your YAML so you can deploy the same application to dev, staging, and production with different configurations via values.yaml overrides.

⛵ Helm Chart Structure — How Templates + Values = Deployed Resources
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        HELM CHART: anihpj/                            │
  │  ┌─────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │     Chart.yaml      │    │        values.yaml               │    │
  │  │  name: anihpj       │    │  replicaCount: 1                 │    │
  │  │  version: 1.0.0     │    │  image: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj    │    │
  │  └─────────────────────┘    └──────────────┬───────────────────┘    │
  │                                             │                        │
  │                    ┌────────────────────────┼──────────────┐         │
  │                    │                        │              │         │
  │                    ▼                        ▼              ▼         │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐  ┌──────────────────────┐  ┌──────────┐  │
  │  │  values-dev.yaml     │  │  values-prod.yaml     │  │ values-  │  │
  │  │  replicaCount: 1     │  │  replicaCount: 3      │  │ staging  │  │
  │  │  resources: small    │  │  resources: medium    │  │ .yaml    │  │
  │  │  debug: true         │  │  autoscaling: true    │  │          │  │
  │  └──────────┬───────────┘  └──────────┬───────────┘  └──────────┘  │
  │             │                         │                              │
  │             └──────────┬──────────────┘                              │
  │                        │                                             │
  │                        ▼                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │                    templates/                                 │   │
  │  │  deployment.yaml  service.yaml  ingress.yaml  configmap.yaml │   │
  │  │  secret.yaml      hpa.yaml      NOTES.txt                    │   │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │   │
  │  │  │ {{ .Values.replicaCount }} → 3 (from values-prod)    │    │   │
  │  │  │ {{ .Values.image.tag }}     → v1.2.3                 │    │   │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │   │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

D.1 Chart Structure & Values

HELM
anihpj Helm Chart Structure
anihpj/
├── Chart.yaml          # name: anihpj, version: 1.0.0
├── values.yaml         # Default values
├── values-dev.yaml     # Dev overrides (smaller resources, debug on)
├── values-prod.yaml    # Prod overrides (3 replicas, higher limits)
├── templates/
│   ├── deployment.yaml # Go-templated Deployment
│   ├── service.yaml    # ClusterIP Service
│   ├── ingress.yaml    # NGINX Ingress with TLS
│   ├── configmap.yaml  # Django settings
│   ├── secret.yaml     # DB credentials + Django SECRET_KEY
│   ├── hpa.yaml        # HorizontalPodAutoscaler
│   └── NOTES.txt       # Post-install instructions

# Install: helm install anihpj ./anihpj -f values-prod.yaml

# values-prod.yaml
replicaCount: 3
image: { repository: ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj, tag: v1.2.3 }
resources: { requests: { cpu: 200m, memory: 256Mi }, limits: { cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi } }
autoscaling: { enabled: true, minReplicas: 3, maxReplicas: 20, targetCPU: 70 }
ingress: { enabled: true, host: api.anihpj.com, tls: true }
monitoring: { enabled: true, serviceMonitor: true }
💡 Helm vs Kustomize — When to Use Which

Helm: Best for distributing applications to others (charts can be shared via Helm repos). Uses Go templates with {{ .Values.x }} syntax. Kustomize: Best for managing variations of the same app within your org. No templates — uses overlay/patch approach. Both are CNCF projects and both appear in CNPA Domain 1.

✅ Appendix D Summary

You now have a complete Helm chart structure for anihpj with environment-specific values files (dev/staging/prod), 7 template files, and monitoring integration. The key Helm concept for CNPA: separation of templates (what) from values (how) — this is what makes platforms configurable and self-service.

🐳 Appendix E: Production-Ready Dockerfile for anihpj

APPENDIX 📋 REFERENCE 🐳 DOCKER

A multi-stage, production-optimized Dockerfile for the anihpj Django application. Multi-stage builds keep your final image small (~150MB vs ~900MB for a single-stage build) by separating build dependencies from runtime. This is a critical CNPA Domain 1 concept — every platform engineer needs to know how to build secure, minimal container images.

🐳 Multi-Stage Build — Builder Stage → Production Stage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                     MULTI-STAGE DOCKER BUILD                          │
  │                                                                      │
  │  STAGE 1: BUILDER                      STAGE 2: PRODUCTION           │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐            ┌────────────────────────┐   │
  │  │ FROM python:3.11-slim  │            │ FROM python:3.11-slim  │   │
  │  │                        │            │                        │   │
  │  │ apt-get install gcc    │            │ COPY --from=builder    │   │
  │  │ + libpq-dev            │  ──COPY──▶ │ /root/.local           │   │
  │  │                        │  (deps)    │                        │   │
  │  │ pip install -r req.txt │            │ COPY . .               │   │
  │  │ (compiles wheels)      │            │                        │   │
  │  │                        │            │ USER appuser (non-root)│   │
  │  │ SIZE: ~900MB           │            │ HEALTHCHECK            │   │
  │  └────────────────────────┘            │ CMD ["gunicorn", ...]  │   │
  │                                        │                        │   │
  │  ❌ DISCARDED after build               │ SIZE: ~150MB ✅        │   │
  │                                        └────────────────────────┘   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

E.1 The Complete Multi-Stage Dockerfile

DOCKERFILE
Dockerfile — Multi-Stage, Production Optimized
# STAGE 1: Build
FROM python:3.11-slim AS builder
WORKDIR /app
RUN apt-get update && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends gcc libpq-dev \
    && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --user --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# STAGE 2: Production
FROM python:3.11-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY --from=builder /root/.local /root/.local
COPY . .
ENV PATH=/root/.local/bin:$PATH
RUN useradd --create-home appuser && chown -R appuser:appuser /app
USER appuser
EXPOSE 8000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s CMD curl -f http://localhost:8000/health || exit 1
CMD ["gunicorn", "anihpj.wsgi:application", "--bind", "0.0.0.0:8000", "--workers", "3"]

# Build: docker build -t ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3 .
# Run:   docker run -p 8000:8000 ghcr.io/anihpj/anihpj:v1.2.3
🔒
5 Production Best Practices in This Dockerfile

① Multi-stage build (smaller attack surface). ② Non-root user (USER appuser). ③ HEALTHCHECK for orchestration. ④ No build tools in final image (gcc stays in builder). ⑤ Pinned base image (3.11-slim, not latest).

📏
Image Size Comparison

Single-stage: ~900MB (includes gcc, headers, build artifacts). Multi-stage: ~150MB (only Python + compiled wheels). That's an 83% reduction — faster pulls, smaller attack surface, lower storage costs.

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip — Container Images

Domain 1 tests container fundamentals: Know the difference between CMD (default, overridable) and ENTRYPOINT (hard to override). Know why multi-stage builds matter (smaller images = faster deploys = better security). Know that HEALTHCHECK tells the orchestrator (K8s/Docker) when your app is actually ready vs just running.

✅ Appendix E Summary

You now have a production-ready multi-stage Dockerfile that builds a secure, minimal (~150MB) image for anihpj. Key takeaways: multi-stage builds separate build from runtime, non-root users are mandatory for production, HEALTHCHECK enables orchestration-level monitoring, and pinned tags (3.11-slim, not :latest) prevent supply chain surprises.

Your one-page exam-day reference: CLI commands for all 5 major tools, CNCF project maturity levels, and 25 essential PE glossary terms. Print this, keep it by your side during final review. The glossary terms are especially important — they appear throughout the exam as both question topics and answer choices.

G.1 Sample Questions (10 of 100)

These 10 sample questions span all 6 domains. Full answer key with detailed explanations is in Chapter 27.4.

#DomainQuestionAnswer
1D1Which component stores the K8s cluster's desired state?etcd
2D1What is the smallest deployable unit in Kubernetes?Pod
3D1Which CNCF project is the observability standard for cloud native?OpenTelemetry
4D2"Platform as a Product" means treating the IDP with what discipline?Product management (user research, roadmaps, feedback loops)
5D2Kyverno is primarily used for what purpose in Kubernetes?Kubernetes-native policy management using YAML (admission control)
6D3In GitOps, what serves as the single source of truth?Git repository
7D3Which Argo project provides progressive delivery for Kubernetes?Argo Rollouts (canary/blue-green deployments)
8D4Crossplane uses which Kubernetes extension mechanism to represent cloud resources?Custom Resource Definitions (CRDs)
9D5What is Spotify's open-source developer portal framework called?Backstage
10D6Name all four DORA metrics used to measure software delivery performance.Deploy Frequency, Lead Time for Changes, Change Fail Rate, Time to Restore Service
📊
How to Use These Questions

① Take them as a timed mock exam (90 minutes for all 100). ② Review wrong answers and re-read the corresponding chapter. ③ Focus extra time on your weakest domain. ④ Retake until you score 80%+ consistently. The exam passing score varies but aim for 75%+.

🎯
Scoring Guide

90-100: You're over-prepared — schedule now! 80-89: Ready, review weak areas. 70-79: Solid, 2 more weeks of focused study. 60-69: Good foundation, need 3-4 more weeks. Below 60: Revisit Parts 1-5 before attempting again.

🎯 CNPA Exam Tip — Question Style

The CNPA exam uses multiple-choice questions, not hands-on labs (unlike CKA/CKAD). Expect: (1) "Which tool would you use for X?" style questions, (2) Scenario-based questions ("A team needs self-service databases. Which approach?"), (3) Comparison questions ("Terraform vs Crossplane"), (4) Definition questions ("What is an Error Budget?"). No CLI commands to run — it's all conceptual assessment.
